                                                                     viii
                                        INTRODUCTION

       Defendant, Tyler James Robinson, by and through undersigned counsel, hereby moves

the Court to exclude from the courtroom during the pendency of this case television cameras and

microphones, still photographers, radio microphones and other similar implements of the

electronic or broadcast media. The exclusion of these devices is necessary to ensure Mr.

Robinson’s rights to due process, to a fair and impartial jury, to counsel, to be free from

unreasonable searches and seizures, and to be free from cruel and unusual punishment, as

required by the Fourth, Sixth, Eighth and Fourteenth Amendments to the United States

Constitution and article I, sections 7, 9, 10, 12, and 14 of the Utah Constitution.

                                     REQUESTED RELIEF

       Mr. Robinson seeks an order of this Court pursuant to the constitutional provisions cited

above and Utah Rule of Judicial Administration 4-401.01 (“Rule 4-401.01”) that prohibits or

restricts further electronic media coverage in this cases because: (1) such action is necessary to

preserve and protect the constitutional provisions set forth above; (2) under Rule 4-401.01,

further electronic media coverage should be prohibited or restricted because (a) the electronic

media coverage in this case has not been and will not in the future be for the predominant

purpose of journalism or dissemination of news to the public (Rule 4-401.01(2)(A) ); (b) the

reasons for doing so are sufficiently compelling to outweigh any presumption that electronic

media coverage by a news reporter shall be permitted (Rule 4-401.01(2)(A) ); (c) a judge may

terminate or suspend electronic media coverage at any time without prior notice if the judge

finds that continued electronic media coverage is no longer appropriate based upon consideration

of one or more of the factors in Paragraph (2)(B) (Rule 4-401.01(3)(B) ) ; and (d) the electronic

media has violated this Court’s decorum order so prohibition or restriction of electronic media

coverage is warranted under Rule 4-401.01(5) .

                                                  1
            It is possible, of course, that in spite of …safeguards, when a President is
         assassinated or some similar crime is committed, the public in all parts of the
         country is so saturated with the publicity respecting the crime that a fair trial
         may indeed be impossible.

J. Skelly Wright, Fair Trial-Free Press, 38 F.R.D. 435, 437 (1966).

                                       RELEVANT FACTS1

       Even before a suspect was arrested for the killing of Mr. Kirk on September 10, 2025,

influential politicians were appearing on national television broadcasts to proclaim they knew for

a fact what the motivation for the killing was and what awaited the killer once he was

apprehended. For instance, shortly after the killing, Utah Governor Cox held a nationally

televised press conference in which he announced that although the “investigation is ongoing”, “I

want to be very clear that this is a political assassination.” NBC NEWS, ‘This is a political

assassination’: Gov. Spencer Cox speaks out on Charlie Kirk’s death, Sep. 10, 2025),

https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5kmC8IyhD-A.2 The Governor also delivered a personal


1
  Citations to documents in the existing record are referenced by the docket entry number
(“Dkt.”) and applicable page number, where appropriate. All videographic references throughout
the motion are on file with defense counsel. Defense counsel notes that there are references
throughout to the paramour of Mr. Robinson both in argument and in electronic media
references. The electronic media references refer to her as “boyfriend,” “Lance Twiggs” and
“Mr. Twiggs,” and using male-gendered pronouns. However, out of respect for Ms. Twiggs and
for clarity, outside of the media references, defense counsel refers to her by her chosen name of
Luna and by she/her pronouns.
2
  The term “political assassination”, while emotionally charged, has no legal meaning. While one
judge, citing People v. Sirhan, 497 P.2d 1121 (1972), has suggested that “a political
assassination raises the possibility of a more widespread plot (with perhaps additional targets)”,
Gov’t of Virgin Islands v. Gereau, No. CRIM. 97-1972, 1973 WL 354203, at *23 (D.V.I. July
23, 1973), even Sirhan Sirhan was not charged with “political assassination”; he was charged
with murder. In Utah, “[a]n actor commits aggravated murder if the actor intentionally or
knowingly causes the death of another individual under any of the following circumstances:…
(xii) the deceased individual was a local, state, or federal public official, or a candidate for public
office, and the homicide is based on, is caused by, or is related to that official position, act,
capacity, or candidacy” Utah Code § 76-5-202(2)(a)(xii). Even after what appears to be an
exhaustive state and federal investigation, Mr. Robinson has not been charged with this offense
or with the comparable federal aggravated murder provision. See 18 U.S.C. § 3592(c)(14). See

 One may legitimately ask, what is so wrong with the head of the executive branch of a
government announcing before a suspect has even been arrested his commitment to pursuing the

                                                  4
       The electronic prejudicial pretrial publicity directed specifically at Mr. Robinson began

early in the morning of September 12, 2025, following Mr. Robinson’s surrender to law

enforcement authorities on the late evening of September 11, 2025.4 It has continued relentlessly

ever since that date.



death penalty based solely on the facts of a crime and without regard to the identity, background,
or character of the person who committed it. The United States Supreme Court answered this
long ago in Woodson v. North Carolina:
       A process that accords no significance to relevant facets of the character and record of the
       individual offender or the circumstances of the particular offense excludes from
       consideration in fixing the ultimate punishment of death the possibility of compassionate
       or mitigating factors stemming from the diverse frailties of humankind. It treats all
       persons convicted of a designated offense not as uniquely individual human beings, but
       as members of a faceless, undifferentiated mass to be subjected to the blind infliction of
       the penalty of death.
428 U.S. 280, 304 (1976).
4
  This motion uses the term “prejudicial pretrial publicity” to refer not to all pretrial publicity,
but only to that publicity that has been recognized by courts, legislatures, and other rule-making
authorities as posing a threat to a defendant’s right to a fair trial. See, e.g., Skilling v. United
States, 561 U.S. 358, 382 (2010) (a presumption of juror prejudice is warranted where the
pretrial publicity references a “confession or other blatantly prejudicial information of the type
readers or viewers could not reasonably be expected to shut from sight.”); Gentile v. State Bar of
Nevada, 501 U.S. 1030, 1046 (1991) (noting that public statements that are “obvious bases for a
finding of prejudice “ include mention of a confession or of “evidence from searches or test
results.”); KUTV, Inc. v. Conder, 668 P.2d 513, 522-23 (Utah 1983) (referring to the American
Bar Association’s Standards Relating to Fair Trial and Free Press (1966) as “instructive” on
this issue); ABA, Fair Trial & Public Discourse (2013 Fourth Ed.), available at
https://www.americanbar.org/groups/criminal_justice/resources/standards/fair-trial-public-
discourse/; id. at Standard 8-2.2 (prohibiting prosecutorial comment on “the character,
credibility, or reputation of a defendant…or the …creed, religion, or sexual orientation of such
person”, “the personal opinion of the prosecutor as to the guilt or innocence of the defendant”,
“the existence or contents of any confession, admission, or statement given by a defendant…, or
the refusal or failure of such person to make a statement”, “the performance or results of any
examinations or tests, or the refusal or failure to submit to an examination or test by a
defendant”, “the nature of physical evidence expected to be presented”, “the…creed,
religion,…character, reputation, or credibility of” prospective witnesses or the victim, and
“information that the lawyer knows or has reason to know would be inadmissible as evidence in
a trial”); id. at Standard 8-3.1 (same prohibitions apply to law enforcement officers and
agencies); id. at Standard 8-2.4 (same prohibitions apply to lawyers acting as legal
commentators); Utah R. Prof’l Cond. 3.6 (providing that “[a] lawyer who is participating or has
participated in the investigation or litigation of a matter shall not make an extrajudicial statement

                                                  5
       Much of the very early prejudicial electronic media coverage in this case was directly

linked to a decision by law enforcement and prosecutorial agencies to release to the media a

three-page Affidavit of Probable Cause that outlined in detail the allegations against Mr.

Robinson, including his alleged “confession” supposedly documented in online posts, and the

finding of inscribed bullets at some unspecified location. Dkt. 2. The affidavit concludes with

investigator Brian Davis’ personal opinion that “[b]ased on the evidence detailed in this

statement, I believe there is probable cause that Tyler Robinson committed the crimes of

aggravated murder by shooting Charlie Kirk in a circumstance that put many around him at

grave risk of death, felony discharge of a firearm, and obstruction of justice for moving and

hiding the rifle believed to be used in the shooting.” Id. at 3.5 Copies of this Affidavit began




that the lawyer knows or reasonably should know will be disseminated by means of public
communication and will have a substantial likelihood of materially prejudicing an adjudicative
proceeding in the matter”, and noting in the Comment that “[t]here are… certain subjects that are
more likely than not to have a material prejudicial effect on a proceeding, particularly when they
refer to a …a criminal matter or any other proceeding that could result in incarceration”, and
then listing most of the same prohibitions set forth in ABA Standard 8-2.2); Utah R. Prof’l Cond.
3.8(e) (“The prosecutor in a criminal case shall:… [e]xercise reasonable care to prevent
investigators, law enforcement personnel, employees or other persons assisting or associated
with the prosecutor in a criminal case from making an extrajudicial statement that the prosecutor
would be prohibited from making under Rule 3.6.”). See also In the Matter of Discipline of
Leavitt, 2025 UT 46, ¶ 2, 579 P.3d 343 (“Rule 3.6 recognizes the risk that a lawyer’s public
commentary, especially in a criminal case, may unfairly taint the jury pool or otherwise
compromise the legal proceeding. As officers of the court, all attorneys have a responsibility to
protect the integrity of the judicial process. This is especially true when the attorney is in a
position of public trust, like an elected county attorney responsible for prosecuting criminal
conduct.”; upholding disciplinary sanction against former Utah County Attorney for violating
Rule 3.6).

admissible at trial.

6
  See 28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(3) (2013) (containing no provision that would allow personnel of the
Department of Justice to release information relating to a criminal proceeding on the basis that it
is contained in a public record); § 50.2(b)(6) (“The release of certain types of information
generally tends to create dangers of prejudice without serving a significant law enforcement
function. Therefore, personnel of the Department should refrain from making available the
following:… (ii) Statements, admissions, confessions, or alibis attributable to a defendant, or the
refusal or failure of the accused to make a statement (iii) Reference to investigative procedures
such as fingerprints, polygraph examinations, ballistic tests, or laboratory tests, or to the refusal
by the defendant to submit to such tests or examinations…(v) Statements concerning evidence or
argument in the case, whether or not it is anticipated that such evidence or argument will be used
at trial.”).
7
  28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(6) (“[P]ersonnel of the Department should refrain from making available

        Respectfully, however, this statement does not go far enough. A more apt description of

what is truly happening in this case with respect to prejudicial media coverage has been

described by one legal researcher as a “Content Tornado.” See Katie Grace Frisbee, Caught In

The Content Tornado: How To Protect Violent-Crime Trials From The Prejudicial Effects Of

Live Streaming, 74 Duke Law J. 1053 (2025). As the author explains:

             The content tornado… refers to the whirlwind of fact, fiction, and opinion
         that emerges when violent-crime trials are live streamed on social media. This
         concept describes how live streamed trials generate emotional and moralized
         content that gets amplified by social media algorithms. The emotional nature
         of violent-crime trials—fueled by anger, grief, and public passion—draws
         viewers in, and as they engage with the content, they become more likely to
         produce their own secondary content on the subject. This user-generated
         secondary content—in the form of videos, comments, and posts—amplifies the
         tornado and mixes accurate information with misinformation, exaggerations,
         and public biases.

Id. at 1053. See also Murthy v. Missouri, 603 U.S. 43, 50 (2024) (“With their billions of active

users, the world’s major social-media companies host a ‘staggering’ amount of content on their

platforms.”); United States v. Bowen, 799 F.3d 336, 357 (5th Cir. 2015) (warning against the

“growing influence of online communications to mold public opinion in our society, and the

danger of mob reactions.”)

        The devastating effect of the content tornado on the search for truth in this capital case is

very well summarized

         Digital amplification intensifies legacy media pitfalls. Social platforms enable
         user-generated content—memes, threads, and live commentary—that evades
         editorial gatekeeping, propagating unverified rumors at viral speeds.
         Algorithms prioritize engagement via outrage, creating echo chambers where
         70% of users encounter reinforcing narratives, per platform transparency
         reports, thus polarizing perceptions and pressuring judicial actors indirectly.



                                                 30
F. Longa, The Presumption of Innocence and the Media An Examination of Fair Trial Rights in

the Digital Era, 4 (2025): 12

         This Court found in its October 27, 2025 order that “[t]his case has generated an immense

amount of pre-trial publicity” and that “[a]s with many high-profile cases, public interest may

both wax and wane, but it is likely to persist.” Dkt. 137, at 6. The Court’s finding that public

interest in this case is “likely to persist” has certainly been borne out, as has the fact that the

overwhelming amount of the most recent publicity has consisted of prejudicial pretrial publicity

as defined above. Recent events have also demonstrated that the Court’s laudable efforts to put

reasonable limits on the media have been to no avail.

         The futility of attempting to contain the “content tornado” in this case and to curb

electronic media abuses through warnings and repositioning of cameras is nowhere better

illustrated than the electronic media’s handling of the violation of the Court’s decorum order on

December 11, 2025. As the Court knows, after the Court ruled that the issue concerning violation

of the protective order was outside the scope of the closed hearing and must be addressed in

public (SEALED Dkt. 273 at 54), the parties necessarily had to discuss the fact on camera that

the violation consisted in part in showing Mr. Robinson in shackles. See Dkt. 256 at 7 (Visser:

“It’s our understanding that his shackles are visible.”); Id. at 8-9 (Hunt: “My understanding is

that there was a zoom-out shot that did inadvertently show the shackles. That was improper. That

was inadvertent. We apologize for that. And they’ve taken measures to make sure that the pool

video that is disseminated to the rest of the pool does not contain any capture of the defendant in

the shackles. We can’t do anything about the live-stream, of course, but we can control what the

rest of the video is that we capture and disseminate. So that is being done.”); Id. at 10 (Visser:

15
   Standard 8-2.4 (b) provides in part that “[a] lawyer who is serving as a legal commentator
should strive to ensure that the lawyer’s commentary enhances the public’s understanding of the
criminal matter and of the criminal justice system generally, promotes respect for the judicial
system, and does not materially prejudice the fair administration of justice, in the particular case
or in general. To that end, a legal commentator should:…refrain from providing commentary
designed to sensationalize a criminal matter.” Standard 8-2.4(c) provides in part that “[a] lawyer
serving as a commentator should exercise great caution if asked to express personal opinions
regarding the performance of the participants or the likely outcome of the proceedings. If the
lawyer chooses to respond to such inquires, the lawyer should identify with specificity the basis
for any such opinions.” The commentary to this standard states: “While legal commentators
appropriately may help explain legal proceedings and principles, they should not be fanning
public emotions. And while criminal trials are by their very nature adversarial, they are not a
sporting match.” American Bar Association, Fair Trial and Public Discourse, 48-51 (2013
Fourth Ed.) (“Public Discourse Standard”), available at
https://www.americanbar.org/content/dam/aba/publications/criminal_justice_standards/fair_trial
_commentaries.pdf

   As explained in Estes v. Texas, , “ [i]n Rideau v. State of Louisiana, 373 U.S. 723…(1963),
this Court constructed a rule that the televising of a defendant in the act of confessing to a crime
was inherently invalid under the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment even without
a showing of prejudice or a demonstration of the nexus between the televised confession and the
trial.” 381 U.S. 532, 538 (1965). The Court in Rideau reasoned: “For anyone who has ever
watched television the conclusion cannot be avoided that this spectacle, to the tens of thousands
of people who saw and heard it, in a very real sense was Rideau’s trial—at which he pleaded

   Understandably, the family members of Mr. Kirk have been emotionally devastated by the
crime and these proceedings. However, precisely because of the searing emotional impact of

jurors may hear from victims’ families. See, e.g., Payne v. Tennessee, 501 U.S. 808, 809 (1991)
(“In the event that [victim impact] evidence is introduced that is so unduly prejudicial that it
renders the trial fundamentally unfair, the Fourteenth Amendment’s Due Process Clause
provides a mechanism for relief.”). See also Andrew v. White, 604 U.S. 86, 93 (2025) (“Payne
…broke little new ground in this respect. By the time of the OCCA’s decision in this case, it was

Process Clause.”); Bosse v. Oklahoma, 580 U.S. 1, 3 (2016) (per curiam) (“The Oklahoma Court
of Criminal Appeals has recognized that Payne ‘specifically acknowledged its holding did not
affect’ Booth ‘s prohibition on opinions about the crime, the defendant, and the appropriate

relevant only to demonstrate the specific harm caused by a particular crime, it seems clear that
the victims’ testimony must reflect the harm caused by the criminal conduct, rather than the
impact of the trial proceedings.” (internal citation omitted)).

       The phrase “media circus”, often overused, is most typically identified with Judge Lance

                                           ARGUMENT

I.     INTRODUCTION

       This argument begins with three findings of fact the Court has already made:

       (1) “The media interest in Mr. Robinson’s trial is arguably unprecedented in the State of
           Utah. Photographs and visual recordings of Mr. Robinson in jail clothing will likely
           be shared and disseminated in various public platforms. There is a strong likelihood
           that a substantial number of potential jurors in Utah County will view these images
           and may associate Mr. Robinson’s jail clothing with indicia of guilt. The danger of
           prejudice is compounded by technological advancements that allow the images to be
           manipulated in ways that do not accurately reflect the truth of the court proceedings”

       (2) “This case has generated an immense amount of pre-trial publicity. As with many
           high- profile cases, public interest may both wax and wane, but it is likely to persist.
           Visual recordings and photographs of Mr. Robinson shackled may give the
           impression that he is dangerous and may undermine his presumption of innocence.
           The potential jury pool in Utah County, based on their proximity, is likely to be even
           more exposed to the images of these proceedings and the messages those images
           send.”

       (3) “This case generates extraordinary ongoing national and international attention, some
           of which is threatening in nature to the parties, their representatives, and non-party
           participants (e.g. witnesses and court personnel).”

Dkt. 137, at 5-6; Dkt. 266, at 2.


18
  See, e.g., Andrew G.T. Moore II, The O.J. Simpson Trial-Triumph of Justice or Debacle?, 41
ST. LOUIS U. L.J. 9, 10 (1996) (“Unfortunately, the [O.J. Simpson] trial became a stage for the
jury, lawyers and judge to pursue their own self-serving purposes. With the defense attorneys
claiming their client was the real ‘victim,’ the prosecution losing sight of its duty to present
evidence fairly, a judge totally smitten with his own self-generated celebrity status, and jurors
being discharged for a variety of problems, including misconduct, the whole proceeding became
an embarrassing reflection of the American legal system.”).

                                                  48
        “The problem is an ancient one. Mr. Justice Holmes stated no more than a commonplace

when, two generations ago, he noted that ‘(a)ny judge who has sat with juries knows that, in spite

of forms, they are extremely likely to be impregnated by the environing atmosphere.’” Groppi v.

Wisconsin, 400 U.S. 505, 510 (1971) (quoting Frank v. Mangum, 237 U.S. 309, 349 (1915)

(dissenting opinion)).

        Nowhere is this statement more true than when television cameras are allowed into the

courtroom at all stages of a criminal case, and when television and internet news crews in the age

of social media use livestream coverage as a platform for the speculations and falsehoods of

those eager to obtain fame, fortune, or political advantage from the tragic events surrounding a

notorious criminal case.

        The United States Supreme Court long ago stressed in Estes v. Texas, that it was not just

the electronic media that was responsible in that case for creating a media circus that deprived

the defendant of a fair trial, but also the trial judge himself, who decided to allow electronic

media coverage of a notorious criminal case and thereby unconstitutionally fanned the flames of

prejudicial pretrial publicity:

         From the moment the trial judge announces that a case will be televised it
         becomes a cause celebre. The whole community, including prospective jurors,
         becomes interested in all the morbid details surrounding it. The approaching
         trial immediately assumes an important status in the public press and the
         accused is highly publicized along with the offense with which he is charged.
         Every juror carries with him into the jury box these solemn facts and thus
         increases the chance of prejudice that is present in every criminal case. And we
         must remember that realistically it is only the notorious trial which will be
         broadcast, because of the necessity for paid sponsorship. The conscious or
         unconscious effect that this may have on the juror’s judgment cannot be
         evaluated, but experience indicates that it is not only possible but highly
         probable that it will have a direct bearing on his vote as to guilt or innocence.




                                                 49
381 U.S. at 545.19 And in Estes,

         The judge decided that the trial proceedings would be telecast. He announced
         no restrictions at the time. This emphasized the notorious nature of the coming
         trial, increased the intensity of the publicity on the petitioner and together with
         the subsequent televising of the trial beginning 30 days later inherently
         prevented a sober search for the truth.

Id. at 550-51.

        Estes also addressed the effect of a judge allowing electronic media coverage of pretrial

proceedings:

         It is contended that this two-day pretrial hearing cannot be considered in
         determining the question before us. We cannot agree. Pretrial can create a
         major problem for the defendant in a criminal case. Indeed, it may be more
         harmful than publicity during the trial for it may well set the community
         opinion as to guilt or innocence. Though the September hearings dealt with
         motions to prohibit television coverage and to postpone the trial, they are
         unquestionably relevant to the issue before us. All of this two-day affair was
         highly publicized and could only have impressed those present, and also the
         community at large, with the notorious character of the petitioner as well as the
         proceeding.

Id. at 565.

        This Court has the right and duty to prohibit or restrict broadcasts of court proceedings in

the specific circumstances of this case under the federal and state constitutions, and under Rule



19
   The Court’s lead opinion in Estes was written by Justice Clark and concurred in by Chief
Justice Warren, and Justices Douglas and Goldberg. Chief Justice Warren wrote a separate
concurring opinion in which Justices Douglas and Goldberg joined. Justice Harlan also wrote a
concurring opinion and agreed with many of the points raised by Justice Clark and Chief Justice
Warren and concluded that “[a]t the present juncture I can only conclude that televised trials, at
least in cases like this one, possess such capabilities for interfering with the even course of the
judicial process that they are constitutionally banned.” Estes, 381 U.S. at 596 (Harlan, J.
concurring). See also Sinclair v. Turner, 434 P.2d 305, 307 n.6 (Utah 1967) (describing Estes as
a case where reversal was required because “there was a radical departure from the dignity and
decorum proper to maintain in a courtroom, the main aspect of which was making a public
drama of the trial by permitting televising and broadcasting during the trial.”); Conder, 668 P.2d
at 517 (citing Justice Clark’s lead opinion in Estes as demonstrating the “potential prejudicial
impact of televising jurors.”).

                                                 50
4-401.01(2)(A), (3)(B) and (5). Importantly, although Rule 4-401.01(2)(A) sets forth a state law

rule-based presumption in favor of electronic media coverage if, but only if ,”the predominant

purpose of the electronic media coverage request is journalism or dissemination of news to the

public,” that presumption can be overcome based on “some or all” of the nine factors set forth in

Paragraph 2(B).

       Under Paragraph 3(B), the court may also terminate or suspend electronic media

coverage at any time without prior notice “if the judge finds that continued electronic media

coverage is no longer appropriate based upon consideration of one or more of the factors in

Paragraph (2)(B).” Finally, under Paragraph (5), upon finding that anyone violated “this rule or

the court’s directives” the court may “terminate or suspend electronic media coverage[.]” Under

Paragraphs (2)(C) and 2(D), and 3(B), a decision to prohibit or restrict media access made under

Paragraph (2)(B) or Paragraph (3)(B) must be accompanied by particularized oral or written

findings that relate to the specific circumstances of the proceedings “rather than merely reflect

generalized views or preferences.” No such findings are required if the decision to prohibit or

restrict media access is made under Paragraph (5). 20




20
   No Utah court has yet interpreted Rule 4-401.01 and thus the Court is without any guidance as
to how to apply the Rule. As a foundational objection, Mr. Robinson objects to the use of the
Rule in this case because it violates the due process void for vagueness doctrine. See Emily
Ittner, Technology In The Courtroom: Promoting Transparency Or Destroying Solemnity?, 22
COMMLAW CONSPECTUS 347, 374-375 (2014), available at
https://scholarship.law.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=1541&context=commlaw (“Utah’s rule
…does not provide sufficient guidance to judges to help them in deciding whether to prohibit or
restrict coverage. The rule requires judges to consider “some or all” of nine factors when
determining whether the presumption of electronic coverage has been overcome and should be
prohibited or restricted. Offering a list of specific factors to aid judges in their decision making is
a positive step. However, the rule is still ambiguous, in that it does not state how judges must
weigh these factors in their determination of whether or not to limit or prohibit coverage.”).

                                                  51
       Three separate issues thus arise under Rule 4-401.01. The first issue is whether the

“predominant purpose” of the electronic media coverage is “journalism or dissemination of news

to the public” Utah R. Jud. Admin. 4-401.01(2)(A). If it is not, then there is no statutory

presumption in favor of electronic media coverage and no entitlement to such coverage. If it is,

then the second issue is whether the presumption has been overcome under the facts set forth in

Paragraph 2(B). The third issue is whether electronic media coverage should be prohibited or

restricted under Paragraph (5) given the electronic media’s admitted violation of the Court’s

decorum order, the consequences of that violation in causing the worldwide distribution of an

electronic fake confession and of other bogus analyses, and its continued violation of that order

as documented above. As argued below, these determinations can be made only after the Court

considers both argument and evidence bearing on the factors, but in this extraordinary case, even

without further evidence, the applicable factors and the electronic media’s violations of the

decorum order weigh heavily in favor of prohibiting any further electronic coverage of this case.

       Moreover, regardless of how the Court determines these three state law issues, it is

elementary but important to stress that a state statute or rule cannot trump the state or federal

constitution principles discussed in this motion. See United States v. Tsarnaev, 595 U.S. 302,

315-16 (2022) (“[S]upervisory rules cannot conflict with or circumvent a constitutional provision

or federal statute” and “most relevant here, lower courts cannot create prophylactic supervisory

rules that circumvent or supplement legal standards set out in decisions of this Court.”);

Chandler v. Florida, 449 U.S. 560, 582 (1981) (“We are not empowered by the Constitution to

oversee or harness state procedural experimentation; only when the state action infringes

fundamental guarantees are we authorized to intervene.” (emphasis added)); Estes, 381 U.S. at

566 (Warren, CJ, concurring) (noting with respect to the trial judge’s comments that “[t]his case



                                                 52
is not being tried under the Federal Constitution”, and that “[t]his Defendant has been brought

into this Court under the state laws, under the State Constitution”, that “I find it difficult to

believe that this trial judge, with over 20 years’ experience on the bench, was unfamiliar with the

fundamental duty imposed on him by Article VI [the Supremacy Clause] of the Constitution of

the United States.”).21

        The constitutional question of whether, in the circumstances of this capital case,

unlimited electronic media coverage of all aspects of this capital case should be permitted to

continue when the media has violated both the letter and spirit of the Court’s decorum order, and

when prejudicial electronic media pretrial publicity has been extreme and continuous and is

unlikely to abate, cannot be answered by merely invoking a rule-based presumption or the

media’s or the public’s “right of access.”

        The Supreme Court has repeatedly held that there is no constitutional right to have

judicial proceedings recorded and broadcast. Chandler, 449 U. S at 569; Nixon v. Warner

Communications, Inc., 435 U.S. 589, 590 (1978) (“[T]here is no constitutional right to have …

testimony recorded and broadcast.”); Estes, 381 U.S. at 539-42, 583-84, 588 (same).22


21
   The same supremacy principle applies to the state constitution and to decisions based on the
state constitution. See State v. DeJesus, 2017 UT 22, ¶ 34, 395 P.3d 111 (“[O]ur constitutional
decisions are set in relative stone in that [t]hey place matters resolved by them beyond the policy
reach of this or other branches of government, and they establish precedent that we ourselves
may be bound by under the doctrine of stare decisis.” (internal quotations omitted)).
22
   After Chandler, no Federal Circuit Court has opined that the Federal Constitution guarantees
the media or the public a right to record or broadcast court proceedings. See, e.g., Westmoreland
v. Columbia Broadcasting Sys., Inc., 752 F.2d 16, 23-24 (2d Cir.1984) (“There is a long leap ...
between a public right under the First Amendment to attend trials and a public right under the
First Amendment to see a given trial televised.”); Whiteland Woods, L.P. v. Township of W.
Whiteland, 193 F.3d 177, 183 (3d Cir.1999) (“The First Amendment does not require states to
accommodate every potential method of recording its proceedings, particularly where the public
is granted alternative means of compiling a comprehensive record.”); United States v. Edwards,
785 F.2d 1293, 1295-96 (5th Cir.1986) (First Amendment does not guarantee a positive right to
televise or broadcast criminal trials); Conway v. United States, 852 F.2d 187, 188-89 (6th

                                                  53
       The Supreme Court put the issue plainly and bluntly in Nixon:

         The First Amendment generally grants the press no right to information about
         a trial superior to that of the general public. Once beyond the confines of the
         courthouse, a news-gathering agency may publicize, within wide limits, what
         its representatives have heard and seen in the courtroom. But the line is drawn
         at the courthouse door; and within, a reporter’s constitutional rights are no
         greater than those of any other member of the public.

435 U.S. at 609 (internal quotation omitted).

       The present motion is not seeking to infringe either the media’s or the public’s “right of

access” to Mr. Robinson’s trial. And it is that “right of access,” not any alleged but non-existent

constitutional right of the electronic media to record and broadcast proceedings for profit or

sensationalism, that serves important constitutional interests. See Press-Enterprise Co. v.

Superior Ct. of California, Riverside Cnty, 464 U.S. 501, 508 (1984) (“[T]he sure knowledge

that anyone is free to attend gives assurance that established procedures are being followed and

that deviations will become known.” (emphasis in original)).

       Because, at most, the media in this case can assert only a “presumption” which is

grounded in a court rule and not in a constitutional provision, for purposes of analyzing Mr.

Robinson’s constitutional claims—any talk of a “strict scrutiny”, “compelling interest”, or “least

intrusive means” standard has no place. See, e.g., Globe Newspaper Co. v. Superior Ct. for



Cir.1988) (per curiam) (no First Amendment right to televise judicial proceedings); United States
v. Kerley, 753 F.2d 617, 621 (7th Cir.1985) (“exclusion of cameras from federal courtrooms is
constitutional”); Rice v. Kempker, 374 F.3d 675, 678 (8th Cir. 2004) (“[W]e hold that neither the
public nor the media has a First Amendment right to videotape, photograph, or make audio
recordings of government proceedings that are by law open to the public.”); Radio & Television
News Ass’n of S. California v. U.S. Dist. Ct. for Cent. Dist. of California, 781 F.2d 1443, 1447
(9th Cir. 1986) (“the media’s ‘right to gather information’ during a criminal trial is no more than
a right to attend the trial and report on their observations”); Combined Communications Corp. v.
Finesilver, 672 F.2d 818, 821 (10th Cir. 1982) (“The First Amendment does not guarantee the
media a constitutional right to televise inside a courthouse”); United States v. Hastings, 695 F.2d
1278, 1280 (11th Cir. 1983) (right of access does not extend to “the right to televise, photograph,
record, and broadcast federal criminal trials”).

                                                 54
Norfolk Cnty, 457 U.S. 596, 607 n. 17 (1982) (“Of course, limitations on the right of access that

resemble ‘time, place, and manner’ restrictions on protected speech, would not be subjected to

…strict scrutiny.”); United States v. Kerley, 753 F.2d 617, 620-21 (7th Cir. 1985) (“A limitation

on the public access to a trial is not subject to the same “strict scrutiny” given a denial of access.

All we have in the case before us is a limitation on the manner of news coverage; the media can

do everything but televise the trial. The limitation can withstand constitutional scrutiny so long

as it is reasonable and neutral, as with time, place, and manner restrictions generally.” (citing

Globe Newspaper Co and numerous other cases).

         Further, the Supreme Court has “always held that the atmosphere essential to the

preservation of a fair trial—the most fundamental of all freedoms—must be maintained at all

costs.” Estes, 381 U.S at 540. See also Press-Enterprise, 464 U.S. at 508 (“No right ranks higher

than the right of the accused to a fair trial.”); United States v. Tijerina, 412 F.2d 661, 667 (10th

Cir. 1969) (“The [First Amendment] argument necessarily places freedom of speech in a

preferred position above fair trial. Some decisions of the Supreme Court place First Amendment

rights in a preferred position. This preferred position has never been approved in a case where a

balance must be had between free speech and fair trial. Indeed, the Court has awarded the

preference to fair trial.” (citation omitted).) In other words, from a constitutional perspective, a

bare rule-based presumption can never outweigh Mr. Robinson’s compelling interest in a fair

trial.

         Lastly, and most importantly, the fact that the State is seeking the death penalty in this

case means that the Court must be especially vigilant to guard against the pernicious effects of

prejudicial pretrial publicity, and do everything it can to ensure that every procedural safeguard




                                                  55
is observed. See Gregg v. Georgia, 428 U.S. 153, 187 (1976) (“When a defendant’s life is at

stake, the Court has been particularly sensitive to insure that every safeguard is observed.”)

       The President of the United States, the Vice President, the Attorney General, the

Governor of the State of Utah, the County Attorney, law enforcement officials, and the electronic

media have all condemned Mr. Robinson before his trial has even begun. The President has told

a nationwide audience that “with a high degree of certainty, we have him”, that “I hope he is

going to be found guilty”, that “I hope he gets the death penalty”, that “the depraved assassin

who planned and executed Charlie’s killing has been arrested and charged with capital murder”,

and that “ God willing, he will receive the full and ultimate punishment for his horrific crime.”

See supra, TIMES NEWS, Donald Trump Confirms. As the Supreme Cout put it long ago:

         With such an opinion permeating their minds, it would be difficult to say that
         each [juror] could exclude this preconception of guilt from his deliberations.
         The influence that lurks in an opinion once formed is so persistent that it
         unconsciously fights detachment from the mental processes of the average
         man. Where one’s life is at stake—and accounting for the frailties of human
         nature—we can only say that in the light of the circumstances here the finding
         of impartiality does not meet constitutional standards…. With his life at stake,
         it is not requiring too much that petitioner be tried in an atmosphere
         undisturbed by so huge a wave of public passion[.]

Irvin v. Dowd, 366 U.S. 717, 727-28 (1961) (internal citations omitted).

       Consistent with Irvin, the Utah Supreme Court held in State v. James, that the fact that

the state is seeking the death penalty is an important factor in assessing the reasonable likelihood

that media coverage will prejudice the right to a fair trial:

         This is a capital case. Not only will a jury be required to determine the guilt or
         innocence of defendant, but if guilt is found, the jury will probably be urged by
         the prosecution to impose the death penalty. In deciding whether to impose the
         death penalty, the jury must weigh aggravating circumstances against
         mitigating circumstances. This is the most momentous judgment a jury can be
         asked to make. The judgment should be made in an atmosphere as free from
         any taint of bias or prejudice as is reasonably possible.



                                                  56
767 P.2d 549, 555 (Utah 1989). See also State v. Arguelles, 2003 UT 1, ¶ 102, 63 P.3d 731

(“We… encourage trial judges…to continue to exercise caution in maintaining quiet and non-

distracting courtroom environments in all cases, and particularly in capital proceedings.”);

Sampson v. United States, 724 F.3d 150, 163 (1st Cir. 2013) (“The right to an impartial jury is

nowhere as precious as when a defendant is on trial for his life.”).

       The prejudicial electronic pretrial publicity in this case, with its obsession on courtroom

attire and restraints, mugshots, demeanor, and other irrelevant and prejudicial factors, has

imperiled not only Mr. Robinson’s constitutional right to an impartial guilt and penalty phase

jury, but also his Eighth Amendment and article 1, section 9 rights to be free from cruel and

unusual punishment. See Beck v. Alabama, 447 U.S. 625, 638 (1980) (“To insure that the death

penalty is indeed imposed on the basis of reason rather than caprice or emotion, we have

invalidated procedural rules that tended to diminish the reliability of the sentencing

determination. The same reasoning must apply to rules that diminish the reliability of the guilt

determination.” (internal quotation omitted)); Caldwell v. Mississippi, 472 U.S. 320, 340 (1985)

(invalidating a procedure that was “incompatible with the Eighth Amendment’s heightened need

for reliability in the determination that death is the appropriate punishment in a specific case.”

(internal quotation omitted)).

       Indeed, the Supreme Court “has stressed the ‘acute need’ for reliable decisionmaking when

the death penalty is at issue.” Deck v. Missouri, 544 U.S. 622, 632 (2005). And it is well

established that reliable decisionmaking must be grounded in admissible evidence, not on innuendo

derived from the defendant’s custodial status, demeanor, or prejudicial pretrial publicity. See,

Holbrook v. Flynn, 475 U.S. 560, 567 (1986) (“Central to the right to a fair trial, guaranteed by the

Sixth and Fourteenth Amendments, is the principle that one accused of a crime is entitled to have



                                                 57
his guilt or innocence determined solely on the basis of the evidence introduced at trial, and not on

grounds of official suspicion, indictment, continued custody, or other circumstances not adduced as

proof at trial.”); Estes, 381 U.S. at 564 (Warren, CJ, concurring) (“[T]he criminal trial under our

Constitution has a clearly defined purpose, to provide a fair and reliable determination of guilt, and

no procedure or occurrence which seriously threatens to divert it from that purpose can be

tolerated.”)

        This Courts factual findings quoted above with respect to Mr. Robinson’s jail attire and

shackles and the likely effect that publicity about such factors will have on a Utah County jury

pool have important implications under the Sixth, Eighth, and Fourteenth Amendment to the

United States Constitution, and under Utah Constitution, article I, sections 7 (due process), 9

(prohibition against cruel and unusual punishment), 10 (“In capital cases the right of trial by jury

shall remain inviolate”), and 12 (right to counsel and to a public trial by an impartial jury).

        In light of the factual circumstances outlined above, continuing to broadcast the

proceedings in this capital case threatens to undermine Mr. Robinson’s fundamental right to a

fair and reliable trial and sentencing hearing by an impartial jury. The United States Supreme

Court has noted that cameras in the courtroom can have a prejudicial effect on the performance

of the jurors, the trial court judge, witnesses and the defendant. See Estes, 381 U.S. at 545-50.

        While the United States Supreme Court has not adopted a per se ban on cameras in the

courtroom for all cases, it has ruled that in notorious cases the broadcast of the proceedings

constitutes prejudicial, reversible constitutional error. See id.; Chandler, 449 U.S. at 582. The

present case is the type of case where broadcasting the case would cause irreparable bias and

prejudice to Mr. Robinson. This case has been highly publicized and emotional since its




                                                  58
inception. Given the nature and history of the case, the exclusion of the electronic media is

necessary to preserve Mr. Robinson’s rights under the United States and Utah Constitutions.

II.    GIVEN THE PARTICULAR CIRCUMSTANCES OF THIS CAPITAL CASE,
       WHERE THE AMOUNT OF PREJUDICIAL ELECTRONIC MEDIA PRETRIAL
       PUBLICITY IS ALREADY EXTREMELY HIGH AND IS LIKELY TO
       CONTINUE, AND WHERE THE ELECTRONIC MEDIA HAS ALREADY
       VIOLATED THE COURT’S DECORUM ORDER AND DIRECTLY CAUSED
       FURTHER PREJUDICE , CONTINUING TO ALLOW LIVE STREAMING OF
       ALL PROCEEDINGS WOULD INFRINGE MR. ROBINSON’S FUNDAMENTAL
       RIGHT TO A FAIR AND RELIABLE TRIAL AND SENTENCING HEARING BY
       AN IMPARTIAL JURY AS GUARANTEED BY THE SIXTH, EIGHTH, AND
       FOURTEENTH AMENDMENTS TO THE UNITED STATES CONSTITUTON
       AND THEIR UTAH CONSTITUTIONAL COUNTERPARTS.

       A.      The Court Has a Constitutional Duty to Take Strong Measures to Ensure
               That Mr. Robinson’s Right to a Fair Trial by an Impartial Jury Is Protected
               From Prejudicial Electronic Pretrial Publicity.

       The Utah Supreme Court has long noted, “It is well established that due process

requirements of the Constitutions of Utah and the United States guarantee that an accused

receive a trial before a fair and impartial jury free from outside influences.” State v. Pierre, 572

P.2d 1338, 1348 (Utah 1977) (footnote omitted). Further, “[t]here can be no doubt that pretrial or

during-trial publicity that comes to the attention of prospective or sitting jurors can deprive a

criminal defendant of a fair trial. Appellate courts have been obliged to reverse convictions on

this ground where the publicity was pervasive and prejudicial.” Conder, 668 P.2d at 518. The

United States Supreme Court has dealt with the detriments of excessive media involvement in

cases by reversing a number of criminal convictions on the ground that excessive pretrial

publicity, of the same or even lesser type and intensity as that present in this case, deprived the

defendants of due process. See Estes, 381 U.S. at 533-52 (1965) (holding that a defendant had

been denied due process because a pre-trial hearing had been televised live and then rebroadcast,

and because the court proceedings had been disrupted by the presence of the media); Rideau ,

373 U.S. at 723-27 (reversing a conviction after the defendant had been denied a change of

                                                 59
venue even though a local television station had broadcast his recorded confession three times,

and 106,000 of the estimated 150,000-person community viewed the broadcast); Irvin, 366 U.S.

at 727-28 (reversing a conviction where pre-trial publicity distributed in the vicinity of the trial

included, inter alia, media accounts of the defendant’s juvenile record, his confessions, and

previous court-martial proceedings); Marshall v. United States, 360 U.S. 310 (1959) (per curiam)

(reversing a conviction because seven of twelve jurors had been exposed to news accounts of

evidence that was not admitted at trial). The leading case during this era, which identified the

need for trial publicity reform and shaped the American Bar Association’s remedial measures,

was Sheppard v. Maxwell, 384 U.S. 333 (1966). See supra, n. 2. There, the Court, on due process

grounds, reversed the murder conviction of Sam Sheppard, whose high-profile trial had been

preceded and pervaded by a media frenzy. Id. at 363. Newspapers had documented Sheppard’s

alleged refusal to cooperate with investigating officials and had published articles discussing

incriminating evidence that was never admitted at trial. Id. at 338-41. Furthermore, reporters had

crowded the defense table at trial, making it very difficult for Sheppard to have private

discussions with his counsel. Id. Despite the chaotic conditions, the trial judge refused to allow a

change of venue and failed to take steps to control the adverse effects of the publicity. Id. at 354

n. 9, 358-59.

       The Supreme Court admonished the trial court in Sheppard for its failure to control the

extrajudicial publicity:

         The fact that many of the prejudicial news items can be traced to the
         prosecution, as well as the defense, aggravates the judge’s failure to take any
         action. Effective control of these sources—concededly within the court’s
         power—might well have prevented the divulgence of inaccurate information,
         rumors, and accusations that made up much of the inflammatory publicity[.]




                                                 60
Id. at 361 (internal citation omitted).23 The Court suggested how the trial judge could have

minimized the prejudicial publicity, including proscribing extrajudicial statements by lawyers

and other trial participants, requesting local officials to implement regulations with respect to the

dissemination of trial information, and warning news media about the impropriety of publicizing

material not introduced at the proceeding. Id. at 361-62. Emphasizing the prejudicial effect of

news media on fair trials, the Court iterated:

         Due process requires that the accused receive a trial by an impartial jury free
         from outside influences. Given the pervasiveness of modern communications
         and the difficulty of effacing prejudicial publicity from the minds of the jurors,
         the trial courts must take strong measures to ensure that the balance is never
         weighed against the accused.... [W]here there is a reasonable likelihood that
         prejudicial news prior to trial will prevent a fair trial, the judge should continue
         until the threat abates, or transfer it to another county not so permeated with
         publicity.

Id. at 362-63. Moreover, the Court recognized that repeatedly reversing convictions would not

suffice as a long-term remedy for the harm of trial publicity. The Court recommended an

alternative solution:

         But we must remember that reversals are but palliatives; the cure lies in those
         remedial measures that will prevent the prejudice at its inception. The courts
         must take such steps by rule and regulation that will protect their processes
         from prejudicial outside interferences. Neither prosecutors, counsel for
         defense, the accused, witnesses, court staff nor enforcement officers coming
         under the jurisdiction of the court should be permitted to frustrate its function.
         Collaboration between counsel and the press as to information affecting the
         fairness of a criminal trial is not only subject to regulation, but it is highly
         censurable and worthy of disciplinary measures.




23
  Here, in contrast to Sheppard, the Court will search the substantial pretrial publicity in this
case in vain for any indication that the defense lawyers representing Mr. Robinson have held
press conferences or otherwise spoken to the media.



                                                 61
Id. at 363. 24

        Under Sheppard and its progeny, this Court has a constitutional duty to construe Rule 4-

401.01 in a manner that adequately protects Mr. Robinson’s due process rights, including his

right to a fair trial. See Gannett Co. v. DePasquale, 443 U.S. 368, 378 (1979) (“a trial judge has

an affirmative constitutional duty” to protect a criminal defendant’s due process rights). Because

it is difficult to remedy any violation of Mr. Robinson’s constitutional rights after the fact, “our

criminal justice system permits, and even encourages, trial judges to be over-cautious in ensuring

that a defendant will receive a fair trial.” Id. at 379 n. 6.; also id. at 379 (“And because of the

Constitution’s pervasive concern for these due process rights, a trial judge may surely take

protective measures even when they are not strictly and inescapably necessary.”).

        B.       The Prejudicial Pretrial Electronic Media Coverage in This Case Clearly
                 Meets the Threshold Standard Set Forth in Sheppard for Invoking This
                 Court’s Affirmative Constitutional Duty to Take Protective Measures to
                 Protect Mr. Robinson’s Due Process Rights by Prohibiting Further
                 Electronic Media Live Streaming.

                 1.    The Sheppard Standard Has Clearly Been Met.

        Sheppard’s strictures that trial courts must take “strong measures to ensure that the

balance is never weighed against the accused”, and that “courts must take such steps by rule and

regulation that will protect their processes from prejudicial outside interferences”, retain special

vitality today, in the age of the widespread dissemination of information and disinformation on

internet/social media platforms by those whose opinions hold special weight in our increasingly

polarized society. For example, when President Trump, as a criminal defendant, used social

media to make threats to and derogatory comments about trial participants in his pending federal


24
  Mr. Robinson discusses below why this and other traditional means short of prohibiting further
electronic media coverage will not be effective in this unique case.



                                                  62
criminal case, the district court stepped in and issued a gag order after the President continued

with such posts after being specifically warned not to do so. See United States v. Trump, 88 F.4th

990, 998 (D.C. Cir. 2023) (“Before and after the district court’s warning, Mr. Trump repeatedly

used his public platform to denigrate and attack those involved in the criminal case against

him.”).

          In upholding the essential part of that gag order on appeal, and concluding that “the

district court had the authority to prevent Mr. Trump from laundering communications

concerning witnesses and addressing their potential trial participation through social media

postings or other public comments”, the appellate court relied primarily on Sheppard and its

progeny, reasoning, “[W]hen a case involves extensive media coverage and public interest, or

when the parties are trying the case in the media rather than the courtroom, a court cannot sit

back and wait for a “carnival atmosphere” to descend before acting. Quite the opposite. Id. at

1004 (citing Sheppard, 384 U.S. at 356-63). Quoting United States v. Nixon, 418 U.S. 683, 707

(1974), the Trump Court noted, “[T]he primary constitutional duty of the Judicial Branch [is] to

do justice in criminal prosecutions[.]” Thus, “[a]s part of that duty, courts must ‘prevent the

prejudice’ to the trial process “at its inception.’” Id. (quoting Sheppard, 384 U.S. at 363) (citing

to Nebraska Press Ass’n. v. Stuart, 427 U.S. 539, 553 (1976) (The cure for prejudice to the trial

“lies in those remedial measures that will prevent the prejudice at its inception.”)) Indeed,

           That is because waiting until the trial is over and reversing the conviction
           would be an ineffective, costly, and wasteful “palliative,” inflicting the
           additional burdens on a defendant and extra expenses on the taxpayers of a
           retrial in an already contaminated public atmosphere, with witness recall and
           evidence growing staler all the while.

Id. (quoting Sheppard, 384 U.S. at 363). The Trump Court thus instructed:

           As a result, courts have an ongoing obligation to ensure that speech about a
           criminal case does not divert the trial from the very purpose of a court system,


                                                  63
          which is to adjudicate controversies, both criminal and civil, in the calmness
          and solemnity of the courtroom according to legal procedures. Due process
          demands that the conclusions to be reached in a case will be induced only by
          evidence and argument in open court, and not by any outside influence,
          whether of private talk or public print. Id. at 351… (quoting Patterson v.
          Colorado ex rel. Attorney General, 205 U.S. 454, 462… (1907)); The courts’
          duty to protect trials from outside influence includes protecting court personnel
          from both the reality and the appearance of undue outside pressure. The
          Supreme Court “has recognized that the unhindered and untrammeled
          functioning of our courts is part of the very foundation of our constitutional
          democracy.” Cox, 379 U.S. at 562[.].

Id. at 1004-05 (additional internal citations and quotation marks omitted; cleaned up). See also

Geders v. United States, 425 U.S. 80, 86-87 (1976) (“If truth and fairness are not to be sacrificed,

the judge must exert substantial control over the proceedings.”).

         Significant to the present case, where President Trump has expressed his strong opinion

through electronic media that Mr. Robinson is part of a “radical left” conspiracy, and a

“depraved assassin”25 who should be convicted and executed, the court also upheld the gag order

in part because “President Trump’s words have real-world consequences.” Trump, 88 F.4th at

1011. The Trump Court elaborated:

                The record also shows that former President Trump’s words have real-
          world consequences. Many of those on the receiving end of his attacks
          pertaining to the 2020 election have been subjected to a torrent of threats and
          intimidation from his supporters. A day after Mr. Trump’s “IF YOU GO
          AFTER ME, I’M COMING AFTER YOU!” post, someone called the district
          court and said: “Hey you stupid slave n[****]r[.] * * * If Trump doesn’t get
          elected in 2024, we are coming to kill you, so tread lightly b[***]h. * * * You
          will be targeted personally, publicly, your family, all of it.” Special Counsel
          Br. 5; see United States v. Shry, No. 4:23-cr-413, ECF 1 at 3 (Criminal
          Complaint) (S.D. Tex. Aug. 11, 2023). The Special Counsel also has advised
          that he has received threats, and that a prosecutor in the Special Counsel’s
          office whom Mr. Trump has singled out for criticism has been “subject to
          intimidating communications.” Special Counsel Mot. 12.

               The former President has repeatedly attacked both the presiding judge and
          his law clerk in a New York state-law lawsuit. Since those attacks, the judge’s

media are, among other things, “when the statement was made in relation to trial, the attention

the case and comments received, and the role and reputation of the speaker.” (emphasis added)).

       In a national public opinion poll taken shortly after Mr. Kirk’s death, just 6% of

said it is a problem, with a majority (59% of U.S. adults) saying political violence is a very big
