       Given these opinion polls, the fact that Mr. Trump’s popularity in Utah County and in

Utah generally is extremely high, the vehemence, scope, and frequency of the attacks that he and

his supporters have mounted against Mr. Robinson, and this Court’s existing findings of fact

quoted above, it should be concluded that he easily meets the standard set forth in Sheppard for

determining whether a notorious criminal case has reached the point where the trial court has a

duty to take “strong measures” to ensure a defendant’s fundamental right to a fair trial by an

impartial jury. That standard is that the defendant need only show that there is “a reasonable

likelihood that prejudicial news prior to trial will prevent a fair trial.” Sheppard, 384 U.S. at 363.

This is the same standard used by the Utah Supreme Court in assessing the need for a change of



                                                  67
venue. See James, 767 P.2d at 555. And it is the same standard used in Rule 4-401.01(2)(B) to

assess whether electronic media should be prohibited or restricted as a matter of state law.

       A full application of that standard to this case is set forth below in relation to the analysis

of Rule 4-401.01(2)(B). Here, Mr. Robinson emphasizes a critical point which leaves no doubt

that he has met the Sheppard standard. More specifically, the Trump-related prejudicial publicity

concerning Mr. Robinson’s alleged connection with the “radical left” is certainly highly

prejudicial and is alone sufficient to satisfy the Sheppard/James reasonable likelihood standard ,

but it is only one of many judicially-recognized categories of prejudicial pretrial publicity that

are present in this case. See, fn. 4, supra. It will be sufficient for present purposes to discuss only

four of those categories.

       First, there are thousands of posts relating to the prosecutor’s allegation that Mr.

Robinson confessed to his roommate and parents, and the alleged lip reader video confession has

also been widely circulated. As noted above in Rideau, the Supreme Court reversed a conviction

solely because the media had publicized a similar video confession. 373 U.S. at 723-27. And as

Skilling and numerous other cases make clear, pretrial publicity about an alleged confession

gives rise to a presumption of prejudice. See, e.g., Skilling, 561 U.S. at 382 (a presumption of

juror prejudice is warranted where the pretrial publicity references a “confession or other

blatantly prejudicial information of the type readers or viewers could not reasonably be expected

to shut from sight.”); Chicago Council of Laws. v. Bauer, 522 F.2d 242, 254 (7th Cir. 1975)

(“DR 7-107(B)(3) prevents comments relative to confessions or statements by the accused.

Disclosures concerning such matters are fraught with almost incurable prejudice.”).

       Second, the Utah Governor, FBI Director Patel, and FBI Deputy Bongino, and others

have all commented that Mr. Robinson is not cooperating with law enforcement, and the



                                                  68
electronic media has dutifully broadcast these comments nationally, often with screen shots such

as the following:




Hannity Staff, HANNITY MONOLOGUE: This Out to be a Moment Where the Whole Country is

United [WATCH], HANNITY, at 1:14 (Sep. 16, 2025), https://hannity.com/media-room/hannity-

monologue-this-ought-to-be-a-moment-where-the-whole-country-is-united-watch/.

       The Seventh Circuit’s decision in Chicago Council of Laws. v. Bauer, is helpful on this

point as well:

         Disclosures concerning such matters are fraught with almost incurable
         prejudice. They could infringe on a defendant’s Fifth Amendment privilege. In
         view of the natural tendency to draw an adverse inference from the assertion of
         the privilege and the careful attempts to avoid comment about such a choice
         during a trial, it seems that courts at least can require that attorneys who
         publicly discuss such matters must prove that their comment was not of the
         type that poses a serious and imminent threat of interference with a fair trial.

522 F.2d at 254.




                                               69
       The State will not be able to produce such proof in this case. Discovery Bates 000007 at

p. 20 indicates that on the evening of his arrest “Tyler Robinson declined to be interviewed

without having an attorney present.” A video of the encounter at Bates 003996-R2 at timestamp

03:36:53 indicates the following:

               06:25 PM / 03:36:53
               So Tyler, um, before we begin, I’m just gonna read you your rights. Mm-hmm
       <affirmative>. Okay. Um, you have the right to remain silent. Anything you say can be
       used against you in court. You have the right to talk to a lawyer before advice before we
       ask you any questions. You have the right to have a lawyer with you during questioning.
       If you cannot afford a lawyer, one will be appointed for you before any questioning
       begins. And if you decide to answer questions now, you can stop at any time. Okay. Um,
       so are you, are you good to proceed? I’d love to talk to you.

              06:26 PM / 03:37:38
              Uh, I would like to speak with my, uh, legal counsel. I’m hoping for a fellow in
       Utah named Doug Terry.

               06:26 PM / 03:37:38
               Okay.

              06:26 PM / 03:37:39
              Uh, told he’s a pretty good, uh, attorney here, but I, I think my folks are trying to
       get ahold of him, but I think his office is closed for the night.

               06:26 PM / 03:37:52
               Okay.

              06:26 PM / 03:37:52
              But, uh, until I’ve had the opportunity to speak with someone, I would like to
       remain silent.

These are facts that the Governor, the FBI Director, the FBI Deputy Director, and every other

law enforcement officer who told the media that Mr. Robinson was refusing to cooperate with

law enforcement knew or should have known. Yet not one governmental official who made such

statements, or any of the electronic media persons who reported such statements, ever mentioned

that Mr. Robinson had a Fifth and Sixth Amendment right to remain silent until his attorney was

present, and that listeners should not draw an adverse inference from such silence. See Doyle v.


                                                70
Ohio, 426 U.S. 610, 619 (1976) (“We hold that the use for impeachment purposes of petitioners’

silence, at the time of arrest and after receiving Miranda warnings, violated the Due Process

Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment.”); State v. Wiswell, 639 P.2d 146, 147 (Utah 1981) (“The

continued attempts by the prosecutor to put the defendant’s silence before the jury after his

having been advised of his right to remain silent amounts to prosecutorial misconduct. The

references to defendant’s silence are fundamental error, which could have affected the result and

are therefore prejudicial.”). See also, Florida. v. Bostick, 501 U.S. 429, 437 (1991) (“[A]an

individual may decline an officer’s request without fearing prosecution…. We have consistently

held that a refusal to cooperate, without more, does not furnish the minimal level of objective

justification needed for a detention or seizure.”).

       Third, another type of prejudicial electronic media coverage that gives rise to a

presumption of prejudice in this case is the electronic media’s insistence on giving prominent

attention to demeaning and grossly unfair characterizations. For example, President Trump has

called Mr. Robinson a “monster”, and a “depraved assassin.”, Vice President Vance described

Mr. Robinson as a person who is “everything that the far left wants from our young men [who]

rejected the conservatism and the spirituality, the values of a small-town family [and]…moved

into a small apartment, he became addicted to porn, he became addicted to hate, and he ended up

sleeping with somebody who doesn’t know whether they’re a man or a woman”. U.S. Attorney

General Bondi called Mr. Robinson a “horrible human being”. Governor Cox called Mr.

Robinson an “evil human being” and described him as a person “deeply indoctrinated by leftist

ideology”. A Retired FBI Agent Parker deemed Mr. Robinson a “true sociopath”. And political

commentator and attorney Megyn Kelly called Mr. Robinson an “evil, evil human being”.




                                                 71
       This type of character assassination has also been held condemned in numerous cases

See, e.g,, United States v. Casellas-Toro, 807 F.3d 380, 387 (1st Cir. 2015) (prejudice presumed

where “[l]ike the broadcasts of Rideau’s confession, the media here publicized ‘blatantly

prejudicial information of the type readers or viewers could not reasonably be expected to shut

from sight.’. The media reported rumors about Casellas’s character—that he was a drug user,

threatened people with firearms, was involved in a hit-and-run vehicle accident, and bragged

about assassinating the then-governor of Puerto Rico. The public took to Facebook and Twitter

to publicly discuss Casellas’s case.” (quoting Skilling, 561 U.S. at 382); Bauer, 522 F.2d at 254

(“DR 7-107(B)(1) prohibits communication concerning ‘character, reputation, or prior criminal

record.’ The possible prejudicial effect of negative comments on such matters is self-evident.”)

       Yet a fourth area of presumptive prejudice that is involved in this case is the numerous

statements by the President, the Governor, and law enforcement officials stating their opinion as

to Mr. Robinson’s guilt, or, in the case of the County Attorney, as to the existence of aggravating

factors relating to the victim, and the propriety of the death penalty. See Leavitt, 2025 UT 46, ¶

61 (upholding disciplinary sanction against former Utah County District Attorney because “his

expression of belief in Baum’s guilt, and his other inflammatory statements did a disservice to

the legal system by creating a substantial risk of tainting the jury pool, thereby jeopardizing a

criminal defendant’s constitutional right to a fair and impartial trial. Further, these comments

impinged upon the presumption of innocence that is so important to our criminal justice

system.”); Id. ¶ 39 (“Leavitt’s position as the County Attorney made the potential for prejudice

even higher. His statements likely carried more weight in the eyes of the public.”).

       As stated in Bauer, “[t]he prohibition on opinion as to guilt or innocence is permissible

since this is a traditional ethical requirement in the legal profession and such an opinion could



                                                 72
never properly be offered in court.” 522 F.2d at 254. The same statement is also obviously true

with respect to a statement of opinion about aggravating factors relating to the victim or the

appropriateness of the death penalty, either by lawyers, or of even more concern here, by

influential politicians and the victim’s family. See Leavitt, 2025 UT 46, ¶ 38 (“Additionally,

Leavitt made the remarks at a live-streamed press conference in which he announced that his

Office was seeking the death penalty against Baum. This was a high-profile case that had already

received much media attention. And the press was in attendance that day. So Leavitt knew that

his statements would be broadcasted and reported on, including in Utah County where the

prospective jury pool lived.”); Weaver v. Bowersox, 438 F.3d 832, 840-41 (8th Cir. 2006)

(petitioner was entitled to habeas relief based in part on the prosecutor’s improper statements

during closing argument in the penalty phase “about his personal belief in the death penalty”;

concluding that “[s]]tatements about the prosecutor’s personal belief in the death penalty are

inappropriate and contrary to a reasoned opinion by the jury,” and noting that “[a] prosecutor

should not emphasize his or her position of authority in making death penalty determinations

because it may encourage the jury to defer to the prosecutor’s judgment.”); Bates v. Bell, 402

F.3d 635, 644 (6th Cir. 2005) (“In the capital sentencing context, prosecutors are prohibited from

expressing their personal opinion as to the existence of aggravating or mitigating circumstances

and the appropriateness of the death penalty. Jurors are mindful that the prosecutor represents the

State and are apt to afford undue respect to the prosecutor’s personal assessment.”). See also,

supra n. 16.

       In sum, the prejudicial pretrial electronic media coverage in this case clearly meets the

threshold standard set forth in Sheppard for invoking this Court’s affirmative constitutional duty

to take protective measures to protect Mr. Robinson’s due process rights.



                                                73
               2.      The United States Supreme Court’s Camera-In-The Courtroom Cases
                       Strongly Support Granting This Motion and Establish Mr. Robinson’s
                       Right to an Evidentiary Hearing to Present Empirical Data and Other
                       Evidence in Support of This Motion.

        As indicated above, the Supreme Court first addressed the broadcasting of criminal

proceedings in Estes v. Texas, 381 U.S. 532 (1965). There, the Court reversed a state court

defendant’s criminal conviction because it found that the televised broadcasting of the trial had

created such a strong likelihood of prejudice that it violated the defendant’s due process rights

under the Fourteenth Amendment. Id. at 542. Justice Clark’s lead opinion began by noting that

         At the outset the motion should be dispelled that telecasting is dangerous
         because it is new. It is true that our empirical knowledge of its full effect on the
         public, the jury or the participants in a trial, including the judge, witnesses and
         lawyers, is limited. However, the nub of the question is not its newness but, as
         Mr. Justice Douglas says, ‘the insidious influences which it puts to work in the
         administration of justice.’ Douglas, The Public Trial and the Free Press, 33
         Rocky Mt. L. Rev. 1 (1960).

Id. at 541.

        The Court then noted the “numerous situations in which [televising a trial] might cause

actual unfairness—some so subtle as to defy detection by the accused or control of the judge.”

Id. at 544-45. In particular, it explained that broadcasting criminal trials might undermine a

court’s ability to invoke Rule 615 of the Federal Rules of Evidence, which permits the exclusion

of potential witnesses from the courtroom to prevent them from being influenced by others’

testimony. Broadcasting would enable excluded witnesses to hear others’ testimony and adjust

their own testimony accordingly. Id. at 547. Likewise, broadcasting would amplify the risk that

jurors could be tainted from exposure to the coverage. Id. at 545-46. Finally, the Court noted that

cameras in the courtroom could subject trial participants—jurors, witnesses, attorneys, and

judges—to psychological pressures from the knowledge that their actions would be reproduced




                                                 74
and disseminated publicly, potentially distorting the trial process by causing participants to

conduct themselves differently. Id. at 545-50.

        As indicated above, Justice Harlan wrote a concurring opinion and agreed with many of

the points raised by Justice Clark’s lead opinion and Chief Justice Warren’s concurring opinion

and he ultimately concluded that “[a]t the present juncture I can only conclude that televised

trials, at least in cases like this one, possess such capabilities for interfering with the even course

of the judicial process that they are constitutionally banned.” Estes, 381 U.S. at 596 (Harlan, J.

concurring).

        Subsequently, in Chandler v. Florida, the Supreme Court clarified that “Estes did not

announce a constitutional rule that all photographic or broadcast coverage of criminal trials is

inherently a denial of due process.” 449 U.S. at 573-74. In the Court’s view, the critical language

in Justice Harlan’s concurring opinion that resulted in a five member majority was his statement

at the beginning of his opinion that “‘[m]y conclusion is that there is no constitutional

requirement that television be allowed in the courtroom, and, at least as to a notorious criminal

trial such as this one, the considerations against allowing television in the courtroom so far

outweigh the countervailing factors advanced in its support as to require a holding that what was

done in this case infringed the fundamental right to a fair trial assured by the Due Process Clause

of the Fourteenth Amendment.’” Id. at 571 (quoting Estes, 381 U.S. at 587 (Harlan, J.

concurring) (emphasis added)).

        In Chandler, which was a routine burglary case, the Court upheld the facial

constitutionality of an experimental program in Florida allowing electronic media coverage in

any criminal case, even over a defendant’s objection. 449 U.S. at 566. The defendant contended

that the program was unconstitutional because Estes established a per se ban against electronic



                                                  75
recordings in all cases, regardless of circumstances. Id. at 570. The Court rejected that contention

and upheld the Florida program because it contained certain essential procedural safeguards. Id.

at 566. Most importantly, it provided that “[t]he judge has sole and plenary discretion to exclude

coverage of certain witnesses, and the jury may not be filmed. The judge has discretionary power

to forbid coverage whenever satisfied that coverage may have a deleterious effect on the

paramount right of the defendant to a fair trial.” Id. (emphasis added). The Court further stressed

that “Florida admonishes its courts to take special pains to protect certain witnesses—for

example, children, victims of sex crimes, some informants, and even the very timid witness or

party—from the glare of publicity and the tensions of being ‘on camera’”, and that “the Florida

guidelines place on trial judges positive obligations to be on guard to protect the fundamental

right of the accused to a fair trial.” Id. at 576-77. The Court also noted that the program

guaranteed the right to an evidentiary hearing:

         Inherent in electronic coverage of a trial is a risk that the very awareness by the
         accused of the coverage and the contemplated broadcast may adversely affect
         the conduct of the participants and the fairness of the trial, yet leave no
         evidence of how the conduct or the trial’s fairness was affected. Given this
         danger, it is significant that Florida requires that objections of the accused to
         coverage be heard and considered on the record by the trial court. In addition
         to providing a record for appellate review, a pretrial hearing enables a
         defendant to advance the basis of his objection to broadcast coverage and
         allows the trial court to define the steps necessary to minimize or eliminate the
         risks of prejudice to the accused. Experiments such as the one presented here
         may well increase the number of appeals by adding a new basis for claims to
         reverse, but this is a risk Florida has chosen to take after preliminary
         experimentation. Here, the record does not indicate that appellants requested
         an evidentiary hearing to show adverse impact or injury. Nor does the record
         reveal anything more than generalized allegations of prejudice.

Id. at 577 (internal citation omitted).

        The Court went on to observe that the existing empirical studies in favor of allowing

electronic media coverage of trials were “limited” and “non-scientific”. Id. at 576, n. 11 (citation



                                                  76
omitted). The Court went on to observe that “further experimentation is necessary to evaluate the

potential psychological prejudice associated with broadcast coverage of trials. Further

developments and more data are required before this issue can be finally resolved.” Id. at 579, n.

12.

        The Court further stressed the need for the defendant to present evidence by observing:

         To say that the appellants have not demonstrated that broadcast coverage is
         inherently a denial of due process is not to say that the appellants were in fact
         accorded all of the protections of due process in their trial. As noted earlier, a
         defendant has the right on review to show that the media’s coverage of his
         case—printed or broadcast—compromised the ability of the jury to judge him
         fairly. Alternatively, a defendant might show that broadcast coverage of his
         particular case had an adverse impact on the trial participants sufficient to
         constitute a denial of due process. Neither showing was made in this case.

Id. at 581.

        Finally, the Court made it clear that it was distinguishing the routine burglary case before

it from the kind of “notorious case” that five members of the Court in ruled could not

constitutionally be televised:

         The concurring opinion of Chief Justice Warren joined by Justices Douglas
         and Goldberg in Estes can fairly be read as viewing the very broadcast of some
         trials as potentially a form of punishment in itself —a punishment before guilt.
         This concern is far from trivial. But, whether coverage of a few trials will, in
         practice, be the equivalent of a “Yankee Stadium” setting—which Justice
         Harlan likened to the public pillory long abandoned as a barbaric perversion of
         decent justice—must also await the continuing experimentation.

Id. at 580-81.

        The risk of prejudice to particular defendants is ever present and must be examined
        carefully as cases arise. Nothing of the “Roman circus” or “Yankee Stadium”
        atmosphere, as in Estes, prevailed here, however, nor have appellants attempted to show
        that the unsequestered jury was exposed to “sensational” coverage, in the sense of Estes
        or of Sheppard v. Maxwell, 384 U.S. 333…. Absent a showing of prejudice of
        constitutional dimensions to these defendants, there is no reason for this Court either to
        endorse or to invalidate Florida’s experiment.

Id. at 582.


                                                 77
        Chandler is important to the present case for four reasons.

        First, it establishes that to pass muster under the federal constitution, a local rule

permitting electronic media coverage over the defendant’s objection must contain certain

procedural safeguards, including the right to an evidentiary hearing and a standard guiding the

decision that provides a trial court with “discretionary power to forbid coverage whenever

satisfied that coverage may have a deleterious effect on the paramount right of the defendant to a

fair trial.” Id. at 566 (emphasis added).

        Second, it establishes that independent of any state rule, a defendant has a due process

right to an evidentiary hearing to demonstrate that “the media’s coverage of his case—printed or

broadcast—compromised the ability of the jury to judge him fairly. Alternatively, a defendant

might show that broadcast coverage of his particular case had an adverse impact on the trial

participants sufficient to constitute a denial of due process. Neither showing was made in this

case.” Id. at 581.

        Third, Chandler expressly recognized in 1981 that “further experimentation [was]

necessary to evaluate the potential psychological prejudice associated with broadcast coverage of

trials”, and that “[f]urther developments and more data are required before this issue can be

finally resolved.” Id. at 579, n. 12. As Exhibit 2, the Declaration of Dr. Christine L. Ruva, PH.D,

demonstrates, the requested studies have now been done by her and other researchers. Under

Chandler, Mr. Robinson is entitled to present the results of those studies and other evidence

through the testimony of Dr. Ruva and by other means.

        Lastly, Chandler establishes that the consensus reached by five members of the Court in

Estes that electronic media coverage is unconstitutional per se in notorious cases is still good




                                                  78
law. By any definition, the present case is a “notorious case” within the meaning of Estes and

therefore electronic media coverage is banned under the federal constitution.

       This last conclusion is supported by the most recent case in which the Supreme Court has

considered the issue of cameras in the courtroom. See Hollingsworth v. Perry, 558 U.S. 183

(2010) (per curiam). In that case, the Court granted a stay of an order of the United States

District Court for the Northern District of California permitting audio and video broadcast of a

bench trial to a number of federal courthouses around the country. Id. at 184. The order had been

made pursuant to a hastily adopted local rule that enacted without sufficient opportunity for

notice and comment. Id. at 184-85. The Court agreed that the local rule was invalid for this

reason. Id. at 199. The importance of Hollingsworth for the present case lies in the reasons the

Court articulated for why public comment on the rule was important, and the court’s discussion

of cameras in the courtroom.

       According to Hollingsworth, “[t]he question whether courtroom proceedings should be

broadcast has prompted considerable national debate. Reasonable minds differ on the proper

resolution of that debate and on the restrictions, circumstances, and procedures under which such

broadcasts should occur. We do not here express any views on the propriety of broadcasting

court proceedings generally.” Id. at 189. Despite this general comment, the Court went on to

observe that:

              The need for a meaningful comment period was particularly acute in this
         case. Both courts and legislatures have proceeded with appropriate caution in
         addressing this question. In 1996, the Judicial Conference of the United States
         adopted a policy opposing the public broadcast of court proceedings. This
         policy was adopted after a multiyear study of the issue by the Federal Judicial
         Center which drew on data from six district and two appellate courts, as well as
         state-court data. In light of the study’s findings, the Judicial Conference
         concluded that “the intimidating effect of cameras on some witnesses and
         jurors [is] cause for concern.” …



                                                79
             In more than a decade since its adoption the Judicial Conference has
         continued to adhere to its position on the broadcast of court proceedings. While
         the policy conclusions of the Judicial Conference may not be binding on the
         lower courts, they are “at the very least entitled to respectful consideration.”
         …Before abandoning its own policy—one consistent with the Judicial
         Conference’s longstanding views—it was incumbent on the District Court to
         adopt a proposed rule only after notice and an adequate period for public
         comment.

Id. at 193-94 (internal citations omitted). The Court ultimately concluded:

         Applicants also have shown that irreparable harm will likely result from the
         denial of the stay. Without a stay, the District Court will broadcast the trial. It
         would be difficult—if not impossible—to reverse the harm from those
         broadcasts. The trial will involve various witnesses, including members of
         same-sex couples; academics, who apparently will discuss gender issues and
         gender equality, as well as family structures; and those who participated in the
         campaign leading to the adoption of Proposition 8. This Court has recognized
         that witness testimony may be chilled if broadcast. See Estes v. Texas, 381 U.S.
         532, 547, 85 S.Ct. 1628, 14 L.Ed.2d 543 (1965); id., at 591, 85 S.Ct. 1628
         (Harlan, J., concurring). Some of applicants’ witnesses have already said that
         they will not testify if the trial is broadcast, and they have substantiated their
         concerns by citing incidents of past harassment. …These concerns are not
         diminished by the fact that some of applicants’ witnesses are compensated
         expert witnesses. There are qualitative differences between making public
         appearances regarding an issue and having one’s testimony broadcast
         throughout the country. Applicants may not be able to obtain adequate relief
         through an appeal. The trial will have already been broadcast. It is difficult to
         demonstrate or analyze whether a witness would have testified differently if his
         or her testimony had not been broadcast. And witnesses subject to harassment
         as a result of broadcast of their testimony might be less likely to cooperate in
         any future proceedings.

Id. at 195-96 (emphasis added) (additional internal citation omitted).

The Court concluded that a central flaw in the local rule was that “[t]he District Court here

attempted to revise its rules in haste, contrary to federal statutes and the policy of the Judicial

Conference of the United States. It did so to allow broadcasting of this high-profile trial without

any considered standards or guidelines in place.” Id. at 196 (emphasis added).

       The fact that the trial was of a high-profile case was of significance because “If Local

Rule 77-3 had been validly revised, questions would still remain about the District Court’s


                                                  80
decision to allow broadcasting of this particular trial, in which several of the witnesses have

stated concerns for their own security. Even districts that allow trials to be broadcast . . .

recognize that a district judge’s discretion to broadcast a trial is limited, …Id. at 198 (internal

citations omitted) (citing Hamilton v. Accu-Tek, 942 F. Supp. 136, 138 (E.D.N.Y.1996)

(broadcast forbidden unless “there is no interference with the due process, the dignity of litigants,

jurors and witnesses, or with other appropriate aspects of the administration of justice”)). The

Court noted distinguishing factors for allowing broadcast of proceedings: “the basis that those

cases were not high profile,” id. (citing E*Trade Financial Corp. v. Deutsche Bank AG, 582 F.

Supp. 2d 528, 535 (S.D.N.Y. 2008)); “or did not involve witnesses”, id. (citing Marisol A. v.

Giuliani, 929 F. Supp. 660, 661 (S.D.N.Y. 1996); Katzman v. Victoria’s Secret Catalogue, 923

F. Supp. 580, 586-87 (S.D.N.Y. 1996)).

       The Court highlighted one court that “did not allow the broadcasting of its proceedings

because the case ‘involv[ed] very sensitive issues.’” Id. (citing Schoeps v. Museum of Modern

Art, 599 F. Supp. 2d 532, 534 (S.D.N.Y. 2009)). The Court found that like Schoeps, the matter

“involve[d] issues subject to intense debate in our society” and that “[t]he District Court intends

not only to broadcast the attorneys’ arguments but also witness testimony.” Id. And thus, the case

was “not a good one for a pilot program. Even the studies that have been conducted thus far have

not analyzed the effect of broadcasting in high-profile, divisive cases.” Id. (emphasis added).

       Hollingsworth establishes that at least five members of the present Court believe that

Justice Harlan’s concurring opinion in Estes is speaking for “the Court”, and that these same five

members share the view of the Estes majority that televising of a notorious case is improper. In

any event, however one reads Hollingsworth, the constitutional rule established by five members

of the Court in Estes is that the televising of a notorious case is unconstitutional. Again, because



                                                  81
of its nature and the extent of prejudicial pretrial publicity in this case, it must be categorized as a

notorious case within the meaning of Estes, and Estes must be followed. See Bosse, 580 U.S. at 3

(“[I]t is this Court’s prerogative alone to overrule one of its precedents…..Our decisions remain

binding precedent until we see fit to reconsider them, regardless of whether subsequent cases

have raised doubts about their continuing vitality.”(internal quotation and citations omitted)

(Thomas, J., concurring)).

                 3.      Lower Court Decisions Also Support the Constitutional Necessity of
                         Protecting Mr. Robinson’s Due Process Rights by Prohibiting Further
                         Electronic Media Live Streaming OR Photographing

          The only comparable recent cases in America where a President and his supporters have

been so directly involved in attempting to influence a criminal prosecution or in being the source

of negative pretrial publicity are the Trump case discussed above, the Timothy McVeigh and

Terry Nichols cases in Oklahoma, and the 9/11 trial of Zacarias Moussaoui in Virginia. All four

of these cases strongly support granting the present motion on constitutional grounds.

          Initially, in United States v. McVeigh, where, as here, “national media coverage of the

[tragedy] was extremely comprehensive”, “[t]he immediate reactions of the President, the

Attorney General, and an FBI spokesman were broadcast across the nation”, “[t]here were

extensive interviews with injured victims, members of the families of the dead …and residents of

Oklahoma City”, and “[t]he Governor of the State of Oklahoma was a continual presence in the

media coverage”, Judge Matsch considered whether to grant a change of venue under the

standard set forth in Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure Rule 21(a), which is even higher than

that set forth in Sheppard and James.26 918 F. Supp. 1467, 1470-71 (W.D. Okla. 1996). He


26
     Fed. R. Crim. P. 21(a) provides:
                The court upon motion of the defendant shall transfer the proceeding as to
           that defendant to another district whether or not such district is specified in the

                                                   82
concluded based on the review of the publicity summarized above that there is “so great a

prejudice against these two defendants in the State of Oklahoma that they cannot obtain a fair

and impartial trial at any place fixed by law for holding court in that state. The court also finds

and concludes that an appropriate alternative venue is in the District of Colorado.” Id. at 1474.

Of course, such an inter-state transfer is not an option in Mr. Robinson’s case and as discussed

below other traditional options are also unavailable, leaving the termination of electronic media

coverage the one thing the Court can do to lessen the flow of electronic media coverage.

       Notably, the court in McVeigh did not abandon its duty under Sheppard to take strong

remedial measures under some misguided notion that the existing prejudicial publicity was

already so pervasive and widespread that it was futile to take any remedial action. See Sheppard,

384 U.S. at 363. Nor did the court simply adopt the preferences of the numerous victims’

families. Instead, the court indicated that although he was “acutely aware of the wishes of the

victims of the Oklahoma City explosion to attend this trial and that it will be a hardship for those

victims to travel to Denver”, he was obligated to rule upon a proper weighing of constitutional

interests that “[t[he interests of the victims in being able to attend this trial in Oklahoma are

outweighed by the court’s obligation to assure that the trial be conducted with fundamental

fairness and with due regard for all constitutional requirements.” McVeigh, 918 F. Supp. at 1474-

75.

       Subsequently, Judge Matsch prevented electronic coverage of the trial because he was

required to do so under the strict ban on electronic coverage imposed by Federal Rule of




         defendant’s motion if the court is satisfied that there exists in the district where
         the prosecution is pending so great a prejudice against the defendant that the
         defendant cannot obtain a fair and impartial trial at any place fixed by law for
         holding court in that district.

                                                  83
Criminal Procedure 53.27 See United States v. McVeigh, 931 F. Supp. 753, 754 (D. Colo. 1996).

In the midst of continued pervasive publicity after the case was transferred to Colorado, he

continued to discharge his duty under Sheppard, imposing on the attorneys “a blanket bar on out

of court comments because no lesser restriction would adequately protect against a substantial

likelihood of prejudicing the proceedings”, and finding that “[t]he compelling interest is the

protection of jurors from being influenced by any such statements which may break through the

barriers constructed by them under the court’s instructions.” United States v. McVeigh, 964 F.

Supp. 313, 316 (D. Colo. 1997). As indicated below, a variation of that remedy has been used in

this case and has had no effect on the excesses of the electronic media.

          Following the conviction of both McVeigh and Nichols in federal court, and the

imposition of a death verdict on McVeigh but not on Nichols, the State of Oklahoma filed a

capital prosecution against Nichols. See Nichols v. Dist. Ct. of Oklahoma Cnty, 2000 OK 12, ¶ 2,

6 P.3d 506. Numerous media organizations filed a motion asking the trial judge to permit them to

televise the preliminary hearing, trial, and other proceedings in this case. Both the State and the

defense objected to the media’s motion. Id.

          On May 8, 2000, the trial judge issued an order granting the Media the right to broadcast

any and all court proceedings in this matter. Id. The trial judge acknowledged that Canon



27
     Fed. R. Crim. P. 53 provides:
             Except as otherwise provided by a statute or these rules, the court must not
           permit the taking of photographs in the courtroom during judicial proceedings
           or the broadcasting of judicial proceedings from the courtroom.
        As explained in United States v. Al-Marimi, 761 F. Supp. 3d 16, 23 (D.D.C. 2024), “the
only legal challenges to Rule 53 have been filed by criminal defendants or media organizations.
In each of those cases, courts concluded that Rule 53’s ban on broadcasting is both constitutional
and justified given the serious risks broadcasting poses to both the defendant’s and government’s
right to a fair trial.”

                                                  84
3(B)(9)(e)(2) of the Code of Judicial Conduct explicitly prohibited broadcast over the objection

of the defendant. Id. However, he determined the Canon violated the State and Federal

Constitutions and therefore need not control his decision. Id. He found the trial judge or

presiding judge has final authority to order, manage and control the courtroom and that camera

coverage would not adversely affect Petitioner’s right to a fair trial. Id. He made this finding

despite the overwhelming prejudicial publicity concerning Nichols, including that he had been

convicted and sentenced in federal court.

       Ruling on a pre-preliminary hearing for a writ of mandamus to overturn the ruling, the

Court of Criminal Appeals of Oklahoma overturned the trial court’s order on constitutional

grounds. Id. ¶ 10. The Court placed primary reliance on the Estes decision:

             In Estes, the U.S. Supreme Court found the use of television, however,
         “cannot be said to contribute materially to [the] objective of a fair trial.” [381
         U.S. at 544]. “Rather its use amounts to the injection of an irrelevant factor
         into court proceedings. In addition experience teaches that there are numerous
         situations in which it might cause actual unfairness-some so subtle as to defy
         detection by the accused or control by the judge.” [Id. at 544-45]. The potential
         impact of television on the jurors, the possibility the quality of the testimony in
         criminal trials may be impaired, the additional responsibilities the presence of
         television places on the trial judge, and the impact of courtroom television on
         the defendant were among the concerns mentioned by the Court. [Id. at 545-
         549].

             The requirement of a public trial is satisfied by the opportunity of
         members of the public and the press to attend the trial and to report what they
         have observed. [Nixon, Inc., 435 U.S. at 610]. No constitutional provision
         guarantees a right to televise trials. First Amendment rights of the press are
         subject to reasonable time, place, and manner restrictions. [Hastings, 695 F.2d
         at 1282. The federal courts prohibit televising of trials by specific rule. See
         Rule 53, Federal Rules of Criminal Procedure.

              Television is a powerful medium. At its best, it educates and informs. At
         its worst, it has the potential for “distorting the integrity of the judicial process
         bearing on the determination of the guilt or innocence of the accused, and,
         more particularly, for casting doubt on the reliability of the fact-finding
         process carried on under such conditions.” Estes, 381 U.S. at 592[ ] (Harlan J.,
         concurrence). Petitioner’s fundamental right to a fair trial assured by the Due


                                                  85
         Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment takes precedence over the
         interest of the press in televising the proceedings in this case.

Id. ¶¶ 6-8.

        After finding that allowing electronic coverage would also violate the state constitution,

the court concluded that “[i]n order to ensure the solemnity of the judicial proceedings, to

minimize the potential tainting of a possible future jury pool, to curtail outside interference with

these criminal proceedings, and to ensure the reliability of the result ultimately determined in this

case”, the application for a writ was granted and the trial court’s order was vacated. Id. ¶ 10. As

in McVeigh, the court found no significance in the fact that by the time the court issued its ruling,

Nichols’s case had already been exposed to massive nationwide publicity.

        Similarly, in United States v. Moussaoui, Court TV filed a motion to record and telecast

the pretrial and trial proceedings. 205 F.R.D. 183, 184 (E.D. Va. 2002). Stressing the

“extraordinary national and international public interest in this prosecution, which involves

allegations that the defendant was a member of the al Qaeda conspiracy to blow up the World

Trade Center in Manhattan and government buildings in the Washington, D.C. area”, COURT

TV asked the court to “ignore or declare unconstitutional the well-established ban on

photographing and broadcasting federal criminal pretrial and trial proceedings.” Id. The United

States vigorously opposed the constitutional challenge to the ban and raised “compelling

objections to the photographing and broadcasting of these proceedings in any format.” Id.

Defendant opposed any broadcast of pretrial proceedings out of concern that it might taint the

potential jury pool, and he opposed any televised replay of the trial if the jury was not

sequestered; if the jury was sequestered, he supported Court TV’s motion. Id.

        The court’s ruling was three-fold. First, the court ruled that it had no discretion to

disregard the present ban on the photographing and broadcasting of federal criminal proceedings.


                                                  86
Id. Second, the court found that the ban did not violate the constitutional rights of either the

public or the broadcast media. Id. Third, the court ruled that “even if the mandatory ban were

declared unconstitutional, given the issues raised in the indictment, any societal benefits from

photographing and broadcasting these proceedings [were] heavily outweighed by the significant

dangers worldwide broadcasting of this trial would pose to the orderly and secure administration

of justice.” Id. at 184. The court’s decision on the first two issues was in line with all of the court

of appeal rulings on those issues cited above in footnote 17. The court’s ruling on the third issue

addressed many arguments that are likely to be raised by the media in this case.

        The court began its analysis as follows:

              Even if Rule 53 and Local Rule 83.3 were found unconstitutional, this
         Court would deny the intervenors’ motion. The right of access is not absolute.
         See [Globe Newspaper, 457 U.S. at 606]; Westmoreland, 752 F.2d at 19. It
         may be tempered by a defendant’s right to a fair trial, see [Press-Enterprise,
         478 U.S. at 14-15], the need to preserve the secrecy of Grand Jury proceedings,
         see [Branzburg v. Hayes, 408 U.S. 665, 684 (1972)], or concerns about witness
         security, see [Globe Newspaper, 457 U.S. at 608-09]. Limitations on access
         may be necessary to “preserve higher values.” [Press-Enterprise Co., 478 U.S.
         at 9].

             Significant concerns about the security of trial participants and the integrity
         of the fact- finding process justify a ban on photographing and broadcasting
         this trial. In particular, we find that audio or visual broadcasting of any portion
         of these proceedings is likely to intimidate witnesses and jurors, as well as
         threaten the security of the courtroom and all those involved in this trial.

Id. at 186.

        The court next addressed several arguments raided by Court TV. First,

              Intervenors contend that televising this trial will enhance its truth seeking
         aspects. They argue that allowing the public to participate through the lens of
         the television camera will serve as a check on the judicial process. However,
         “it is impossible to believe that the reliability of a trial as a method of finding
         facts and determining guilt or innocence increases in relation to the size of the
         crowd watching it.” [Estes, 381 U.S. at 595] (Harlan, J., concurring). The
         presence of interested spectators, attorneys, jurors and a judge will satisfy the



                                                   87
       safeguards of a public trial and ensure the integrity of these proceedings. See
       id.; see also [Globe Newspaper, 457 U.S. at 606].

Id.

      Second, the court considered the following argument:

           Intervenors stress that the state of audio-visual technology has reached
       such a degree of miniaturization and efficiency as to avoid the tangle of wires
       and obtrusiveness of cameras, microphones and lighting which led the
       Supreme Court to hold that televising the trial so interfered with the orderly
       process of justice that it violated the defendant’s rights to due process and his
       Sixth Amendment right to a fair trial. See [Estes, 381 U.S. at 550-51] We agree
       with the intervenors that broadcast technology has changed dramatically since
       1965 and that many of the specific problems discussed in Estes may no longer
       apply.

            Advances in broadcast technology, however, have also created new threats
       to the integrity of the fact- finding process. The traditional public spectator or
       media representative who attends a federal criminal trial leaves the courtroom
       with his or her memory of the proceedings and any notes he or she may have
       taken. These spectators do not leave with a permanent photograph. However,
       once a witness’ testimony has been televised, the witness’ face has not just
       been publicly observed, it has also become eligible for preservation by VCR or
       DVD recording, digitizing by the new generation of cameras or permanent
       placement on Internet web sites and chat rooms. Today, it is not so much the
       small, discrete cameras or microphones in the courtroom that are likely to
       intimidate witnesses, rather, it is the witness’ knowledge that his or her face or
       voice may be forever publicly known and available to anyone in the world.

           As the United States argues, this intimidation could lead foreign
       prosecution witnesses, outside the jurisdiction of the Court, to refuse to testify
       or withhold their full testimony out of reasonable fears for their personal
       safety. It could similarly lead witnesses favorable to the defense to refrain from
       coming forward for fear of being ostracized. The permanent preservation of
       images of law enforcement witnesses could also jeopardize their future careers
       or personal safety. How could an agent whose face was known throughout the
       world ever be able to work undercover or interview witnesses on the street
       effectively?

           Knowledge that the proceedings were being broadcast may also intimidate
       jurors. Excluding cameras and other recording devices from the courtroom will
       help preserve the anonymity of the jurors who are selected to serve and
       minimize the potential for a “popular verdict.” See [Estes, 381 U.S. at 592]
       (Harlan, J., concurring).



                                              88
              The intervenors, obviously sensitive to these concerns, argue that they
         would agree to mask the faces of all jurors or witnesses who did not wish to be
         photographed. That response is insufficient for two reasons. First, it creates an
         additional management issue for the Court in what is an already complex case.
         As a practical matter, names of trial witnesses in criminal cases are not usually
         exchanged in advance. Therefore, witness requests not to be photographed
         would likely have to be addressed just as the witness is called to testify, and
         directions would have to be given to camera operators not to photograph those
         witnesses. How much time that colloquy might take and the impact on the
         witness is hard to determine, but it would certainly add to any nervousness that
         person might have about testifying and could delay the trial while the camera
         was being adjusted. Second, mistakes can be made. If a witness’ or juror’s face
         were inadvertently televised, the harm cannot be undone. Given that this case
         involves allegations that members of the al Qaeda conspiracy, a world wide
         terrorist organization with cells operating within this country, monitor trial
         proceedings involving its members, any mistake could expose the juror or
         witness to serious risks to their personal safety.

              Even if every witness’ face were obscured, the faces of the attorneys, court
         staff, and security officers would be exposed, subjecting them to the possibility
         of long-term security problems. In addition, publicly broadcast photographic
         images of the set- up of the courtroom, the location of the cell block, the
         number of deputy marshals, where the deputies stand in the courtroom,
         placement of doors and other courtroom details could jeopardize the security of
         these proceedings. Intervenors have offered no solutions to these problems.

Id. at 186-87.

       This aspect of the court’s ruling is especially compelling in this case where there have

already been multiple violations of the court’s decorum order directing the media to use its “best

efforts” to avoid visually recording court security staff, clerks, and judicial assistants, and

counsel for the media has expressed the cavalier attitude with respect to even more serious

violations that “[t]hey’re not perfect; mistakes happen.” Dkt. 256, at 9; see also Dkt. 265.

       Next, the court adopted an argument urged by the United States:

              The United States also argues that any retrial of this already highly
         publicized case would be rendered extremely difficult if the trial proceedings
         have been broadcast. A potential juror who had watched portions of the first
         trial could be tainted. If witnesses experienced any recrimination from
         members of the public for testifying in the first trial, those witnesses may be
         unwilling or hesitant to testify at a retrial.


                                                  89
Id. at 187.

        The Moussaoui Court also ruled that another factor justifying denial of the motion was

that “world- wide broadcasting of these proceedings, either by television, radio or the Internet,

would be an open invitation to any trial participant to engage in showmanship or make a public

spectacle for the world to see or hear.” Id.

        Lastly, the court addressed the intervenor’s argument that the intense national and

international interest in the case, coupled with the needs of the victims, justified allowing Court

TV to televise the proceedings:

             We are very aware of the extensive, legitimate public interest in this case,
         and understand that many thousands of people in this country and throughout
         the world, suffered devastating losses on September 11, 2001. It is
         understandable that they might want to watch this trial. However, contrary to
         what intervenors have argued, the purpose of this trial is not to provide
         catharsis to the victims or to educate the world about the American legal
         system. Instead, the purpose is to determine the innocence or guilt of this
         defendant for the specific crimes charged in the Indictment. It remains a
         bedrock principle of our legal system that the courts have the right “to conduct
         their business in an untrammeled way.” [Wood v. Georgia, 370 U.S. 375, 383
         (1962)]. To ensure that both this defendant and the United States receive a fair
         and orderly trial, none of these proceedings should be photographed or
         broadcast in any form.

             We find that all of these concerns provide compelling reasons for
         prohibiting any photographing or broadcasting of either the pretrial or trial
         proceedings. Accordingly, the intervenors’ Motion to Record and Telecast will
         be denied by an Order issued with this Memorandum Opinion.

Id. at 188.

        To summarize, under Sheppard and its progeny, once it is shown that there is “a

reasonable likelihood that prejudicial electronic media coverage prior to trial will prevent a fair

trial”, the Federal Constitution imposes a duty on a trial court to take “strong measures” to

protect to the fullest extent possible a capital defendant’s Sixth, Eighth, and Fourteenth



                                                 90
Amendment and Utah Constitutional rights to a fair and reliable trial and sentencing hearing by

an impartial jury. That standard has clearly been met in this case. Both the Supreme Court’s

camera-in-the-courtroom cases and lower court decisions support granting the present motion on

constitutional grounds as a necessary remedy to fully protect Mr. Robinson’s state and federal

constitutional rights.

        C.      The Fact That the State and Its Agents Released Extremely Prejudicial
                Information and Opinions to the Media in Violation of Applicable Law and
                Standards Weighs in Favor of Granting This Motion.

                1.       Introduction

        “A very important factor in many of these cases is the fact that the publicity has been

engendered by reports given by government agents. It has been suggested that where government

agents have been responsible for the dispensing of information, thereby giving such information

their imprimatur, a court is more likely to consider [remedial relief].” United States v. Houlihan,

926 F. Supp. 14, 16, n. 3 (D. Mass. 1996) (collecting cases); see also Sheppard, 384 U.S. at 363

(“The courts must take such steps by rule and regulation that will protect their processes from

prejudicial outside interferences. Neither prosecutors, counsel for defense, the accused,

witnesses, court staff nor enforcement officers coming under the jurisdiction of the court should

be permitted to frustrate its function. Collaboration between counsel and the press as to

information affecting the fairness of a criminal trial is not only subject to regulation, but is highly

censurable and worthy of disciplinary measures.”); Bauer, 522 F.2d at 253 (7th Cir. 1975)

(special rules regulating release of information by prosecutors and law enforcement officials are

justified because “[t]hey have the ability to influence and ensure proper governmental procedure

without resort to public opinion” and “they know what charges may be brought and are a prime

source of damaging statements.”); U.S. ex rel. Bloeth v. Denno, 313 F.2d 364, 372 (2d Cir. 1963)

(en banc) (reversing a conviction where the defendant was denied his right to an impartial jury

                                                  91
by prejudicial pretrial publicity, and emphasizing the fact that “much of the prejudicial matter,

particularly that from the files of a state institution, and in part the attack on the insanity defense,

came from the prosecution.”).

        As indicated above, law enforcement and prosecutorial agencies have made numerous

disclosures to the media of highly prejudicial material, in direct violation of controlling statutes,

rules, and ethical guidelines. On September 12, 2025, someone on the joint state and federal law

enforcement team released to the media an Affidavit of Probable Cause that was posted

unredacted on the internet by media outlets, and a substantial portion of the affidavit was then

read to a national audience during Governor Cox’s “We GOT HIM” press conference, all in

violation of Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3), Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d), Utah Code § 63G-2-

305(10), (11), and (18), and Utah Rule of Professional Conduct 3.6 (“Prof’l Rule 3.6”). On the

same date, someone in law enforcement released Mr. Robinson’s mugshot to the media in direct

violation of Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3), Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d), and Utah Code § 63G-2-

305(10), (11), (18) and (80).28 On September 15, 2025, Attorney General Bondi, FBI Director

Patel, and FBI Deputy Director Bongino made statements to the media in blatant violation of 28

C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(6) and Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3). On September 16, 2025, an even more

detailed Statement of Probable Cause was read to and then released to the media in its entirety

during the County Attorney’s September 16, 2025 press conference, and that reading was

                                                   92
accompanied by what was essentially a penalty phase closing argument on victim impact issues,

in violation of Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3), Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d), Utah Code § 63G-2-

305(10), (11), and (18), and Prof’l Rule 3.6. As is further indicated, the sheriff held his own

press conference at which he expressed his opinion about the strength of the evidence against

Mr. Robinson, and commented on his demeanor, which also violated the same Utah Code and

opinion about Mr. Robinson’s guilt and character and discussed the forensic evidence in the case,

which also violated the same provisions.

       As argued below, the documents, photographs, information and opinions disclosed to the

media on these occasions were not properly released, and these disclosures, which reached the

public primarily through the electronic media, have severely impaired Mr. Robinson’s

opportunity to be tried by a fair and impartial jury that can reach reliable conclusions based on

evidence produced and tested in court. To prevent further eroding of that opportunity, the Court

should prohibit any further electronic media live streaming or photography.

       The media will no doubt argue that the “bell cannot be unrung” and the fact that the

prosecution and law enforcement have already polluted the jury pool argues in favor of allowing

the electronic media to continue to do so. But there are at least four compelling answers to this

argument. The first two reasons are explored in U.S. ex rel. Bloeth v. Denno:

         It may be argued that since the trial strategy adopted by [the defense] called for
         emphasis on the number and brutality of Bloeth’s crimes as a basis for the
         defense of insanity, no harm was done in choosing a jury already familiar with
         his otherwise inadmissible history. There are at least two insurmountable
         difficulties with this argument. The publicity, partly sponsored by the
         prosecution, created opinions of guilt long before trial, far removed from any
         safeguards against inadmissible matter, and included inadmissible material
         emanating from the prosecution denigrating the insanity defense and so
         predisposing the prospective jurors to reject it. A defendant is entitled to be



                                                 93
           tried on the evidence and arguments before the jury in open court, under the
           guidance of a judge.

           The opinions formed in this case, moreover, were based not solely on accounts
           of the crime at issue, but on … accounts containing expressions of the
           [opinion] of the District Attorney…, The interest of the public in the
           knowledge of the apprehension of the [suspect]… exists, but cannot justify the
           denial of an impartial jury to the one accused.

313 F.2d at 373.29

          In addition, the “can’t unring the bell” argument fails to account for the cumulative

impact of prejudicial electronic media coverage. As explained in the Declaration of Ruva,

           The volume of coverage in notorious cases can be overwhelming. And studies
           show that the quantity of pre-trial publicity to which jurors are exposed affects
           their level of bias. Across studies extending back several decades, researchers
           have demonstrated that individuals exhibit a greater propensity to pre-judge
           defendants when they are exposed to more publicity—both as a general
           conclusion (i.e., showing a general propensity to prejudgment based on an
           increase in the amount of exposure ), and in response to specific thresholds
           (i.e., showing that prospective jurors exposed to at least three newspaper
           articles about a crime were more likely to believe there was ample evidence
           against a defendant ). This effect has been demonstrated in both naturally
           occurring and controlled settings, confirming the validity of the proposition.

Ex. 2, ¶ 9 (footnotes omitted).

          Lastly, the Supreme Court has expressly ruled in the context of protecting the privacy of

criminal justice records that “the fact that an event is not wholly ‘private’ does not mean that an

individual has no interest in limiting disclosure or dissemination of the information.” U.S. Dep’t

of Just. v. Reporters Committee for Freedom of Press, 489 U.S. 749, 770 (1989) (internal

quotation omitted). See also Prison Legal News v. Exec. Off. for U.S. Att’ys, 628 F.3d 1243,

1249 (10th Cir. 2011) (“That the video and photographs were, at the time of the trials, displayed

publicly, may impact the family’s expectation of privacy in those materials but does not negate



29
     Unlike in Denno, the defense in this case has in no way attempted to try this case in the press.

                                                   94
it…. Reporters Committee thus requires an examination whether, as a practical matter, the extent

of prior public disclosure has eliminated any expectation in privacy.” (citing Reporters

Committee, 489 U.S. at 762-63)). Here, although Mr. Robinson’s fair trial and privacy interests

have been badly damaged, they have not yet been “eliminated”, an outcome that the present

motion is seeking to avoid.

               2.      The Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause,
                       and the Mugshots Should Never Have Been Released to the Media,
                       and the Governor’s, the District Attorney’s, the Sheriff’s, and the
                       Department of Justice’s Press Conferences and Other Media
                       Appearances All Violated Controlling Statutes, Rules and
                       Regulations.

       Numerous statutes, rules, and regulations should have governed the governmental release

to the media of prejudicial documents, photographs, information and opinions. These are:

                       a) Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) and Utah Code § 63G-2-305(80)

       Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) provides that: “The following records are private if

properly classified by a governmental entity:… (d) other records containing data on individuals

the disclosure of which constitutes a clearly unwarranted invasion of personal privacy.”

       The United States Supreme Court has described an identically worded exemption under

the federal Freedom of Information Act (Exemption 7(C)) as reflecting privacy interests in

“avoiding disclosure of personal matters,” Reporters Committee, 489 U.S. at 762; maintaining

“the individual’s control of information concerning his or her person,” id. at 763; avoiding

“disclosure of records containing personal details about private citizens,” id. at 766; and

“keeping personal facts away from the public eye” Id. at 769.

       Embarrassing and humiliating facts—particularly those connecting an individual to

criminality—qualify for these descriptors. See, e.g., id. at 771 (finding a privacy interest in

criminal rap sheets); Detroit Free Press Inc. v. United States Dep’t of Justice, 829 F.3d 478, 480


                                                 95
(6th Cir. 2016) (en banc) (holding that individuals enjoy a non-trivial privacy interest in their

booking photos, for purposes of determining whether to apply the FOIA exemption for

information compiled for law enforcement purposes where the disclosure could constitute an

unwarranted invasion of personal privacy); World Publishing Co. v. U.S. Dep’t of Justice, 672

F.3d 825, 830-32 (10th Cir. 2012) (same); Karantsalis v. U.S. Dep’t of Justice, 635 F.3d 497,

499 (11th Cir. 2011) (per curiam) (same); Rimmer v. Holder, 700 F.3d 246, 257 (6th Cir. 2012)

(the names and identifying information of individuals associated with investigation of a murder);

ACLU v. U.S. Dep’t of Justice, 655 F.3d 1, 8 (D.C. Cir. 2011) ( “there is no real dispute that the

scope of Exemption 7(C) can extend even to convictions and public pleas”); Kiraly v. FBI, 728

F.2d 273, 277 (6th Cir. 1984) (FBI files identifying individuals suspected of criminal activity but

not indicted or tried). See also Deseret News Publishing Co. v. Salt Lake Cnty., 2008 UT 26, ¶ 33

n. 6, 182 P.3d 372 (“Our interpretation of the phrase “clearly unwarranted invasion of privacy”

finds support in the United States Supreme Court’s interpretation of identical language contained

in the federal Freedom of Information Act.”).

       The Utah Supreme Court held in Deseret News Pub. Co that under Utah Code § 63G-2-

302(2)(d), “most investigative reports …could qualify for nonpublic status” but not when (1)

names have been redacted from the report and there is no evidence to show that if the report is

released that anyone will be able to connect the dots and figure out who the people are or (2) the

disclosure of names involves only the person who initiated the complaint or public officials.

2008 UT 26, ¶¶ 34-40. The Court also held that “section 63-2-302(2)(d) necessarily demands an

expansive and searching evaluation of the interests that might make an invasion of personal

privacy warranted.” Id. ¶ 33.




                                                 96
       In this case, the Affidavit of Probable Cause contained Mr. Robinson’s name, date of

birth, his parent’s home address, and the details about the ongoing investigation. Dkt. 2. As

indicated in the statement of facts, the entire unredacted affidavit began appearing on the internet

on the morning of September 12, 2025, shortly after the document was created, so it is highly

unlikely that before release of the document anyone conducted “an expansive and searching

evaluation of the interests that might make an invasion of personal privacy warranted.” Deseret

News Publishing Co., 2008 UT 26, ¶ 33.

       And although the Affidavit used the generic terms “family members” and “roommate”,

by disclosing Mr. Robinson’s name, date of birth, and his parent’s home address the actual

names of the parents and the roommate were easily discoverable. Indeed, by the early morning of

September 12, 2025, the media were at the parent’s home and at Mr. Robinson’s apartment

where they interviewed neighbors, published details about Mr. Robinson’s background, and

broadcast his mug shot. See CBS NEWS, FBI searches childhood home of Charlie Kirk killing

suspect (YouTube, Sep. 12, 2025), https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2QwkFKBBM6M; WSJ

News, What We Know About the Charlie Kirk Shooting Suspect Tyler Robinson (YouTube, Sep.

12, 2025), https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=W5aIkefBZWc. On the same date, electronic

media outlets were posting Matt and Amber’s Robinson’s name and their pictures on the

internet. See, e.g., ONEINDIA NEWS, Charlie Kirk LATEST: Tyler Robinson’s Father Becomes A

National Hero | Shocking Story Behind ARREST

suspect-2128904.

         By September 13, 2025, the New York Post had identified “Lance Twiggs” as Mr.

         The United States Supreme Court has noted that the privacy interest recognized in the

federal counterpart to Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) “must be understood ... in light of the

consequences that would follow” from unlimited disclosure. Nat’l Archives & Records Admin. v.

Favish, 541 U.S. 157, 170 (2004). Here, the predictable harm to

privacy rights caused by the authorities’ release to the media of the Affidavit of Probable Cause

(Dkt.2), and later, the more expansive Statement of Probable Cause (Dkt. 1) are briefly

summarized in the attached Exhibit 3                                       and Exhibit 4




Ex. 3.




         The predictable harm to Tyler Robinson’s privacy and fair trial rights caused by the

authorities’ release to the media of the Affidavit of Probable Cause and the more expansive

Statement of Probable Cause is more extensive, as fully documented in the statement of facts. As

there indicated, release of these documents, which are based on several layers of hearsay that

have never been subject to any adversary testing, led directly to the media repeatedly publishing,

dramatizing, and having their “experts” make derogatory and speculative comment s about such

material, creating in the minds of the jury pool in this case the lasting impression that Mr.

Robinson is both guilty and dangerous enough to require execution.




                                                 99
        Particularly damaging has been the display of Mr. Robinson’s mug shot in virtually every

one of the electronic media stories about this case, which are often juxtaposed against loving

images of Mr. Kirk and his family. As was concluded in Detroit Free Press, in the course of

holding that the disclosure of a mug shot violated to federal counterpart to Utah Code § 63G-2-

302(2)(d), “ a disclosed booking photo casts a long, damaging shadow over the depicted

individual”, and “modern technology only heightens the consequences of disclosure—’in today’s

society the computer can accumulate and store information that would otherwise have surely

been forgotten.’” 829 F.3d at 482 (quoting Reporters Committee, 489 U.S. at 771). The court

elaborated:

         Booking photos—snapped “in the vulnerable and embarrassing moments
         immediately after [an individual is] accused, taken into custody, and deprived
         of most liberties”—fit squarely within this realm of embarrassing and
         humiliating information. Karantsalis, 635 F.3d at 503. More than just “vivid
         symbol[s] of criminal accusation,” booking photos convey guilt to the viewer.
         Id. (emphasis in original). Indeed, viewers so uniformly associate booking
         photos with guilt and criminality that we strongly disfavor showing such
         photos to criminal juries. See United States v. Irorere, 69 Fed.Appx. 231, 235
         (6th Cir. 2003) (“[T]he Sixth Circuit has condemned the practice of showing
         ‘mug shot’ evidence to a jury ‘as effectively eliminating the presumption of
         innocence and replacing it with an unmistakable badge of criminality.’ “
         [further citation omitted]); see also United States v. McCoy, 848 F.2d 743,
         745-46 (6th Cir. 1988) (finding the district court erred in overruling an
         objection to lineup photos, which “suggest that [the defendant] is a ‘bad guy’
         who belongs in jail”). This alone establishes a non-trivial privacy interest in
         booking photos.

Id. at 482.

        In light of the predictable damaging consequences that flowed from the release of the

Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the mug shots, it should be

concluded that a violation of Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) took place when these materials were

released because whatever interest the press or public had in the release of such material, that




                                                100
interest was far outweighed by the privacy and fair trial interests put at serious risk by the

disclosure.

       Moreover, with regard to the mugshot images, Utah Code § 63G-2-305(80) specifically

provides:

       The following records are protected if properly classified by a governmental entity:…

                (80) an image taken of an individual during the process of booking the
         individual into jail, unless:

         (a) the individual is convicted of a criminal offense based upon the conduct for
         which the individual was incarcerated at the time the image was taken;

         (b) a law enforcement agency releases or disseminates the image:

                 (i) after determining that the individual is a fugitive or an imminent
                 threat to an individual or to public safety and releasing or
                 disseminating the image will assist in apprehending the individual or
                 reducing or eliminating the threat; or

                 (ii) to a potential witness or other individual with direct knowledge of
                 events relevant to a criminal investigation or criminal proceeding for
                 the purpose of identifying or locating an individual in connection with
                 the criminal investigation or criminal proceeding;

         (c) a judge orders the release or dissemination of the image based on a finding
         that the release or dissemination is in furtherance of a legitimate law
         enforcement interest; or

         (d) the image is displayed to a person who is permitted to view the image
         under Section 17-22-30

       Here, Mr. Robinson was already in custody when his mug shots were released and none

of the other exceptions apply. Release of the mug shots thus violated both Utah Code § 63G-2-

302(2)(d) and Utah Code § 63G-2-305(80).

               b) Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10) and Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3)

       Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10) provides:



                                                 101
              The following records are protected if properly classified by a
         governmental entity:
                …
                  (10) records created or maintained for civil, criminal, or administrative
         enforcement purposes or audit purposes, or for discipline, licensing,
         certification, or registration purposes, if release of the records:

         (a) reasonably could be expected to interfere with investigations undertaken for
         enforcement, discipline, licensing, certification, or registration purposes;

         (b) reasonably could be expected to interfere with audits, disciplinary, or
         enforcement proceedings;

         (c) would create a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial or
         impartial hearing;

         (d) reasonably could be expected to disclose the identity of a source who is not
         generally known outside of government and, in the case of a record compiled
         in the course of an investigation, disclose information furnished by a source
         not generally known outside of government if disclosure would compromise
         the source; or

         (e) reasonably could be expected to disclose investigative or audit techniques,
         procedures, policies, or orders not generally known outside of government if
         disclosure would interfere with enforcement or audit efforts.

       With regard to Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(a), the Utah Supreme Court held in Deseret

News Publishing, that disclosure of a “report of an ongoing investigation ‘reasonably could be

expected to interfere with investigations undertaken for enforcement, discipline, licensing,

certification, or registration purposes’”. 2008 UT 26, ¶ 18. The Court further held that “the

County was required to conduct a conscientious and neutral evaluation of the report’s GRAMA

status without regard to existing designations or classifications.” Id. ¶ 24.

       In this case, as indicated above, the speed with which the disclosures were made after

creation of the documents and images suggests strongly that prior to the disclosure, no

government official conducted a “a conscientious and neutral evaluation of the report’s GRAMA

status without regard to existing designations or classifications.” Id. Instead, the September 15,



                                                 102
2025, media appearances by FBI Director Patel and Deputy Director Bongino cited above

indicate that material was being released pursuant to their blanket policy that it was in the

“public interest” to do so, without any regard for weighing the constitutional and statutory

interests of Mr. Robinson, his family, or Luna. That is exactly what Deseret News Publishing

prohibits. And unlike in Deseret News Publishing, where the investigation was not ongoing

when the disclosures were made, here it clearly was. See, e.g., Kash Patel, Director Patel’s

Remarks at the September 12, 2025, Press Conference on the Utah Valley University Shooting,

FBI, https://www.fbi.gov/news/speeches-and-testimony/director-patel-remarks-september-12-

press-conference-utah-valley-university-shooting (“This is very much an ongoing investigation,
