as the governor said, and we will continue to work with state and local authorities to develop the

investigation, to provide them the evidence they need for their ongoing prosecutions. And we

will be here to answer every call they absolutely have as long as it takes, for as long as we need

to find and apprehend whatever suspects were involved in this crime.”).

       Finally, this is not a case like Deseret News Publishing where competing interests fought

to a draw, requiring that the documents be disclosed. See Deseret News Publishing, 2008 UT 26,

¶ 24 (GRAMA “mandate that when competing interests fight to a draw, disclosure wins.”).

Instead, it is one involving the constitutional and statutory right to privacy of Mr. Robinson, his

parents, and Luna, and Mr. Robinson’s fundamental constitutional right to a fair, impartial, and

reliable guilt and penalty trial. For reasons discussed in the Introduction, Mr. Robinson’s

fundamental constitutional right to a fair and impartial trial, in combination with the privacy

rights and interests at stake, must prevail over any competing interest. The disclosures in this

case therefore violated Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(a).




                                                103
       It must also be stressed that the disclosures also violated Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c)

and (10)(d). Although no Utah appellate court has construed these provisions, it is clear that the

disclosure of Mr. Robinson’s name and birthdate, of his parent’s address, and of detailed

accounts of statements made to law enforcement by the parents and by Luna, “reasonably could

be expected to disclose the identity of a source who is not generally known outside of

government and, in the case of a record compiled in the course of an investigation, disclose

information furnished by a source not generally known outside of government if disclosure

would compromise the source.” Id. Indeed, the media had discovered the identities of these

sources shortly after the disclosures of the Affidavit of Probable Cause on September 12, 2025.

       Lastly, and most importantly, Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c), which creates a broad

category requiring non-disclosure of any record if its release “would create a danger of depriving

a person of a right to a fair trial or impartial hearing” seems to contemplate that the Court will

utilize the categories of information set forth in Comment 5 to Prof’l Rule 3.6 to determine what

information in a record would create such a danger. See supra, n. 4.

       This interpretation is bolstered by Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3), which specifically

references “court rule(s)” and “federal regulation[s]”, stating:

                 The following records are not public:

         (a) a record that is private, controlled, or protected under Sections 63G-2-302,
         63G-2-303, 63G-2-304, and 63G-2-305; and

         (b) a record to which access is restricted pursuant to court rule, another state
         statute, federal statute, or federal regulation, including records for which access
         is governed or restricted as a condition of participation in a state or federal
         program or for receiving state or federal funds.

       This provision incorporates into the structure of Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3) rules like

Prof’l Rule 3.6 and federal regulations like 28 C.F.R. § 50.2 that if a record is protected under



                                                104
either Prof’l Rule 3.6 or 28 C.F.R. § 50.2, it becomes a non-public record by virtue of Utah Code

§ 63G-2-201(3). Thus, both Prof’l Rule 3.6 and 28 C.F.R. § 50.2 must be taken into account in

conducting a comprehensive GRAMA analysis.

       As just one example, Prof’l Rule 3.6 (b)(2) permits a lawyer to state “information

contained in a public record”, however, 28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(3) contains no such exception. This

means that a federal official who attends a press conference in Utah and reads the contents of an

affidavit of probable cause to the media which covers subjects covered in Comment 5 of Prof’l

Rule 3.6 may, for the reasons stated below, be violating Prof’l Rule 3.6, but he will most

certainly be violating 28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(6) and therefore will also be violating Utah Code §

63G-2-201(3).

       As discussed above in footnote four and further below, Comment 5 to Prof’l Rule 3.6

specifies certain categories of information that if released are deemed to be “more likely than not

to have a material prejudicial effect on a proceeding.” Comment 5 has been interpreted as an

essential part of Prof’l Rule 3.6. See Leavitt, 2025 UT 46, ¶ 31 (concluding under Prof’l Rule 3.6

that there was substantial evidence in the record that the former Utah District Attorney knew or

reasonably should have known that his statements had a substantial likelihood of materially

prejudicing the proceedings because “[t]hese …statements are of the type specifically identified

in comment 5 to Prof’l Rule 3.6 as ‘more likely than not to have a material prejudicial effect on a

proceeding.’”). In light of Leavitt, it makes sense for the Court to take into account the categories

of information set forth in Comment 5 in determining whether release of the Affidavit of

Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the mugshots created “a danger of

depriving a person of a right to a fair trial or impartial hearing.” See Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10).




                                                105
        At the same time, the Court should recognize that Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c) is more

protective of a criminal defendant’s right to a fair trial than is Prof’l Rule 3.6. For instance, Prof’l

Rule 3.6(a) states:

         A lawyer who is participating or has participated in the investigation or
         litigation of a matter shall not make an extrajudicial statement that the lawyer
         knows or reasonably should know will be disseminated by means of public
         communication and will have a substantial likelihood of materially prejudicing
         an adjudicative proceeding in the matter.

Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c) creates a more protective standard by providing that release of a

record is prohibited even if the release would only “create a danger of depriving a person of a

right to a fair trial or impartial hearing.”

        Similarly, Prof’l Rule 3.6 (b) provides in part that “[n]otwithstanding paragraph (a), a

lawyer may state: (1) the claim, offense or defense involved and, except when prohibited by law,

the identity of the persons involved; (2) information contained in a public record[.]” However,

Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c) contains no such exceptions.

        In this case, the release of the Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable

Cause, and the mugshots clearly created “a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial

or impartial hearing.” Both the Affidavit and the Statement contained information that allowed

the media to readily determine “the identity of a witness” (Prof’l Rule 3.6, Comment 5(i)), and

both contained commentary on: “the character…of a…suspect” (id. 5(i)); “the expected

testimony of a witness” (id.); “the existence [and] contents of any confession, admission or

statement given by a…suspect” (id. 5(ii)); the “performance (and) results of any examination or

test” and “the identity [and] nature of physical evidence expected to be presented” (id.

5(iii));”opinion[s] as to the guilt…of a …suspect” (id. 5(iv)); and “information that the lawyer




                                                  106
knows or reasonably should know is likely to be inadmissible as evidence in a trial, and that

would, if disclosed, create a substantial risk of prejudicing an impartial trial.” (id. 5(v)).

        In regard to the release of the mugshots, there is no doubt that the release of these images

“create[d] a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial or impartial hearing” (Utah

Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c)), because these images have long been recognized by courts as

inherently prejudicial and thus any prosecutor knew or should have known that their release to

and widespread dissemination by the media would have “a substantial likelihood of materially

prejudicing an adjudicative proceeding in the matter.” Prof’l Rule 3.6 (a). and would be

“information that the lawyer knows or reasonably should know is likely to be inadmissible as

evidence in a trial, and that would, if disclosed, create a substantial risk of prejudicing an

impartial trial.” Comment 5(v).

        The cases on this point are numerous and in agreement. See, e.g., Eberhardt v.

Bordenkircher, 605 F.2d 275, 280 (6th Cir. 1979) (reversing a state court conviction on Sixth

and Fourteenth Amendment grounds, in part because the admission into evidence of mugshots

was a “clea[r] erro[r] which touch[es] on fundamental rights and affect the overall fairness of the

trial Eberhardt received…. The use of mug shots has been strongly condemned in federal trials,

as effectively eliminating the presumption of innocence and replacing it with an unmistakable

badge of criminality.”(collecting cases)). See also World Publishing, 672 F.3d at 828-29 (“A

booking photograph is a vivid symbol of criminal accusation, which, when released to the public,

intimates, and is often equated with, guilt. Further, a booking photograph captures the subject in

the vulnerable and embarrassing moments immediately after being accused, taken into custody,

and deprived of most liberties.” (quotation omitted)); United States v. Romero-Rojo, 67 F. App’x

570, 572 (10th Cir. 2003) (unpublished) (holding that the admission of a booking photo at trial



                                                  107
can be unduly prejudicial under Fed. R. Evid. 404(b) because “mugshots carry a clear

implication of criminal activity that breaches the rule against admitting evidence of the

defendant’s bad character or previous brushes with the law.”).

        In sum, release of the Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and

the mugshots was prohibited by Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10) and § 63G-2-201(3).

                        c) Utah Code § 63G-2-305(11)

        Utah Code § 63G-2-305(11) provides, “The following records are protected if properly

classified by a governmental entity … (11) records the disclosure of which would jeopardize the

life or safety of an individual.”

        There is no caselaw on this provision. However, in the unique context of this case, the

release of the Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the mugshots

jeopardized the life or safety of Mr. Robinson, his parents, and Luna.

        To reiterate the clear and present danger of violence which has existed since the

inception of this case, on September 10, 2025, President Trump appeared on national television

and announced that “[R]adical left political violence has hurt too many innocent people and

taken too many lives” and that “the monster who attacked [Kirk] was attacking our whole

country.” See supra, FOX NEWS, BREAKING: Trump releases Oval Office statement.

        The next day, in a statement to reporters at the White House, Mr. Trump was asked what

his message was to “conservative influencers, podcasters, TV hosts that … might be targeted by

some of these radical groups” and he answered, “[w]e have radical left lunatics out there, and we

just have to beat the hell out of them.” See supra, Donald J. Trump (2nd Term), Remarks. This

call-to-arms was widely reported in the media. See supra, D. Sanger. .As indicated below, from

that point forward, things went downhill even further and the political and violent rhetoric

dramatically escalated.

                                               108
       It is demonstrated above that the release of the Affidavit of Probable Cause and the mug

shot on September 12, 2025 allowed the electronic media to quickly identify Mr. Robinson, his

parents, and Luna and to use the hearsay statements in the affidavit to create a narrative strongly

implicating Mr. Robinson. The release of the Statement of Probable Cause on September 16,

2025, for no apparent reason other than advocacy since the magistrate had already found

“substantial evidence” to support the charges (Ex. 1), added further fuel to the fire of

incrimination that the electronic media had already set ablaze. The predictable result was even

more violent rhetoric from the President and his supporters. And as we know from the extensive

record built in United States v. Trump, “[t]he [r]ecord …shows that former President Trump’s

words have real-world consequences. Many of those on the receiving end of his attacks …have

been subjected to a torrent of threats and intimidation from his supporters.” 88 F.4th at 1011; see

also supra at pp. 62-66 (full summary of the record in Trump).

       In the volatile circumstances of the present case, the Affidavit of Probable Cause, the

Statement of Probable Cause, and the mugshots should have been withheld from the media under

Utah Code § 63G-2-305(11).

                       d) Utah Code § 63G-2-305(18)

       Utah Code § 63G-2-305(18) provides, “The following records are protected if properly

classified by a governmental entity…(18) records prepared for or by an attorney, consultant,

surety, indemnitor, insurer, employee, or agent of a governmental entity for, or in anticipation of,

litigation or a judicial, quasi-judicial, or administrative proceeding.”

       Only one Utah case has considered this provision, Utah Legal Clinic v. Salt Lake City

Corp., 2019 UT App 58, 440 P.3d 948. Challenging the denial of disclosure of records related to

the criminal prosecution of one of ULC’s clients, the Utah Court of Appeals ultimately held that

the public interest advanced by a legal clinic was weak, and that the city’s interest in

                                                 109
nondisclosure of city prosecutor records outweighed any public interest in access to the records..

Id. ¶¶ 21-22. The court also clarified that “when government records are properly classified into

a category of record that is designated by statute as “protected,” the records ‘may be ordered to

be disclosed ... only if the person or party seeking disclosure of the record has established, by a

preponderance of the evidence, that the public interest favoring access is equal to or greater than

the interest favoring restriction of access[.]’” Id. ¶ 15 (quoting § 63G-2-305(17)-(18) and Utah

Code § 63G-2-406(1)) The court did not elaborate on the meaning of Utah Code § 63G-2-

305(18), but its citation to Utah Code § 63G-2-305(17), which protects from disclosure “records

that are subject to the attorney client privilege” is helpful in clarifying that Utah Code § 63G-2-

305(18) is not aimed at attorney work-product.

       On its face, Utah Code § 63G-2-305(18) is broad enough to shield from disclosure the

Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the mugshots.

                       e) Utah Rule of Professional Conduct 3.6

       Prof’l Rule 3.6(a) states the basic prohibition on the release of information by lawyers

relating to criminal and civil proceedings. That provision provides:

         (a) A lawyer who is participating or has participated in the investigation or
         litigation of a matter shall not make an extrajudicial statement that the lawyer
         knows or reasonably should know will be disseminated by means of public
         communication and will have a substantial likelihood of materially prejudicing
         an adjudicative proceeding in the matter.

       As indicated above, Comment 5 to Prof’l Rule 3.6 lists certain subjects that are “more

likely than not to have a material prejudicial effect on a proceeding, particularly when they refer

to a …a criminal matter or any other proceeding that could result in incarceration.” The listed

subjects relevant to an analysis of the Affidavit of Probable Cause and the Statement of Probable

Cause are set forth above. Those are the same subjects that are relevant to the analysis of the

statements made by the Governor and the County Attorney with one exception. During his three

                                                 110
nationally televised media appearances on September 14, 2025, the Governor asserted that Mr.

Robinson was not cooperating with law enforcement. Comment 5(ii) prohibits comment on “the

existence or contents of any confession, admission or statement given by a defendant or suspect

or that person’s refusal or failure to make a statement.” The Governor’s statements about Mr.

Robinson’s alleged lack of cooperation with law enforcement (he did in fact turn himself in)

clearly violated this prohibition because the Governor is a lawyer who in all of his media

appearances has represented himself as a member of the investigative and prosecutorial team.

       Similarly, the Utah County Sheriff, acting as an agent of the prosecution, held his own

press conference on September 16, 2025 at which he expressed his opinion about the strength of

the evidence against Mr. Robinson, and commented on his demeanor, which also violated Prof’l

Rule 3.6 (a) and Comment 5.

       One of the remaining issues to be addressed is whether the statements made by the

County Attorney at his press conference on September 16, 2025, and the other statements made

by the Governor in his “WE GOT HIM” press conference on September 12, 2025 and in his later

media appearances also violated Prof’l Rule 3.6(a) and Comment 5.

       It will be recalled that during his “WE GOT HIM” press conference, the Governor read

verbatim most of the content of the Affidavit of Probable Cause. Similarly, at his September 16,

2025 press conference the County Attorney read verbatim the entirety of the Statement of

Probable Cause attached to the Information, although he also went off-script to deliver what was

essentially a penalty phase closing argument on the subject of victim impact. Similarly, in his

three November 14, 2025 national media appearances, the Governor went off-script in the sense

that he did not appear to be reading from either the Affidavit of Probable cause or the Statement




                                               111
of Probable Cause, and he discussed several topics that were nowhere mentioned in the Affidavit

or the Statement, such as:

   •   “WE GOT HIM”

   •   “We have additional forensic evidence that has been processed. We’ll be sharing that
       when charges are filed on Tuesday. So everything we know confirms that this is the
       person.”

   •   “the suspect has not been cooperating so far.”

   •   “We’re getting all this information from family members, again people around the
       suspect, and then forensic evidence that we have which confirms everything and more
       than what we were able to share in that initial press conference.”

   •   That there had been a “radicalization that happened in a very short period of time”

   •   “Clearly there was a lot of gaming going on. Friends that have confirmed that there was
       kind of that deep dark internet, the Reddick culture and these other dark places of the
       internet where this person was going deep. You saw that on the casings

   •   “there clearly was a leftist ideology with this assassin”

   •   he was “deeply indoctrinated with leftist ideology”

   •   “all we can confirm is that those conversations definitely were happening, and they did
       not believe it was actually him. It was all joking until he admitted that it actually was
       him.”

   •   “we’re collecting much more, including forensic evidence.”

   •   “This is a direct assault on America, a direct assault against every single one of us.”

See supra, Stelloh, et al., Suspect in Charlie Kirk’s killing.

       Prof’l Rule 3.6 (b) provides in part: “[n]otwithstanding paragraph (a), a lawyer may state:

… (2) information contained in a public record[.]” Unfortunately, there are no Utah cases

interpreting this provision. The fundamental issue raised with respect to this provision is whether

the state may draft an affidavit or statement containing all of the subjects that are prohibited by

Comment 5, file it with the Court, and then read it verbatim to the media during a press

conference. Mr. Robinson urges that such an interpretation of this exception would swallow the


                                                 112
rule.30 Such an interpretation would also conflict with the Utah Supreme Court’s holding in

Deseret News Publishing, that the holder of a record is “ required to conduct a conscientious and

neutral evaluation of the report’s GRAMA status without regard to existing designations or

classifications.” 2008 UT 26, ¶ 24.

        As noted previously, Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c), requires the record holder to assess

whether release of a record “would create a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial

or impartial hearing.” In this case, as demonstrated above, when that standard is applied to the

Affidavit of Probable Cause and the Statement of Probable Cause in light of Comment 5, it is

clear that these documents should never have been released. A prosecutor in discharge of his

duty under Deseret News Publishing would have to come to the same conclusion with respect to

these or similar documents containing Comment 5 violations, which means that a prosecutor

would never be in a position to adopt a strategy of loading up a document that violated the

principles of Comment 5 and thereafter use the filing of it to excuse reading it verbatim at a press

conference. In other words, under Deseret News Publishing, a prosecutor has a duty to properly

classify a document and to screen out objectionable material under Utah Code § 63G-2-

305(10)(c) and other provisions of GRAMA such as Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) and Utah

Code § 63G-2-305(10)(a) before a document is filed with a court as a public record.

       A similar duty is imposed by the American Bar Association’s Fair Trial and Public

Discourse Standards (Conduct by Lawyers Participating in a Criminal Matter), available at


30
  See Att’y Grievance Comm’n of Maryland v. Gansler, 835 A.2d 548, 569 (Md. App. Ct. 2003)
(“by strictly limiting what is considered a public record, this definition enables all of the
components of MRPC 3.6 to filter objectionable publicity, preventing the ‘public record’
exception from swallowing the general rule of restricting prejudicial speech.”). Gansler is cited
several times with approval in Leavitt, 2025 UT 46, ¶ 60, as is In re Brizzi, 962 N.E.2d 1240,
1247 (Ind. 2012) (per curiam), discussed below.



                                                113
https://www.americanbar.org/groups/criminal_justice/resources/standards/fair-trial-public-

discourse/. Standard 8-2.1(c) provides:

         This Standard should not be construed as prohibiting a lawyer participating in
         a criminal matter from releasing or authorizing the release of a record or
         document that the lawyer is required to release under state open records laws
         or the federal Freedom of Information Act, upon receipt of a proper request. A
         lawyer participating in a criminal matter should not place statements or
         evidence into the court record or into a document that is deemed an open
         record under applicable law solely for the purpose of circumventing this
         Standard. If the lawyer has reason to believe that the public release of a record
         or document would create a substantial probability of harm to the fairness of a
         trial or other overriding interest, the lawyer should consider seeking a sealing
         order[.].

        The Commentary to this Standard provides in part:

              [M]ost open records laws contain exemptions for material that would be
         prejudicial to an ongoing enforcement proceeding, to the fairness of a trial, or
         that would constitute an unwarranted intrusion on personal privacy. The
         purpose of this paragraph is to remind lawyers that they must be familiar with
         the open records laws in their jurisdiction, including the exceptions pertinent to
         these Standards. If the lawyer believes that all or part of a document falls
         within such an exception, the lawyer should withhold such material if its
         release would violate these standards. In such a situation, the lawyer should
         promptly provide notice to the party requesting the information so that the
         lawyer’s decision can be subject to judicial review.

             On the other side of the coin, lawyers should not insert information into a
         document that is deemed an open record solely to circumvent these Standards.
         Nor should lawyers make a statement during a judicial proceeding, or include
         information in a public court filing, solely to circumvent these Standards. If a
         lawyer is aware that certain information contained in a court filing, or that will
         be adduced at a hearing, poses a significant danger to the fairness of the trial or
         other overriding interest that would warrant sealing, the lawyer should consider
         applying for a sealing order for that filing or proceeding, in whole or in part.

Id.at 37.31




31
  The commentary is available at chrome-
extension://efaidnbmnnnibpcajpcglclefindmkaj/https://www.americanbar.org/content/dam/aba/p
ublications/criminal_justice_standards/fair_trial_commentaries.pdf/.

                                                114
        Public Discourse 8-2.1 Standard and the Commentary are consistent with the duty

already imposed on Utah prosecutors by Deseret News Publishing. Where that duty was not

discharged in Mr. Robinson’s case, neither the Governor nor the County Attorney may take

refuge in Prof’l Rule 3.6(b)(2) as an excuse or justification for violating Prof’l Rule 3.6(a).

        Nor does Prof’l Rule 3.6 (b)(2) apply to any statements that are not contained in a filed

record, such as the County Attorney’s penalty phase closing argument about victim impact, or

the statements of the Governor quoted above. See Gansler, 835 A.2d at 569 (“To receive the

protection of the ‘public record’ safe harbor, the lawyer must not provide information beyond

quotations from or references to public government records…. The “public record” safe harbor,

whether construed narrowly or broadly, could not apply possibly to any statement that introduced

information to the public for the first time.”); In re Brizzi, 962 N.E.2d at 1247 (“Gansler

held…and we concur, that to receive the protection of the public record safe harbor, a lawyer

may not provide information beyond quotations from or references to the contents of the public

record Moreover, we hold that a prosecutor must make clear that what is being disclosed is, in

fact, the contents of the probable cause affidavit or other identified public document so the

statements cannot be misunderstood to be the prosecutor’s own opinion about the evidence or the

suspect’s guilt.”).

        The Governor’s and the County Attorney’s off-script statements, combined with the

statement in the Affidavit of Probable Cause that was released to the media in which Attorney

General investigator Brian Davis stated his personal opinion that “I believe there is probable

cause that Tyler Robinson committed the crimes of aggravated murder by shooting Charlie Kirk

in a circumstance that put many around him at grave risk of death, felony discharge of a firearm,

and obstruction of justice for moving and hiding the rifle believed to be used in



                                                 115
the shooting” (Dkt. 2), and combined further with the Statement of Probable Cause (Dkt. 1)

which was released to the media and is still on the County Attorney’s website in which Mr. Gray

gives his person opinion that “[b]ased upon evidence received from Brian Davis of the Utah

State Bureau of Investigations, I have reason to believe the defendant committed the offense(s)

as charged herein”, have severely impaired Mr. Robinson’s constitutional right to be tried by a

fair and impartial jury. See Leavitt, 2025 UT 46, ¶ 39 (“Leavitt’s position as the County Attorney

made the potential for prejudice even higher. His statements likely carried more weight in the

eyes of the public.” (citing Gansler, 835 A.2d at 559 (“Comments by prosecuting attorneys, in

particular, have the inherent authority of the government and are more likely to influence the

public.”); In re Brizzi, 962 N.E.2d at 1246 (“A prosecutor’s opinion of guilt is particularly likely

to create prejudice, given that his or her words carry the authority of the government and are

especially persuasive in the public’s eye.”)).

       This reasoning applies with even greater force to statements by Presidents and

Governors. And because of the prestige of all of these individuals, the prejudice is highly

unlikely to diminish even after a lengthy period of time. So, we conclude that the time between

these statements and the trial date did not neutralize the substantial likelihood that they would

materially prejudice the trial. Moreover, these individuals are all highly invested in this case and

will no doubt be offering more of the same right up to the trial. See id. ¶ 40 (“Leavitt contends

that the potential for prejudice was lessened because Baum’s trial did not take place until much

later. We agree that the statements’ proximity to trial is relevant. However, it is not

dispositive…. And we conclude that the time between these statements and the trial date did not

neutralize the substantial likelihood that they would materially prejudice the trial.”).




                                                 116
          Prof’l Rule 3.6(a) was repeatedly violated in this case and Prof’l Rule 3.6(b)(2) does not

provide an escape hatch from these violations.

                         f) 28 C.F.R. § 50.2

          28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(1) and (2) set forth the basic ground rules applicable to the release of

information by personnel of the Department of Justice relating to criminal proceedings. Those

sections provide:

          Guidelines to criminal actions.

           (1) These guidelines shall apply to the release of information to news media
           from the time a person is the subject of a criminal investigation until any
           proceeding resulting from such an investigation has been terminated by trial or
           otherwise.

           (2) At no time shall personnel of the Department of Justice furnish any
           statement or information for the purpose of influencing the outcome of a
           defendant’s trial, nor shall personnel of the Department furnish any statement
           or information, which could reasonably be expected to be disseminated by
           means of public communication, if such a statement or information may
           reasonably be expected to influence the outcome of a pending or future trial.32

28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(3), like Prof’l Rule 3.6(b) contains exceptions to this general rule, but unlike

Prof’l Rule 3.6 (b) it does not contain a public record exception.

          28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(6) is comparable to Comment 5 and states as follows:

           The release of certain types of information generally tends to create dangers of
           prejudice without serving a significant law enforcement function. Therefore,
           personnel of the Department should refrain from making available the
           following:

           (i) Observations about a defendant’s character.

           (ii) Statements, admissions, confessions, or alibis attributable to a defendant, or
           the refusal or failure of the accused to make a statement.




32
     Available at: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-28/chapter-I/part-50/section-50.2.

                                                  117
         (iii) Reference to investigative procedures such as fingerprints, polygraph
         examinations, ballistic tests, or laboratory tests, or to the refusal by the
         defendant to submit to such tests or examinations.

         (iv) Statements concerning the identity, testimony, or credibility of prospective
         witnesses.

         (v) Statements concerning evidence or argument in the case, whether or not it
         is anticipated that such evidence or argument will be used at trial.

         (vi) Any opinion as to the accused’s guilt, or the possibility of a plea of guilty
         to the offense charged, or the possibility of a plea to a lesser offense.

The statements made to the electronic media by FBI Director Patel, Deputy Director Bongino,

and Attorney General Bondi are discussed above at pp. 15-17. It is clear without further

discussion that all three of these officials violated 28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(6). Moreover, by virtue of

Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3), these individuals also violated state law because that provision

provides that “[t]he following records are not public:…a record to which access is restricted

pursuant to …federal regulation.”

                       g) Conclusion

       In sum, the multiple violations discussed above underscore the need to take strong

remedial action to prevent the further erosion of Mr. Robinson’s right to trial by a fair and

impartial jury. Mr. Robinson next turns to demonstrating that because the traditional means of

controlling prejudicial pretrial publicity are not available or have not been or will not be effective

in this particular case, prohibiting further electronic media live streaming and in-court

photography is the only available remedy available to the Court.

       D.      The Traditional Means of Controlling Prejudicial Pretrial Publicity Are Not
               Available or Have Not Been or Will Not Be Effective in This Case

       A good and fairly recent summary of the remedies traditionally available to control

prejudicial pretrial publicity is provided in the ABA Public Discourse Standard 8-5.3 entitled

“Mitigating the Effects of Publicity on the Fairness of a Trial.” Standard 8-5.3 provides:

                                                 118
             If a case has been the subject of significant publicity, the court should
         consider the following options, to the extent available under applicable law in
         the jurisdiction and subject to the standards elaborated below, as means of
         mitigating the prejudicial effects of such publicity. The court should select the
         most effective option or options in light of the circumstances presented that
         will be the least disruptive to the proceedings and to jurors. The options
         include:

             (a) ordering a continuance;

             (b) conducting voir dire as to pretrial publicity;

             (c) providing clear cautionary instructions to the jury from the outset of
             jury selection;

             (d) providing clear cautionary instructions to court personnel, parties,
             lawyers, and witnesses;

             (e) providing lawyers with additional peremptory challenges;

             (f) impaneling additional alternate jurors;

             (g) importing jurors from another district or locality;

             (h) ordering a severance;

             (i) impaneling an anonymous jury;

             (j) sequestering the jury; and

             (k) ordering a change of venue.

       One of these options (providing clear cautionary instructions to court personnel, parties,

lawyers, and witnesses) has been tried in this case in the form of Dkt. 23, the Court’s Protection

Order re: Pre-Trial and Trial Publicity (“Pretrial Publicity Order”) issued September 16, 2025.

Unfortunately, the order came too late to prevent the initial prejudicial pretrial publicity which

stemmed directly from the decisions by prosecutors and state law enforcement to issue detailed

statements to the media and to release the Affidavit of Probable Cause (Dkt. 2) and the Statement

of Probable Cause (Dkt. 1). This remedy has also been ineffective in stemming the flow of



                                                 119
subsequent prejudicial pretrial publicity by officials and others seeing themselves as not bound

by the Pretrial Publicity Order and by such publicity stemming from the reporting of court

proceedings by the electronic and internet media.

       Two of these options (impaneling additional alternate jurors and sequestering the jury)

are designed to protect against prejudicial publicity occurring during trial and as noted in the

commentary to this Standard “sequestering the jury is considered very burdensome and today is

rarely used except in the most highly publicized case because of the fiscal cost and the

resentment it can engender among jurors.” See supra, n. 32 (Public Discourse Standards

Comments, at 77).

       One of these options (ordering a severance) is irrelevant to this case, one of them

(importing jurors from another district or locality) is not permitted by state law per State v.

Richins, 2025 UT 10, ¶ 72, 568 P.3d 1046 and one of them (impaneling an anonymous jury) has

been overturned on appeal in NBC v. Stewart, 360 F.3d 90 (2d Cir, 2004) and United States v.

Quattrone, 402 F.3d 304 (2005), as acknowledged by the Commentary to Standard 8-5.3. See

supra, n. 32 (Public Discourse Standards Comments, at 79, n.92).

       Four of these options (conducting voir dire as to pretrial publicity, providing clear

cautionary instructions to the jury from the outset of jury selection, providing lawyers with

additional peremptory challenges, and ordering a change of venue) are all designed to address

prejudicial pretrial publicity after it has already taken place. These remedies do not address

Sheppard‘s central concern that “we must remember that reversals are but palliatives; the cure

lies in those remedial measures that will prevent the prejudice at its inception.” Sheppard, 384

U.S at 363 (emphasis added).




                                                 120
       Given the nature and extent of the pretrial publicity that has already occurred in this

capital case, and that is likely to continue up to and including the yet unknown date of the trial,

allowing the electronic media to continue to live stream all proceedings in this case until that

date based on the unrealistic hope that the kind of indelible prejudicial pretrial publicity that the

electronic media has produced and will continue to produce can be fully remedied through a

combination of a change of venue, voir dire, and jury instructions is both unrealistic and

untenable.

       As Chief Justice Rehnquist declared for the majority of the Court in Gentile v. State Bar

of Nevada:

         Few, if any, interests under the Constitution are more fundamental than the
         right to a fair trial by “impartial” jurors, and an outcome affected by
         extrajudicial statements would violate that fundamental right. See, e.g.,
         Sheppard, [384 U.S. at 350-351]; Turner v. Louisiana, [379 U.S. 466, 473
         (1965)] (evidence in criminal trial must come solely from witness stand in
         public courtroom with full evidentiary protections). Even if a fair trial can
         ultimately be ensured through voir dire, change of venue, or some other device,
         these measures entail serious costs to the system. Extensive voir dire may not
         be able to filter out all of the effects of pretrial publicity, and with increasingly
         widespread media coverage of criminal trials, a change of venue may not
         suffice to undo the effects of statements such as those made by petitioner.

501 U.S. 1030, 1075 (1991).

       The Chief Justice’s observations ring especially true in this case where the prejudicial

publicity has been worldwide, and has relentlessly focused on every recognized form of

prejudicial publicity, including: character assassination; alleged confessions to Mr. Robinson’s

family, partner, and even his own attorney; mug shots; shackles; jail clothes; incriminating

demeanor; an alleged refusal to cooperate with law enforcement; Mr. Robinson’s perceived

similarity to Luigi Mangione; Mr. Robinson’s alleged romantic relationship with a transgender

person; the unqualified and repeated assertion by the Governor that he has been “deeply



                                                 121
indoctrinated with leftist ideology”; and, perhaps most pernicious of all, the theme repeatedly

stressed by President Trump and those that support him that Mr. Robinson and the “radical left”

have joined together to murder Charlie Kirk.

        In the indelible words of the Fifth Circuit, “if you throw a skunk into the jury box, you

can’t instruct the jury not to smell it.” Dunn v. United States, 307 F.2d 883, 886 (5th Cir. 1962).

See also Irvin, 366 U.S. at 727 (“With [a prejudicial pretrial] opinion permeating their minds, it

would be difficult to say that each could exclude this preconception of guilt from his

deliberations. The influence that lurks in an opinion once formed is so persistent that it

unconsciously fights detachment from the mental processes of the average man.”); Id. at 728

(“No doubt each juror was sincere when he said that he would be fair and impartial to petitioner,

but the psychological impact requiring such a declaration before one’s fellows is often its

father…. As one of the jurors put it, ‘You can’t forget what you hear and see.’”); Levine v. U.S.

Dist. Ct. for Cent. Dist. of California, 764 F.2d 590, 600 (9th Cir. 1985)(“We recognize…that

jury instructions are often an ineffective remedy.”)); United States v. Concepcion Cueto, 515

F.2d 160, 164 (1st Cir. 1975) (“But a juror’s impressions [of impartiality], however honest,

cannot be dispositive where the improper information is as damning and material as this.”);

Bloeth, 313 F.2d at 372 (“[M]erely going through the form of obtaining jurors’ assurances of

impartiality is insufficient” where “the extent and nature of the publicity has caused such a build-

up of prejudice that excluding the preconception of guilt from the deliberations would be too

difficult for the jury[.]”).

        Here, as in Delaney v. United States, “[o]ne cannot assume that the average juror is so

endowed with a sense of detachment, so clear in his introspective perception of his own mental

processes, that he may confidently exclude even the unconscious influence of his preconceptions



                                                122
as to probable guilt, engendered by a pervasive pre-trial publicity.” 199 F.2d 107, 112-13 (1st

Cir. 1952); accord, Murphy v. Florida, 421 U.S. 794, 802 (1975) (“Even [a juror’s assurance] of

impartiality might be disregarded in a case where the general atmosphere in the community or

courtroom is sufficiently inflammatory.”); State v. Lafferty, 749 P.2d 1239, 1251 (Utah 1988)

(same).

          What these cases have found as a matter of common sense have been backed up by

numerous social science studies. As explained by Judge Gertner in United States v. Houlihan:

           Studies by social scientists suggest that pretrial publicity affects potential
           jurors in ways of which they may not be aware, which they may not be able to
           articulate. Publicity, even if it is not strictly speaking, inflammatory, which
           suggests culpability, or which links the defendant to the criminal activities of
           others, as here, directly affects the readers’ perception of guilt, how likely they
           are to give weight to negative facts about the defendant at trial, to resolve
           conflicts in credibility against the defendants. Publicity unrelated to the crime
           can even have an impact; to be sure, the more similar the publicized cases to
           the case on trial, as here, the greater the effect.”

926 F. Supp. 14, 16-17 (D. Mass. 1996) (citing studies). Accord, United States v. Footman, 33 F.

Supp. 2d 60, 62 (D. Mass. 1998) (Gertner, J.) (barring evidence of a prior conviction because

“[a]necdotal evidence and precedent, not to mention social science studies, make it quite clear

that the jury cannot perform the mental acrobatics the Government would require of them, and

further, that a limiting instruction would be useless”). See also Ex. 2, ¶¶ 33-36 (Dr. Ruva

reviewing and updating social science research demonstrating that biased jurors may not

recognize their biases—undermining voir dire and the effectiveness of limiting instructions).

          What was said about the ability of Oklahoma prospective jurors to set aside the

overwhelming prejudicial pretrial publicity in the Oklahoma City bombing trial is equally true

here:

           Properly motivated and carefully instructed jurors can and have exercised the
           discipline to disregard that kind of prior awareness. Trust in their ability to do


                                                  123
         so diminishes when the prior exposure is such that it evokes strong emotional
         responses or such an identification with those directly affected by the conduct
         at issue that the jurors feel a personal stake in the outcome. That is also true
         when there is such identification with a community point of view that jurors
         feel a sense of obligation to reach a result which will find general acceptance
         in the relevant audience.

McVeigh, 918 F. Supp. at1473.

       In addition, it is not at all clear in light of State v. Richins whether the defense will be

afforded any sort of meaningful in-person individual voir dire, even though, like Richins, this is a

high-profile aggravated murder case involving a vast amount of prejudicial pretrial publicity.

2025 UT 10, ¶¶ 37-49. Tellingly, the reason given in Richins for the denial of such questioning

was that it would “‘compromis[e] ‘the] privacy of the prospective jurors’” and would not be

conducive to creating “ ‘the safe environment needed to answer personal questions truthfully and

free from any pressure to conform to perceived judicial or community standards.’” Id. ¶ 47

(quoting the Presiding Judge). Televising Mr. Robinson’s proceedings certainly risks

compromising the same values.

       Like voir dire and jury instructions, contemplating an eventual moving of the trial to

another location in Utah does nothing to stem the current flow of electronic prejudicial publicity,

and in all likelihood will not be a feasible remedy in this particular high profile and emotionally

charged case, especially if the Court allows that flow to continue unchecked. As Chief Justice

Rehnquist stressed in Gentile v. State Bar of Nevada, , “with increasingly widespread media

coverage of criminal trials, a change of venue may not suffice to undo the effects of [prejudicial

pretrial publicity].” 501 U.S. at 1075; see also Trump, 88 F.4th at 1017 (“Moving the trial to a

different location would also be ineffective. Mr. Trump’s rhetoric has national reach. See Special

Counsel Mot. Reply 9 (noting that Mr. Trump has more than 6 million followers on the platform

Truth Social.”); Levine, 764 F.2d at 600 (“A change of venue would be appropriate if the


                                                 124
publicity surrounding a trial is centered on a specific geographical location. A postponement

would be appropriate if the publicity is temporary. This case presents neither situation.”).

       Regarding the wishful thinking that a continuance will somehow diminish the electronic

media’s thirst for the sensationalistic and biased coverage of this case, this Court has already

found that “[a]s with many high-profile cases, public interest may both wax and wane, but it is

likely to persist.” Dkt. 137, at 6. In any event, it cannot be denied that “[a]llowing prejudicial

[electronic media coverage] to go unchecked for an even longer pre-trial period would simply

compound the problem”, further, “[d]elays also ‘entail serious costs to the [judicial] system,” and

“frustrate the public’s interest in the swift resolution of criminal charges.” Trump, 88 F.4th at

1018 (quoting Gentile, 501 U.S. 1075). See also Bloeth, 313 F.2d at 373 (“While time elapsing

between the original publicity and the trial might in many cases blunt the effect of publicity and

obviate the need for change of venue, it did not in this case, in view of the saturation nature of

the publicity in the non-metropolitan area covered.”).

       Finally, one option not listed in Public Discourse Standard 8-5.3 that has already been

tried by the Court is to simply warn violators of the Court’s decorum order that violations will be

taken seriously. A similar approach was tried, and failed, in United States v. Trump, where the

court noted:

             The Supreme Court has been clear that the First Amendment permits, and
         Article III and due process principles require, courts to do more to protect the
         integrity of the criminal justice process than to shake their finger at a [violator]
         and tell him not to do what the law already forbids. See Sheppard, 384 U.S. at
         362-363[.]

Trump, 88 F.4th at 1008-09. See also Wright, 38 F.R.D. at 438-39 (“I realize, of course, that the

conduct of the press in the past with reference to the protection of the rights of the accused has

not been exemplary. Moreover, even after the press has erred on the side of prejudicial publicity



                                                 125
in landmark cases, …its acts of contrition have consisted of little more than the utterance of

pious platitudes.”).33

          E.     Conclusion

          For all of the reasons stated above, the Court should conclude that it has carefully

considered all available options to terminating electronic media coverage and that none of the

available options would remedy the compelling fair trial, security, privacy, and other factors

discussed herein that singly or in combination compel the conclusion that termination of

electronic media coverage is constitutionally required in this case.

III.      THE TERMINATION OR RESTRICTION OF FURTHER ELECTRONIC MEDIA
          LIVE STREAMING AND PHOTOGRAPHIC COVERAGE OF ANY
          PROCEEDINGS IS ALSO WARRANTED UNDER RULE 4-401.01.

          A.     Introduction

          Rule 4-401.01 was adopted by the Utah Supreme Court’s Rules Committee in 2013 based

on the recommendation of the Final Report of the Judicial Council Study Committee on

Technology Brought into The Courtroom (April 10, 2012) (“Final Report”).34 The Final Report is

a mere 18-pages in length, only 7-pages of which are devoted to the issue of cameras-in-the-

courtroom. Attached to the Final Report are the following Tab numbered documents: (1) an


33
  That is certainly true in this case where media counsel’s response to the decorum order
violation on December 11, 2025 was to claim, based on no presented evidence, that the violation
was “inadvertent”, and that:
           They understand their obligations. They take it very seriously. They’re not
           perfect; mistakes happen. We’re trying to correct as much as we can.
Dkt. 256, at 9. As described above, as counsel was making this argument his cameraman was
violating the decorum order by filming court staff. See supra, p 34.
34
     Available at:
https://legacy.utcourts.gov/committees/Tech_in_Courtrooms/2012.03.23%20Final%20Report.pd
f.



                                                  126
undated document entitled Cameras In The Courtroom: A State-By State Guide, by the Radio

Television Digital News Association (RTDNA), an advocacy organization for journalists and

news organizations; (2) a 1996 law review article by California media lawyer Kelli Sanger; (3) a

1994 Federal Judicial Center report entitled Electronic Media Coverage of Federal Civil

Proceedings: An Evaluation of the Pilot Program In Six District Court and Two Courts of

Appeals “(“1994 Federal Report”); (4) a 2010 law review article by then Ninth Circuit Judge

Alex Kozinski, Of Cameras and Courtrooms, 20 FORDHAM INTELL. PROP. MEDIA & ENT. L.J.

1107 (2010); (5) opinion in Estes v. Texas; (6) opinion in Chandler v. Florida; (7) proposed Rule

4-401, which is more pro-media than the version eventually adopted;35 (8) existing Rule 4-401;

(9) a seven page 2011 Utah State Courts Publication Entitled Social Media Subcommitte of the

Judicial Outreach Committee, which does not address cameras-in-the-courtroom; (10) a 10-page

undated report by five judges entitled Electronic Media Report; and (11) a two page undated

document with no author listed, that does not address cameras in the courtroom.




35
   Jeff Hunt, one of the counsel for the media in this case was one of the authors of the Final
Report. See Journalists Now Allowed to Tweet, Live Blog from Utah Courtroom, Rep.
Committee For Freedom Press (Nov. 20, 2012) https://www.rcfp.org/journalists-now-allowed-
tweet-live-blog-utah-courtrooms/. (“The Utah Supreme Court in February 2011 established a
committee to study the impact of expanding media coverage in courtrooms. The committee was
made up of judges, courtroom administrators and media attorneys, including Hunt.”). Mr. Hunt’s
stance is reflected in his quote that “[t]he language in the rule defining a news reporter is quite
broad, and we drafted it that way intentionally, …It includes any person who is gathering
information for dissemination to the public. You don’t have to be connected to or employed by a
traditional media outlet.”) Although the Judicial Council initially accepted this broad definition,
it narrowed the Rule in 2014 to its present form. See Utah Court Rules – Published for Comment
(Posted: Feb. 24, 2015) https://legacy.utcourts.gov/utc/rules-comment/category/cja04-0401-01/.
The Rule was narrowed again in 2015 to provide that the presumption of openness applied only
“where the predominant purpose of the coverage is journalism or dissemination of news to the
public.” Id.



                                               127
       Relying solely on the 1994 Federal Report, the 1996 law review article by the media

lawyer, which surveyed some pre-1996 state survey data, and Judge Kozinski’s 2010 law review

article (which focused exclusively on his own qualified pro-camera-in-the-courtroom views and

a brief summary of some of the results of the 1994 Federal Report), the authors of the Final

Report opted to swing for the fence with this sweeping generalization:

         The concern most often raised about electronic media coverage is that such
         coverage may harm the decorum of the proceedings and negatively impact trial
         participants. The extensive empirical research and broad-based experience of
         other states, however, suggest that these concerns are unfounded. For example,
         several states, including Arizona, California, Florida, Hawaii, Kansas,
         Louisiana, Maine, Massachusetts, Minnesota, New Jersey, New York, Ohio,
         Virginia, and Washington, have studied the impact of electronic media
         coverage on courtroom proceedings, focusing particularly on the effect that
         cameras have upon courtroom decorum and upon witnesses, jurors, attorneys,
         and judges. See Kelli L. Sager & Karen N. Fredericksen, Televising the
         Judicial Branch: In Furtherance of the Public’s First Amendment Rights, 69 S.
         CAL. L. REV. 1519, 1543 (1996) (“Televising the Judicial Branch”).

              The results from the state studies were unanimous: electronic media
         coverage of courtroom proceedings—whether civil or criminal—has no
         detrimental impact on the parties, jurors, counsel, or courtroom decorum.
         Televising the Judicial Branch, 69 S. CAL. L. REV. at 1544, 1547. For example,
         the state studies revealed that fears about witness distraction, nervousness,
         distortion, fear of harm, and reluctance to testify were unfounded. Id. at 1543-
         44. A three-year pilot program permitting electronic media coverage of civil
         proceedings in six federal district courts and two federal circuit courts reported
         similar results, as well a favorable view of such coverage by the judges who
         participated in the program. See Federal Judicial Center, ELECTRONIC MEDIA
         COVERAGE OF FEDERAL CIVIL PROCEEDINGS: AN EVALUATION OF THE PILOT
         PROGRAM IN SIX DISTRICT COURTS AND TWO COURTS OF APPEALS (1994); see
         also Televising the Judicial Branch, 69 S. CAL. L. REV. at 1545; Alex
         Kozinski & Robert Johnson, Of Cameras and Courtrooms, 20 FORDHAM
         INTELL. PROP. MEDIA & ENT. L.J. 1107, 1114 (2010) (summarizing state and
         federal studies on electronic media coverage of judicial proceedings and
         concluding that the empirical research demonstrates no detrimental impact on
         trial participants or on courtroom decorum).

Final Report, at 8-9.




                                                128
       Because of the age of the Final Report and the fact that it was relying solely on pre-1996

state survey studies, Mr. Robinson retained Dr. Christine L. Ruva, PH.D., who has conducted

many of the modern empirical studies on the effects of pretrial publicity, to “review, evaluate,

and update the empirical research discussed in the Final Report by the Utah Judicial Council

Study Committee on Technology (April 10, 2012)[.]” Ex. 2 ¶ 2. After an exhaustive review of

the vast amount of relevant empirical studies that are now available, Dr. Ruva turned her

attention to the 2012 Final Report and after careful review ultimately concluded that “the

empirical research discussed in the 2012 Judicial Council Final Report is limited and dated and is

based on unreliable survey research.” Id. at Conclusion. In paragraphs 39-62, Dr. Ruva explains

in detail the bases for her expert opinion, carefully walking through all the available literature

that was not considered in the Final Report and examining the fundamental flaws in the pre-1996

state survey data relied upon in the Final Report.

       From a lawyer’s viewpoint, some of Dr. Ruva’ most striking findings relate to the Final

Report’s failure to even mention Hollingsworth v. Perry, 558 U.S. 183 (2010), which, as

explained supra at p. 81, establishes that two years before the Final Report at least five members

of the present Court believed that Justice Harlan’s concurring opinion in Estes was speaking for

“the Court”, and that these same five members share the view of the Estes majority that

televising of a notorious case is improper.

       Dr. Ruva’s declaration states:

                50) The 2012 Final Report’s description of the outcome of the 1994
         federal civil trial pilot program is as follows: “The pilot program was in effect
         from 1991 through 1994. Notwithstanding the Federal Judicial Center’s
         ultimate recommendation that federal trial courts allow cameras in civil
         proceedings, the federal judiciary declined to continue the program when the
         study period expired.” Final Report at 10. However, that description does not
         tell the whole story. As explained by the United States Supreme Court in
         Hollingsworth v. Perry, 558 U.S. 183, 193 (2010):


                                                 129
                In 1996, the Judicial Conference of the United States adopted a policy
             opposing the public broadcast of court proceedings. This policy was
             adopted after a multiyear study of the issue by the Federal Judicial Center
             which drew on data from six district and two appellate courts, as well as
             state-court data. In light of the study’s findings, the Judicial Conference
             concluded that “the intimidating effect of cameras on some witnesses and
             jurors [is] cause for concern.” Administrative Office of United States
             Courts, Report of Proceedings of Judicial Conference of the United States
             47 (Sept. 20, 1994).

               In more than a decade since its adoption the Judicial Conference has
             continued to adhere to its position on the broadcast of court proceedings.

             (emphasis added).

             51) Hollingsworth v. Perry itself recognizes that “[t]his Court has
         recognized that witness testimony may be chilled if broadcast.” Id. at 195,
         citing Estes v. Texas, 381 U.S. 532, 547 (1965); id., at 591 (Harlan, J.,
         concurring). The 2012 Final Report does not discuss Hollingsworth.

       Several other key points made by Dr. Ruva are: (1) “the Sager and Kozinski articles cited

in the report are law review articles and not empirical research articles. They cite state surveys

and one federal survey regarding civil court proceedings…. Survey data cannot be used to infer

causation, and this is not its only limitation - e.g., response bias, recall failure or misinformation

effects (may not accurately recall past event and information after the event affects recall.)” id. ¶

40; (2) “the three-year pilot federal study published in 1994 that found judges have a favorable

opinion of electronic media coverage of court in civil cases tells us nothing about the possible

prejudicial effect such coverage could have on courtroom proceedings, witness testimony and

memory, and juror bias, behavior, and decision making.” id. ¶ 41; (3) “survey research is widely

used, but it comes with several limitations and pitfalls that researchers need to consider” id. ¶ 42;

(4) “[i]n fact, five peer-reviewed journal articles have critiqued the survey studies relied upon in

the 2012 Final Report, and three of these studies were published before the 2012 Report and are

nowhere discussed in it.” id. ¶ 43; (5) “[i]n addition to not acknowledging these critiques, the


                                                 130
2012 Final Report’s description of the existing studies is flawed” id. ¶ 49; (6) “None of these

surveys assessed, or could assess, whether live streaming of all aspects of a high profile criminal

case in the age of social media could cause irreparable conscious and unconscious bias and

trauma to prospective jurors or caused untoward effects in other trial participants[.]” id. ¶ 52; (7)

“[f]inally, the 2012 Final Report does not address the pre-2012 empirical research discussed

above and could not have considered the substantial empirical research that has taken place since

2012.” id. ¶ 53.

       The bottom line here is that in deciding the present motion, this Court should place little

or no weight on the unsupported, dated, and slanted conclusions of the 2012 Final Report.

Instead, the Court should carefully consider “the substantial empirical research that has taken

place since 2012” as reviewed in Dr. Ruva’s declaration. Ex. 2, ¶ 53.

       B.      The “Predominant Purpose” of the Electronic Media Coverage in This Case
               Is Not “Journalism or Dissemination of News to the Public.”

       Judge Kozinski’s law review article, indicates that while generally an advocate for court-

controlled filming and later release of some portions of a trial (he said nothing about pretrial

proceedings and also noted that “nobody seriously believes that cameras should be allowed for

every moment of every trial”), he was of the view that:

         Trials would be more open and transparent if they were held in Madison
         Square Garden, and that would certainly be technologically feasible. Yet the
         Supreme Court has said that a defendant “is entitled to his day in court, not in a
         stadium.” [Estes, 381 U.S. at 549 (1965)] if cameras in the courtroom rob
         criminal defendants and civil litigants of their dignity, and promote a public
         perception of trials as more about sensational entertainment than a sober search
         for truth, courts may be justified in parting ways with other public institutions,
         and public expectations, to exclude cameras in favor of forms of reporting that
         better advance respect for the rule of law and the guarantee of a fair trial.

Kozinski, at 1122.




                                                 131
        Consistent with Judge Kozinski’s view, Rule 4-401.01(2)(A) since 2015 has provided

that: “There is a presumption that electronic media coverage by a news reporter shall be

permitted in public proceedings where the predominant purpose of the electronic media

coverage request is journalism or dissemination of news to the public.” (emphasis added). No

Utah court has construed any aspect of Rule 4-401.01, and the emphasized language is not a

feature of any other state or federal court rule or statute. The Court is therefore left with little

guidance as to how to apply this aspect of the Rule.36

        In his article, Judge Kozinski provided this example of when courts should prohibit

camera coverage on the ground that such coverage “rob[s] criminal defendants and civil litigants

of their dignity, and promote[s] a public perception of trials as more about sensational

entertainment than a sober search for truth”:

         Unlike concern with the effect of cameras inside the courtroom, this argument
         retains real bite after the O.J. experience. Consider footage of the verdict
         (available now on YouTube). It’s high drama: As the courtroom waited, the
         camera zoomed in for an intense close-up of O.J.’s face, and remained there
         for the agonizing moments before-and during-the verdict. Because the camera
         was positioned above the jury, O.J. appeared to gaze ominously into the
         camera’s eye. Ron Goldman’s sister began to cry, and the camera pivoted for a
         close-up of her face. From there, to the stunned faces of the prosecution. And
         back to Ron Goldman’s sister. As the Supreme Court has said, “[t]he television
         camera is a powerful weapon” and “inevitable close-ups of [the accused’s]
         gestures and expressions during the ordeal of his trial might well transgress his
         personal sensibilities [and] his dignity.” [Estes, 381 U.S. at 549]. That’s to say
         nothing of the impact on the victim’s family, or the public perception of a trial
         depicted in such a manner.


36
   But see United States House of Representatives, Rules of the House of Representative, Rule
XI(4) (Jan. 10, 2023) (permitting committee hearings or committee meetings to be covered by
audio and visual means “for the education, enlightenment, and information of the general public,
on the basis of accurate and impartial news coverage”, but specifically providing that “it is the
intent of this clause that audio and video recordings of any coverage…may not be used for any
partisan political campaign purpose or be made available for such use” and that “[i]f audio or
visual coverage of the hearing or meeting is to be presented to the public as live coverage, that
coverage shall be conducted and presented without commercial sponsorship.”).

                                                  132
Kozinski, at 1122.

       Judge Kozinski describes exactly the kind of sensationalism and dramatization that has so

far characterized the prejudicial electronic pretrial publicity in this case with its almost exclusive

close-up focus on Mr. Robinson’s and the Court’s demeanor, Mr. Robinson’s alleged

confessions, his mugshot and shackles, and the uninformed, speculative, and sensationalistic

musings of media talking heads.

       The extent to which advertising profit and sensationalism are at the root of most of the

publicity in this case is underscored by the sensationalistic content of the numerous

representative postings referenced above, by the fact that they all feature product advertisements,

and by the further undeniable fact that “[j]ust hours after the tragic news of Charlie Kirk’s

assassination was confirmed, a flurry of ads featuring memorial merchandise exploded across

social media platforms like Facebook and Instagram.” Foreign Brands Profit From Memorial

Merchandise After Tragedy, HERE SHREVEPORT (Sep. 26, 2025); see also Keenan Chen, Merch

sellers cash in on Kirk’s killing with flood of social media ads, THE GUARDIAN, (Sep. 25, 2025,

at 11:26 ET)

https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2025/sep/25/charlie-kirk-merch-social-media-ads

(itself featuring advertisements). “In a viral post with more than 8,000 shares and hundreds of

thousands of likes, the brand See Jesus In You promoted an ‘Echo of Freedom’ shirt with an

American flag and Kirk’s signature, calling him a symbol of ‘faith, liberty, and America’. The

simple shirt, which Kirk was wearing when he was assassinated, has become a hot commodity

among his conservative supporters. Another clothing brand, Liberty Faith Gear, struck a much

more combative tone in marketing its version of the freedom tee, as it urged ‘patriots [to] rise

when freedom is attacked. We do not hide. We do not bow’”. Chen, Merch sellers cash in.



                                                 133
       Some courts have attempted to prevent such dramatization and sensationalism by

prohibiting the media from focusing on the defendant’s demeanor, or, as Judge Kozinski

suggested, by taking control of the camera away from the media and releasing a court video only

after a delay to allow time for the parties to lodge objections to release. See Kozinski, at 1127-28

(noting that “it’s no longer the case that the courtroom camera must be operated by the media, as

it was during the O.J. trial” and that “[c]ombined with a delay before posting”, a court-created

video “can be presented in as boring and straightforward a fashion as you please: no close-ups,

no moving cameras and no filming of the defense table or the gallery.”).

       In the highly publicized case of State v. Bryan Kohberger, the trial court allowed the

media to retain control of all cameras, but insisted that the media stop zooming in on

Kohberger’s demeanor, a measure that this Court has so far not imposed. This solution, however,

failed to work because the media could not control itself. The trial judge therefore ruled:

              As demonstrated by some of the examples presented in the defense’s
         Motion to Remove Cameras from Courtroom, media cameras, both still and
         video, have and continue to zoom in on Kohberger despite the Court’s order
         that images should capture the courtroom in its entirety. At least some of the
         images are of Kohberger entering and exiting the courtroom. This is in direct
         violation of the Court’s previous directives not to film or take photographs
         until the Court is on the record and to immediately stop recording and taking
         photographs when the hearing concludes. It is the intense focus on Kohberger
         and his every move, along with adverse headlines and news articles, that leads
         the Court to conclude that continued photograph and video coverage inside the
         courtroom by the media should no longer be permitted. This step should help
         ensure Kohberger’s right to a fair trial by an impartial jury and achieve a
         proper administration of justice.

              Additionally, defense counsel has raised continued concerns with video
         footage and photographs capturing private documents on counsel table. This is
         again in violation of the Court’s directives. The State has also raised issues of
         vulnerable victims and witnesses being filmed during testimony. At this
         juncture, the Court has no confidence that directives to stop photographing or
         videoing during such testimony would be adhered to. Further, the duty on the
         bailiffs within the courtroom to ensure a safe and secure environment is being



                                                134
         eclipsed by the burden to monitor media and the public who may be audio
         recording, videoing, or photographing the proceedings.

State v. Bryan Kohberger, Case No. CR29-22-2805 (D.C. Latah County Idaho, Nov. 17, 2023)

(Order Denying Second Motion to Intervene and Granting Defendant’s Motion to Remove Media

Cameras From the Courtroom, attached as Exhibit 5).

       A majority of the justices in Estes unequivocally condemned the kind of sensationalistic

and profit-driven electronic media coverage that has occurred in this case, where sensationalism,

advertising, and zooming in on Mr. Robinson for the purpose of commenting on his demeanor,

shackles, and attire is clearly the predominant purpose of the electronic coverage. Justice Clark’s

lead opinion in Estes states:

         The television camera is a powerful weapon. Intentionally or inadvertently it
         can destroy an accused and his case in the eyes of the public. While our
         telecasters are honorable men, they too are human. The necessity for
         sponsorship weighs heavily in favor of the televising of only notorious cases,
         such as this one, and invariably focuses the lens upon the unpopular or
         infamous accused. Such a selection is necessary in order to obtain a sponsor
         willing to pay a sufficient fee to cover the costs and return a profit. We have
         already examined the ways in which public sentiment can affect the trial
         participants. To the extent that television shapes that sentiment, it can strip the
         accused of a fair trial.

381 U.S. at 549-50.

       Chief Justice Warren, CJ, joined by Justices Douglas and Goldberg, agreed with and

elaborated upon Justice Clark’s analysis, writing that:

         The entire hearing on September 24 was televised live by station KLTV of
         Tyler, Texas, and station WFAA—TV of Dallas, Texas. Commercials were
         inserted when there was a pause in the proceedings. On the evening of
         Monday, September 24, both stations ran an edited tape of the day’s
         proceedings and interrupted the tape to play the commercials ordinarily seen in
         the particular time slot.

Id. at 554; see also id. at 571 (“The televising of trials would cause the public to equate the trial

process with the forms of entertainment regularly seen on television and with the commercial


                                                 135
objectives of the television industry. In the present case, tapes of the September 24 hearing were

run in place of the ‘Tonight Show’ by one station and in place of the late night movie by another.

Commercials for soft drinks, soups, eyedrops and seatcovers were inserted when there was a

pause in the proceedings. In addition, if trials were televised there would be a natural tendency

on the part of broadcasters to develop the personalities of the trial participants, so as to give the

proceedings more of an element of drama. This tendency was noticeable in the present case.”).

       Perhaps most significantly, Justice Harlan, who supplied the crucial fifth vote in the case,

also agreed with Justice Clark’s analysis:

         It is said that the pictorial broadcasting of trials will serve to educate the public
         as to the nature of the judicial process. Whatever force such arguments might
         have in run-of-the-mill cases, they carry little weight in cases of the sort before
         us, where the public’s interest in viewing the trial is likely to be engendered
         more by curiosity about the personality of the well-known figure who is the
         defendant (as here), or about famous witnesses or lawyers who will appear on
         the television screen, or about the details of the particular crime involved, than
         by innate curiosity to learn about the workings of the judicial process itself.
         Indeed it would be naive not to suppose that it would be largely such factors
         that would qualify a trial for commercial television ‘billing,’ and it is precisely
         that kind of case where the risks of permitting television coverage of the
         proceedings are at their greatest.

Id. at 594-95 (Harlan, J., concurring).

       Consistent with the various opinions on Estes, the Utah Supreme Court concluded in

Sinclair v. Turner, :

         We appreciate that the facts of life must be taken into account. The public is
         intrigued by the sensational, and thus by crime and the facts surrounding it.
         Consequently, it is but natural and perhaps necessary that the information
         media cater to that appetite. Yet this should also be considered: the service
         they perform so vitally affects the public welfare that in consideration of the
         guarantees of freedom of expression the law assures to them, they should be
         regarded as having corresponding responsibility to perform their duties in an
         awareness that in our system, they and the public whom they serve, all have an
         essential interest in, and in a sense are all involved in law enforcement. There
         should be a minimizing of sensationalism by the exercise of reasonable
         restraint and good judgment in reporting the news in as fair and objective a


                                                 136
         manner as possible, in an awareness of the desirability and indeed of the
         necessity of according those suspected or accused of crime fair treatment and a
         trial by an impartial court and jury.

434 P.2d at 307 (emphasis added).

       In other words, according to Estes and Sinclair, the goal of televising court proceedings is

to make money and to cater to the appetite of the public for the “sensational.” “Suggestions that

televised trials educate the public are disingenuous. The goal is not education but sensationalism

and profit. To be sure, what we must be most concerned about is the defendant’s right to a fair

trial, not entertainment.” Nevers v. Killinger, 990 F. Supp. 844, 849, n. 2 (E.D. Mich. 1997),

rev’d on other grounds, 169 F.3d 352 (6th Cir. 1999); Moussaoui, 205 F.R.D. at 188 (“contrary

to what intervenors have argued, the purpose of this trial is not to provide catharsis to the victims

or to educate the world about the American legal system. Instead, the purpose is to determine the

innocence or guilt of this defendant for the specific crimes charged in the Indictment.”).

       Given the sensationalistic and profit-driven nature and the extraordinary extent of the

electronic media coverage of Mr. Robinson’s case as outlined above, and for the reasons stated

by the Justices in Estes and by the Utah Supreme Court in Sinclair, it should be concluded that

because the “predominant purpose” of the electronic media coverage in this case has not been

and is unlikely ever to be “journalism or dissemination of news to the public[.]”, The

presumption in favor of electronic media coverage does not apply to Mr. Robinson’s case under

the express terms of Rule 4-401.01(2)(A).

       C.      Any Presumption in Favor of Electronic Media Coverage Has Been
               Overcome.
