       The 2012 Final Report expressly acknowledges that

         [E]lectronic media coverage of trial court proceedings raises concerns about
         fair trial rights, personal privacy, safety, security interests, and other legitimate
         interests. Accordingly, the proposed revision of Rule 4-401 balances those
         interests by permitting electronic media coverage of open judicial proceedings

                                                 137
         while allowing a judge to prohibit or restrict such coverage to protect fair trial
         rights, privacy, security, and other important interests.

Final Report, at 11.

       Rule 4-401.01(2)(B) requires that when determining whether the presumption of

electronic media coverage has been overcome and whether such coverage should be prohibited

or restricted beyond the limitations in the Rule, a judge “shall” consider “some or all” of nine

specific factors. In this capital case, all of these factors weigh heavily in favor of prohibiting

electronic live streaming and photographic coverage, and their combined weight clearly

outweighs any factor that favors a contrary result. The factors are:

       (i)     whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will
               prejudice the right of the parties to a fair proceeding;

       This is the first and clearly the weightiest factor under the Rule because it is based on Mr.

Robinson’s state and federal constitutional right to fair, impartial, and reliable proceedings, as

well as on the state’s independent due process right to fair proceedings. Mr. Robinson cannot

speak for the State, but he does note that in a previous filing the State and the Sheriff urged the

Court to “limi[t] media coverage for all in-person and remote hearings.” Dkt. 109, at 18-19

(classified as private).37 What Mr. Robinson can speak to is the fact that the Supreme Court has

“always held that the atmosphere essential to the preservation of a fair trial—the most

fundamental of all freedoms—must be maintained at all costs.” Estes, 381 U.S. at 540. At the

same time, the Court has consistently held that there is no First Amendment right to live stream

or photograph court proceedings.




37
  Although in Dkt. 137, this Court granted defendant’s motion to strike this pleading, it did so
with the understanding that “as agreed to by the Defendant, the court has considered the
substance of the State and Sheriff’s opposing memorandum.” Id. at 3 (emphasis in original).

                                                 138
        As also indicated above, in State v. James, the Utah Supreme Court applied a reasonable

likelihood standard in a change of venue context, which is the same standard used in Rule 4-

401.01(2)(B). 767 P.2d at 555 (Utah 1989). James thus provides essential guidance in applying

this particular factor.

        The James Court cited and followed the California’s Supreme Court’s decision in Maine

v. Superior Court, 438 P.2d 372 (Cal. 1968), in holding that regardless of language used in a

Utah statute, the constitutionally correct standard to apply when assessing the effects of pretrial

publicity is the reasonable likelihood standard. James, 767 P.2d at 552. It also cited and followed

Frazier v. Superior Court of Santa Cruz County, 486 P.2d 694 (Cal. 1971) in holding that “a

reasonable likelihood of prejudice does not mean that the prejudice must be more probable than

not.” Id. Finally, it cited and followed Martinez v. Superior Court of Placer County, 629 P.2d

502 (Cal. 1981) in holding that “the factors which have been considered criteria of the potential

for prejudice from pretrial publicity…include (1) the standing of the victim and the accused in

the community; (2) the size of the community; (3) the nature and gravity of the offense; and (4)

the nature and extent of publicity.” Id. Each of these factors support granting the present motion.

        (1) the standing of the victim and the accused in the community

        Neither Mr. Kirk nor Mr. Robinson is a resident of Utah County. However, Mr. Kirk has

consistently been portrayed in the electronic media as a Christian, a devoted father, a national

hero, a martyr, and a potential Republican candidate for the Presidency, while Mr. Robinson has

consistently been portrayed as a “monster” (President Trump), a “depraved assassin” (President

Trump), a person who is “everything that the far left wants from our young men [who] rejected

the conservatism and the spirituality, the values of a small-town family [and]…moved into a

small apartment, he became addicted to porn, he became addicted to hate, and he ended up



                                                139
sleeping with somebody who doesn’t know whether they’re a man or a woman” (Vice President

J.D. Vance), a “horrible human being” (Attorney General Bondi), a “evil human being”

(Governor Cox), a person “deeply indoctrinated by leftist ideology” (Governor Cox), ); a “true

sociopath” (Retired FBI Agent Parker); and a “evil, evil human being” (Megyn Kelly).

       Given these starkly contrasting portrayals, which will no doubt continue in the future, this

factor weighs strongly in favor of finding a reasonable likelihood that further electronic media

live streaming and photographic coverage will prejudice the right of Mr. Robinson to fair

proceedings. See, e.g., Martinez, 629 P.2d at 507-08 (this factor weighed in favor of a venue

change where although “ the victim was ‘no public figure,’ his status nonetheless appears to be

significant” and where “[o]n the other hand, the press described petitioner in terms unlikely to

evoke the sympathy or concern of the community.”).

       (2) the size of the community

       In the 2024 Presidential Election, Utah County listed 368,875 registered voters. See

supra, Countywide Summary Results for Utah County, 2024 Gen. Election. This Court has

already found: that “the media interest in Mr. Robinson’s trial is arguably unprecedented in the

State of Utah”; that “[p]hotographs and visual recordings of Mr. Robinson in jail clothing will

likely be shared and disseminated in various public platforms”; that “[t]here is a strong

likelihood that a substantial number of potential jurors in Utah County will view these images

and may associate Mr. Robinson’s jail clothing with indicia of guilt”; that “[t]he danger of

prejudice is compounded by technological advancements that allow the images to be

manipulated in ways that do not accurately reflect the truth of the court proceedings”; that

“[t]his case has generated an immense amount of pre-trial publicity”; that “[a]s with many high-

profile cases, public interest may both wax and wane, but it is likely to persist”; that “[v]isual



                                                 140
recordings and photographs of Mr. Robinson shackled may give the impression that he is

dangerous and may undermine his presumption of innocence”; that “[t]he potential jury pool in

Utah County, based on their proximity, is likely to be even more exposed to the images of these

proceedings and the messages those images send”; and that “[t]his case generates extraordinary

ongoing national and international attention, some of which is threatening in nature to the parties,

their representatives, and non-party participants (e.g. witnesses and court personnel).” Dkt. 137,

at 5-6; Dkt. 266, at 2.

        As stated in Maine v. Superior Ct. of Mendocino Cnty., even “a large city may …become

so hostile to a defendant as to make a fair trial unlikely.” 438 P.2d at 380, n. 13. And relatively

speaking, Utah County cannot be considered a “large” venue, especially when one contemplates

how many prospective jurors would need to be removed from service because of the death

penalty qualification process, and how many of the prospective pool voted for Mr. Trump in

2024 Presidential Election. See, e.g., Casellas-Toro, 807 F.3d at 386-87 (1st Cir. 2015) (finding a

presumption of prejudice warranted even though “more than 3 million people live in [the

venue]”); Powell v. Superior Ct., 283 Cal. Rptr. 777, 783, 788 (Cal. Ct. App. 1991) (even though

the potential jury pool in Los Angeles County was 6.526 million, a change of venue of the case

involving the beating of Rodney King was required because “there is a substantial probability

Los Angeles County is so saturated with knowledge of the incident, so influenced by the political

controversy surrounding the matter and so permeated with preconceived opinions that potential

jurors cannot try the case solely upon the evidence presented in the courtroom.”).

        Given the Court’s findings, and considering the nature and extent of the prejudicial

publicity in this capital case and the relatively small size of the eligible jury pool in Utah County,




                                                 141
this factor weighs in favor of a finding that there is a reasonable likelihood that further electronic

media live streaming will prejudice the right of the parties to a fair proceeding.

        (3) the nature and gravity of the offense

        As indicated above, in State v. James, the Utah Supreme Court held that the fact that the

State is seeking the death penalty is an important factor weighing in favor of a finding that there

is a reasonable likelihood that media coverage will prejudice the right to a fair trial. As instructed

by James, in a capital case, “[t]he judgment should be made in an atmosphere as free from any

taint of bias or prejudice as is reasonably possible.” 767 P.2d at 555. As explained in Martinez,

“[t]he peculiar facts or aspects of a crime which make it sensational, or otherwise bring it to the

consciousness of the community, define its ‘nature’; the term ‘gravity’ of a crime refers to its

seriousness in the law and to the possible consequences to an accused in the event of a guilty

verdict.” 629 P.2d at 506.

        Here, the peculiar facts or aspects of the crime which make it sensational are obvious.

From the beginning, the crime has been characterized by the President and his supporters,

including the Governor, as a cold-blooded “political assassination” that has had a profound

impact on the entire nation. The electronic media has dutifully broadcast that sensational

characterization across the nation on an almost daily basis. And as in Martinez,

         The seriousness of the charged crime stands out clearly. Murder is a crime of
         utmost gravity; inasmuch as the state is seeking the death penalty, it is a crime
         of the gravest consequences to petitioner. Because it carries such grave
         consequences, a death penalty case inherently attracts press coverage; in such a
         case the factor of gravity must weigh heavily in a determination regarding
         [remedial measures].

Id. at 507.




                                                 142
        (4) the nature and extent of publicity.

        The sensationalistic and prejudicial nature and overwhelming extent of the prejudicial

electronic and other pretrial publicity in Mr. Robinson’s case is set forth above at length. That

publicity features every one of the several categories of publicity that courts and other authorities

have recognized as inherently prejudicial. See, e.g., Skilling, 561 U.S. at 382 (a presumption of

juror prejudice is warranted where the pretrial publicity references a “confession or other

blatantly prejudicial information of the type readers or viewers could not reasonably be expected

to shut from sight.”); Gentile, 501 U.S. at 1046 (noting that public statements that are “obvious

bases for a finding of prejudice “ include mention of a confession or of “evidence from searches

or test results.”). See also supra, fn. 4 (setting forth the categories of prejudicial publicity that are

considered inherently prejudicial under governing standards).

        In addition to featuring every category of prejudicial pretrial publicity recognized in

Skilling, Gentile, and the relevant standards, the prejudicial electronic media publicity in this

case has also heavily stressed several additional sensationalistic themes that courts have

recognized as necessitating strong remedial measures.

        For example, in Maine, the court stressed that “[p]olitical factors have no place in a

criminal proceeding, and when they are likely to appear, as here, they constitute an independent

reason for [requiring remedial action].” 438 P.2d at 380. See also People v. Tidwell 473 P.2d

748, 754 (Cal. 1970) (en banc) (news items indicating there was “considerable political debate

concerning the fiscal impact of the trial,” was one of the factors supporting a change of venue).

        Similarly, in Powell, political factors required a change of venue from Los Angeles

County, the court concluding that “[w]hile we recognize that the incident and some of its

ramifications have received widespread publicity elsewhere, the impact on residents of Los



                                                  143
Angeles is unquestionably much greater because of the unabated and acrimonious total

involvement of city officials and local community leaders. There is no doubt that these political

biases would invade the jury box if the case were tried in Los Angeles County.” 283 Cal. Rptr. at

787.

       As in Tidwell, Mr. Robinson’s case is also one where there has been extensive debate in

the electronic media about the cost of the trial.38 However, the prejudicial political rhetoric here

has gone far beyond a concern for the cost of the trial and has featured a relentless drumbeat of

electronic media interviews of a Republican President, Vice President, Attorney General, FBI

Director, White House staff, and the Governor, all pushing the narrative that Mr. Robinson is

somehow part of a “radical left” conspiracy that must be stamped out to protect the republic. As

in Powell, the impact of this inflammatory political rhetoric in the predominantly Republican

Utah County is “unquestionably much greater because of the unabated and acrimonious total

involvement of city officials and local [state, and federal] community leaders.” 283 Cal. Rptr, at

787.

       Another dominant theme in the electronic media coverage has been an almost

pathological focus on Mr. Robinson’s mug shots, shackles, jail clothes, alleged incriminating

demeanor, body language, and lip movements, and his alleged refusal to cooperate with law

enforcement. Here, as long as the cameras are allowed to remain, Mr. Robinson is in a no-win

situation. If he sits in court without any facial expression or verbal communication with his

attorneys he will be labelled by the media as a stone-faced, remorseless sociopath. In fact, that

was exactly the commentary during the Sheriff’s September 16, 2025 press conference. On the

other hand, if he attempts to engage with his lawyers in a normal human interaction and happens

to smile at something that has nothing to do with his case, then he will be labelled as a smirking

remorseless sociopath.

        This Court has correctly found that “[p]hotographs and visual recordings of Mr.

Robinson in jail clothing will likely be shared and disseminated in various public platforms”, that

there is “a strong likelihood that a substantial number of potential jurors in Utah County will

view these images and may associate Mr. Robinson’s jail clothing with indicia of guilt”, that

“[t]he danger of prejudice is compounded by technological advancements that allow the images

to be manipulated in ways that do not accurately reflect the truth of the court proceedings”, and

that “[v]isual recordings and photographs of Mr. Robinson shackled may give the impression

that he is dangerous and may undermine his presumption of innocence”, and that “the potential

jury pool in Utah County, based on their proximity, is likely to be even more exposed to the

images of these proceedings and the messages those images send.” These findings are consistent

with the conclusions reached by the United States Supreme Court in Deck, 544 U.S. at 633, and

should lead the Court to conclude that in light of how the electronic media has chosen to cover the




                                                145
proceedings, and the likelihood that such sensationalistic coverage will continue, that the Court

will not allow any further live streaming or photographing of any of the proceedings.

       The electronic media, guided by commentators like the Vice President and the Governor,

also seem to be unusually fixated on Mr. Robinson’s alleged romantic relationship with his

roommate and with the fact that the roommate is apparently transitioning from a male to a female.

And this fixation has continued even though the Governor in his September 14, 2025 CNN

interview could not come up with any explanation as to the legal relevance of these facts. The

Governor mentioned these themes in all three of his national broadcasts on September 14, 2025,

and the Vice President, against the backdrop of a large fireworks display and huge applause,

announced at a recent Turning Point event on December 21, 2015 that Mr. Robinson was a person

who is “everything that the far left wants from our young men [who] rejected the conservatism

and the spirituality, the values of a small-town family [and]…moved into a small apartment, he

became addicted to porn, he became addicted to hate, and he ended up sleeping with somebody

who doesn’t know whether they’re a man or a woman”. Faced with a similar situation, the court

in Frazier v. Superior Ct., concluded:

         We recognize that in some criminal trials there is at least the possibility,
         regrettably, that the defendant may be judged on his appearance and life style
         rather than on his proven acts. In the circumstances here shown, however, that
         consequence is more probable than possible…. There may not be, as the trial
         court found, hostility toward this particular defendant as an individual; but so
         long as there is hostility towards the group or subculture with which he is
         identified, he runs the very real risk of being judged not for what he has done
         but for what he is, or what he appears to be.

486 P.2d at 699.

       The community’s involvement in the hunt for the killer and the electronic media’s

outpouring of support for Mr. Kirk and his family are also important factors to consider when

assessing the nature and extent of prejudicial publicity. Here, as reported by FBI Director Kash


                                                146
Patel on September 12, 2025, “[l]ast night, we had a total of approximately 7,000 interviews—

excuse me, 7,000 leads. As of this morning, thanks to your great work, we have over 11,000

leads that were called in to the FBI, and we are running out every single lead that we can. Every

one of those leads will be run out.” See supra, Kash Patel, Director Patel’s Remarks. Consistent

with this widespread local community sentiment, the electronic media has produced lengthy

tributes to Mr. Kirk and have closely followed all aspects of his family’s grieving process, where

the event has been attended by thousands and online viewings of such events are in the millions.

As was concluded in Maine, “[w]e do not hold it to be an invariable rule that sympathy for a

victim demonstrates antipathy to the alleged perpetrators of an offense. But such pervasive civic

involvement in the fate of a victim, particularly when the events all transpire in a relatively small

community, is a strong indication that [remedial measures] should be [employed].” 438 P.2d at

379 (footnotes omitted).

       A final factor to consider when assessing the nature and extent of prejudicial publicity in

this case is the fact, discussed more fully below, that such publicity, especially in the electronic

media, has focused on violent rhetoric, which in turn has already led to threats against and

harassment of trial participants, including counsel and Mr. Robinson’s parents. See Exs. 3, 4. As

was the case in Powell, “[s]uch unacceptable attempts to influence the judicial proceedings at

this early stage add another impermissible factor into the boiling cauldron surrounding this case,

making it imperative to take every step possible to ensure that an impartial unbiased jury be

seated.” 283 Cal. Rptr. at 787.

       In sum, for all of the foregoing reasons, there is clearly a reasonable likelihood that

continuing to allow the electronic media to live-stream all proceedings in this case will prejudice




                                                 147
Mr. Robinson’s fundamental State and Federal constitutional rights to fair, impartial, and reliable

proceedings.

       (ii)    whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will
               jeopardize the safety or well-being of any individual;

international attention, some of which is threatening in nature to the parties, their representatives,

and non-party participants (e.g. witnesses and court personal).” Dkt. 265, at 2.

Mr. Robinson’s alleged actions have put the nation on the brink of a civil war that will be averted

only if there is “crackdown” on Mr. Robinson and all of this alleged supporters.

       Given this widespread and frightening attitude and the vehemence of the fire and

serious risk of injury if the Court were to permit their images and conduct to be broadcast to the

world on a prolonged and eventually daily basis in this highly emotionally charged case.39

       As Judge Kozinski wrote in his article, “When the public sees a trial for itself, or through

the lens of the camera, there’s always a risk of misunderstanding: The public may mistake

zealous advocacy for obstruction of justice, or vice versa.” Kozinski, at 1123.

       All it would take to lead to violence would be one unhinged and violent person who was

influenced by the inflammatory rhetoric in this case, was offended by Mr. Robinson, or the

performance of counsel on either side, or the Court, or other trial participants, and felt it was his

or her patriotic duty in this new “civil war” to engage in violence against the perceived “enemy.”

Indeed, one of the Court’s justification for restraining Mr. Robinson in the courtroom was for his

both Mr. Robinson and court patrons are likely to feel strong emotions.” Dkt. 137, at 6. But

restraints on Mr. Robinson, and armed guards in combat gear in the courtroom, while they will

go a long way in projecting the prejudicial image of Mr. Robinson as guilty and dangerous and

therefore deserving of death, will do nothing to stop the threat of violence outside the courtroom.

That threat cannot be entirely eliminated even if electronic media coverage is not permitted, but

       As stated in Moussaoui:

         The traditional public spectator or media representative who attends a federal
         criminal trial leaves the courtroom with his or her memory of the proceedings
         and any notes he or she may have taken. These spectators do not leave with a
         permanent photograph. However, once a witness’ testimony has been
         televised, the witness’ face has not just been publicly observed, it has also
         become eligible for preservation by VCR or DVD recording, digitizing by the
         new generation of cameras or permanent placement on Internet web sites and
         chat rooms. Today, it is not so much the small, discrete cameras or
         microphones in the courtroom that are likely to intimidate witnesses, rather, it
         is the witness’ knowledge that his or her face or voice may be forever publicly
         known and available to anyone in the world…. Even if every witness’ face
         were obscured, the faces of the attorneys, court staff, and security officers
         would be exposed, subjecting them to the possibility of long-term security
         problems.

205 F.R.D. at 186-87. See also Perry v. City & Cnty. of San Francisco, No. 10-16696, 2011 WL

2419868, at *78 (9th Cir. Apr. 27, 2011) (“The presence of cameras in the courtroom is likely to

heighten the level and the potential of threats to judges. The number of threats against judges has

escalated over the years, and widespread media exposure could exacerbate the problem.

Additionally, all witnesses, jurors, and United States Marshals Service personnel may be put at

risk because they would no longer have a low public profile.”(Statement of Chief Judge Becker

before the United States Senate)).

       There is also a legitimate concern in this case that because the threats of violence and

“civil war” are originating from President Trump and his supporters, that the trial participants

will be so distracted by security concerns that they will be hindered in the performance of their

duties. As concluded in United States v. Trump,


                                                152
         Messages designed to generate alarm and dread, and to trigger extraordinary
         safety precautions, will necessarily hinder the trial process and slow the
         administration of justice. For example, trial personnel and participants will be
         distracted or delayed by objectively reasonable concerns about their safety and
         that of their family members, as well as by having to devote time and resources
         to adopting safety measures or working with investigators.

88 F.4th at 1014.

       As most recently observed by the New York Times, “[t]oday’s algorithmically driven-

attention hungry information ecosystem does more than distort reality. It also stokes anger that

divides society along political or social lines…For those caught in the vortex of an internet

storm-fairly or not- the experience can be traumatic (and dangerous).” Steven Meyers, Top

Leaders Fuel a Surge In Falsehoods: A Traditional Restraint on Rumors Fades, at A2, A12, NY

TIMES (Dec. 27, 2025). See also, Ex. 2, ¶ 17 (Dr. Ruva: “Conventional, and social media, and

especially live stream publicity of or about traumatic events such as assassinations, terrorism,

and other mass acts of violence can cause trauma and emotional responses such as anger, disgust,

and fear that distort decision-making and lead to bias.”).

       There is also another aspect to the issue of whether there is a reasonable likelihood that

electronic media coverage will jeopardize the safety or well-being of any individual. As aptly

summarized:

         The tragedy also highlights risks that will shape America’s trajectory in the
         coming years. In the short term, there is a heightened danger of copycat actions
         or retaliatory violence, as ideologically motivated individuals draw inspiration
         from either the act itself or the reactions to it. Security requirements for public
         figures—already significant—will only intensify. In the medium term, the
         event is likely to deepen polarization, as each side interprets the killing through
         its own tribal lens. This will provide fertile ground for online radicalization and
         recruitment, further eroding social cohesion. In the long term, unless these
         dynamics are checked, the United States faces the prospect of democratic
         backsliding. Trust, empathy, and tolerance could continue to diminish, while
         political violence becomes normalized as a form of expression rather than
         condemned as an aberration.

A Nation Divided: What the Charlie Kirk Shooting Reveals About America, THE SOUFAN

       Many of the media outlets in this case have compared this case to the Kennedy

assassination. Whether that comparison is accurate or not has itself been the subject of fierce

debate.40 But one aspect of the Kennedy assassination and its aftermath does have relevance to

the issue considered here. As was found in the Warren Commission Report, the media’s clamor

for unrestrained access to Mr. Oswald combined with the media’s overreliance on statements

made by law enforcement presented serious obstacles to the obtaining of a fair trial for Oswald

and contributed to his murder before he ever had the opportunity for a fair trial:

              Newspaper, radio, and television reporters were allowed uninhibited
         access to the area through which Oswald had to pass when he was moved from
         his cell to the interrogation room and other section of the building, thereby
         subjecting Oswald to harassment and creating chaotic conditions which were
         not conductive to orderly interrogation or the protection of the rights of the
         prisoner.

        As alluded to by the Governor, the federal courts have so far blocked pioneering efforts

by the Utah Legislature to control the flow of harmful social media content to minors. See Utah

Minor Protection in Social Media Act, Utah Code §§ 13-71-101 to 401. Two legislative findings

in support of that legislation were that “the state has a compelling interest in safeguarding the



                                                  156
well-being and privacy of minors in the state”, and that “prolonged and unregulated social media

use has been linked to adverse effects on the mental health of minors, including increased rates

of anxiety, depression, and social isolation.” Utah Code § 13-71-102.

       A federal district court has issued a preliminary injunction barring the enforcement of the

Act on the ground that the plaintiff social media association was likely to succeed on the merits

of its claim that the Act violated its members’ First Amendment rights. NetChoice, LLC v. Reyes,

748 F. Supp. 3d 1105 (D. Utah 2024). That decision is currently on appeal to the Tenth Circuit.

See NetChoice v. Brown, et al, Case No. 24-4100 (10th Cir., Oct. 11, 2024). Because of the

pendency of this appeal, there are currently in force no effective controls in Utah on the use of

social media by minors. This means that in this case, unless this Court cuts off the steady stream

of hateful, violent, fabricated imagery and narratives, and downright false conspiracy and

political rhetoric that has flowed directly from the electronic media coverage of this case,

through the amplifying and distorting filter of social media, to the eyes and ears of all Utah

citizens, then all minors in Utah who use social media will be exposed to such toxic coverage.41

       Even if there was a First Amendment right to record and broadcast court proceedings,

which there clearly is not, the State’s “compelling interest in safeguarding the well-being …of

minors in the state” would clearly outweigh the existence of such a hypothetical right in this

case, given the false and incendiary nature of the electronic media coverage that has pervaded

this case. See Globe Newspaper, 457 U.S. at 607 (finding that “safeguarding the physical and
