        (iv) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will
             constitute an unwarranted invasion of personal privacy of any person;

       In Lauro v. Charles, after arresting and charging Lauro as a criminal defendant, the

police handcuffed Lauro, walked him outside the station house, drove him around the block in an

unmarked police car, and walked Lauro back to the police station so that a television news crew

could film him in a staged “perp walk.” 219 F.3d 202, 203 (2d Cir.2000). Lauro’s civil suit

against the police department and police officers asserted that the perp walk was an unreasonable

seizure that violated his privacy rights under the Fourth Amendment. Id. at 208.

       Lauro stated that Fourth Amendment privacy protection applies to a person not only

during an initial search and seizure by police, but also to persons lawfully in police custody. Id.

at 212. Lauro was lawfully under arrest. But with regard to the “perp walk” occurring after

arrest, viewing that “seizure” either as (1) a separate seizure occurring when Lauro was forcibly

removed from the police station, paraded before news cameras, and then returned to the station,

or (2) as “a continuation and aggravation of the seizure that occurred when he was arrested,” the

Fourth Amendment required that seizure to be reasonable. Id.; see also Lauro v. City of New

York, 39 F. Supp. 2d 351, 363, fn. 8 (S.D.N.Y.1999), reversed and remanded by Lauro, 219 F.3d

202. The Second Circuit concluded that the staged perp walk was unrelated to the object of the

arrest, had no legitimate law enforcement justification, and invaded Lauro’s privacy to no

purpose, and therefore violated the Fourth Amendment. 219 F.3d at 213.

       In a crucial passage of the opinion, the Court ruled:

             Despite its adverse effects on Lauro’s dignity and privacy, the perp walk
         might nevertheless have been reasonable under the Fourth Amendment, had it
         been sufficiently closely related to a legitimate governmental objective… In


                                                158
        this respect, Charles argues that “the importance of the press in informing the
        general public about the administration of criminal justice has long been
        recognized by the Supreme Court,” …., and that this interest suffices to justify
        the perp walk before us.

             The Supreme Court, however, in [Wilson v. Layne, 526 U.S. 603 (1999)]
        explicitly rejected an identical argument when it was proffered in support of
        the media ride-along in that case. The defendants had asserted three
        justifications for the presence of the media: (1) that officers should have
        discretion to decide when it would further the mission of law enforcement to
        allow media presence; (2) that accurate reporting on law enforcement activities
        was a significant interest; and (3) that the presence of media could deter law
        enforcement abuses. See [id. at 612-13]. The Court found that none of these
        justifications was sufficient to overcome the Wilsons’ Fourth Amendment
        rights, noting, “[w]ere such generalized ‘law enforcement objectives’
        themselves sufficient to trump the Fourth Amendment, the protections
        guaranteed by that Amendment’s text would be significantly watered down.”
        Id. at 612 …. Moreover, with respect to the argument that the ride-along
        served to inform the public about the administration of law enforcement, the
        Court commented:

            There is certainly language in our opinions interpreting the First
        Amendment which points to the importance of “the press” in informing the
        general public about the administration of criminal justice.... But the Fourth
        Amendment also protects a very important right, and in the present case it is in
        terms of that right that the media ride-alongs must be judged...

            [T]he need for accurate reporting on police issues in general bears no
        direct relation to the constitutional justification for the police intrusion into a
        home in order to execute a felony arrest warrant. Id. at 612-13[.].

Lauro, 219 F.3d at 212-13.

       Wilson, along with Ayeni v. Mottola, 35 F.3d 680 (2d Cir.1994), both involved media

videos of a suspect taken in the suspect’s home. But Lauro concluded that:

        It is not the case, however, that the holdings in Ayeni and Wilson turned solely
        on the special status of the home, or that the Fourth Amendment’s privacy
        protections end at the door of one’s house. Our court recognized, rather, that
        the privacy interests threatened in Ayeni went beyond those that attach to the
        inviolability of the home, and concluded that obtaining and broadcasting
        images of the Ayenis in a humiliating situation itself infringed on their Fourth
        Amendment privacy rights…. In doing so, Ayeni was consistent with long-
        standing Fourth Amendment jurisprudence, which has declined to set spatial
        boundaries on the rights protected by that amendment. For, as the Supreme


                                                159
         Court has famously stated, “the Fourth Amendment protects people, not
         places.” Katz v. United States, 389 U.S. 347, 351 [(1967)].

Lauro, 219 F.3d at 211 (emphasis added).

       In the district court, the defendant in Lauro argued that infringement on the plaintiff’s

privacy rights was justified by the importance of the press in informing the general public about

the administration of criminal justice. The district court disagreed, holding that “this Court is

convinced beyond doubt that the perp walk procedure is not designed nor intended for the

purpose of information dissemination, but rather for the purposes of incident dramatization and

arrestee humiliation.” Lauro v. City of New York, 39 F. Supp. 2d at 364, n. 9.

       On appeal, the defendant again raised this argument, and the Second Circuit also rejected

it, but on the ground that Wilson established the important Fourth Amendment principle that “the

reasonableness of the police’s actions in conducting a search or seizure must be judged, in part,

through an assessment of the degree to which those actions further the legitimate law

enforcement purposes behind the search or seizure.” Lauro, 219 F.3d at 211 (emphasis added).

The capture of the suspect’s image in Lauro served no such interest because “in staging the perp

walk, Detective Charles engaged in conduct that was unrelated to the object of the arrest, that

had no legitimate law enforcement justification, and that invaded Lauro’s privacy to no purpose.

By exacerbating Lauro’s seizure in an unreasonable manner, Charles violated the Fourth

Amendment.” Id. at 213. See also State v. Tiedemann, 2007 UT 49, ¶ 34, 162 P.3d 1106 (“Utah’s

search and seizure provisions (which are identical to those in the federal constitution) provide ‘a

greater expectation of privacy than the Fourth Amendment as interpreted by the United States

Supreme Court.’”) (quoting Brigham City v. Stuart, 2005 UT 13, ¶ 11, 122 P.3d 506 (“article I,

section 14 of the Utah Constitution provides a greater expectation of privacy than the Fourth

Amendment as interpreted by the United States Supreme Court.”)).


                                                160
       Similarly, in this case, allowing the media to film and broadcast to the world close-up

images of Mr. Robinson without his consent and while he is being held without bail pursuant to

his initial arrest violates his Fourth Amendment and article I, section 14 right to privacy because

“obtaining and broadcasting images of [him] in a humiliating situation itself infringe[s] on [his]

Fourth Amendment privacy rights” and such conduct is unrelated to the object of the arrest and

served no legitimate law enforcement justification. See also Houchins v. KQED, Inc., 438 U.S. 1,

5 n.2 (1978) (plurality opinion) (“Inmates in jails, prisons, or mental institutions retain certain

fundamental rights of privacy; they are not like animals in a zoo to be filmed and photographed

at will by the public or by media reporters, however ‘educational’ the process may be for

others.”); Demery v. Arpaio, 378 F.3d 1020, 1028-29, 1031 (9th Cir. 2004) (holding that

streaming live images of pretrial detainees to internet was not reasonably related to a non-

punitive purpose, and thus, violated the Fourteenth Amendment) (“[W]e fail to see how turning

pretrial detainees into the unwilling objects of the latest reality show serves any …legitimate

goals…. [T]the otherwise valid governmental interest in assuring accountability and public

scrutiny cannot justify broad public exposure of pretrial detainees’ intimate circumstances, as

none of the purposes justifying pretrial detention are served thereby. The Sheriff’s policy is all

the more troubling because displaying images of the County’s pretrial detainees to internet users

from around the world is not rationally connected to goals associated with educating the citizenry

of Maricopa County.”).

       In words of Justice Clark in Estes,

         [W]e cannot ignore the impact of courtroom television on the defendant. Its
         presence is a form of mental—if not physical—harassment, resembling a
         police line-up or the third degree. The inevitable close-ups of his gestures and
         expressions during the ordeal of his trial might well transgress his personal
         sensibilities, his dignity, and his ability to concentrate on the proceedings
         before him—sometimes the difference between life and death—


                                                 161
         dispassionately, freely and without the distraction of wide public surveillance.
         A defendant on trial for a specific crime is entitled to his day in court, not in a
         stadium, or a city or nationwide arena. The heightened public clamor resulting
         from radio and television coverage will inevitably result in prejudice.

381 U.S. at 549.

        Finally, just as Mr. Robinson retains a Fourth Amendment and article I, section 14

privacy right not to be photographed or filmed without his consent so too does he and other trial

participants retain such a right under the state and federal constitutional right to privacy and

under general principles of law establishing the right to privacy for all persons. See generally,

Deseret News Publishing, 2008 UT 26, ¶ 13 (“[T]the right of individual privacy concerning

personal information acquired by governmental entities [is] entitled to constitutional

protection.”]; Stien v. Marriott Ownership Resorts, Inc., 944 P.2d 374, 377-78 (Utah Ct. App.

1997) (recognizing a common law right to privacy which is violated by “(1) intrusion upon the

plaintiff’s seclusion or solitude, or into plaintiff’s private affairs, (2) appropriation, for the

defendant’s advantage, of the plaintiff’s name or likeness, (3) public disclosure of embarrassing

private facts about the plaintiff, and (4) publicity which places the plaintiff in a false light in the

public eye.”); Shattuck-Owen v. Snowbird Corp., 2000 UT 94, ¶ 11, 16 P.3d 555 (“We adopt the

standard set forth in Stien.”); Mau v. Rio Grande Oil, 28 F. Supp. 845, 846 (N.D. Cal. 1939)

(“The right of privacy, conceding it to exist, is a purely personal one, that is, it is a right of each

individual to be let alone, or not to be dragged into publicity.”(internal quotation omitted)).

        In this case where the prosecution is seeking the death penalty, Mr. Robinson’s parents

and many other persons, including young students, will be witnesses, either in the guilt phase of

the case, or, if there is a conviction of aggravated murder, at a penalty phase. Penalty phase trials

inevitably involve the disclosure of intimate and sometimes embarrassing details about the

family background and history of the accused and his family, and about the victim’s family. As


                                                   162
indicated

Doubtless many other witnesses will also object to being filmed, but unfortunately Rule 4-401.01

does not provide a notice provision or other mechanism for bringing those objections to the

attention of the Court.

       As a general matter, however, what United States Court of Appeal Chief Judge Edward

Becker said about this issue in explaining to the Senate in September 2000 why the Judicial

Conference of the United States continues to oppose cameras in the courtroom rings true:

              Broadcasting of trials presents many of the same concerns about privacy as
         does the indiscriminate dissemination of information on the Internet that was
         once only available at the courthouse. Witnesses and counsel frequently
         discuss very sensitive information during the course of a trial. Often this
         information relates to individuals who are not even parties to the case, but
         about whom personal information may be revealed. Also, in many criminal and
         civil trials, which the media would most likely be interested in televising,
         much of the evidence introduced may be of an extremely private nature,
         revealing family relationships and personal facts, including medical and
         financial information. This type of information provided in open court, is
         already available to the public through the media. Televising these matters
         sensationalizes these details for no apparent good reason.

              Involvement in a federal case can have a deep and long-lasting impact on
         all its participants, most of whom have neither asked for nor sought publicity.
         In this adversarial setting, reputations can be compromised and relationships
         can be damaged. In fact, according to the [1994] FJC study on live courtroom
         media coverage, 56% of the participating judges felt that electronic media
         coverage violates a witness’s privacy. This is not to say that the Conference
         advocates closed trials; far from it. Nevertheless, there is a common-sense
         distinction between a public trial in a public courtroom—typically filled with
         individuals with a real interest in the case—and its elevation to an event that
         allows and encourages thousands to become involved intimately in a case that
         essentially concerns a small group of private people or entities.

             The issue of privacy rights is one that has not been adequately considered
         or addressed by those who would advocate the broadcasting of trials. This
         heightened awareness of and concern for privacy rights is a relatively new and
         important development that further supports the position of the Judicial
         Conference to prohibit the use of cameras in the courtroom.




                                               163
Statement of Judge Becker on behalf of the Judicial Conference of the United States, Hrg. 106-

1029 (Allowing Cameras And Electronic Media In The Courtroom) (Sep. 6, 2000), reprinted in

Perry v. City & Cnty. of San Francisco, No. 10-16696, 2011 WL 2419868, at *78-79 (9th Cir.

Apr. 27, 2011).

       Because Mr. Robinson and other trial participants have important privacy rights that

would be violated by filming them without their consent this factor weighs heavily against

permitting continued electronic media coverage in the circumstances of this case.

       (v)   whether electronic media coverage will create adverse effects greater than
             those caused by media coverage without recording or transmitting images or
             sound;

       This issue has been empirically addressed in several studies which are reviewed in the

attached declaration of Dr. Christine L. Ruva, PH.D. See Ex.2 ¶¶ 20-24. As she concludes in

paragraph 24:

        Collectively, these studies on modality effect of information presentation
        demonstrate that visual and multimedia evidence pose a substantially greater
        risk of prejudicing jurors than auditory evidence or print formats. Visual
        formats—especially graphic images, video re-creations, and televised
        coverage—amplify emotional responses, increase perceptions of guilt, and
        alter liability assessments, while auditory evidence exerts minimal biasing
        effect. Moreover, jurors often fail to recognize their susceptibility to these
        influences, and judicial instructions have proven largely ineffective in
        mitigating such biases. These findings strongly support the need for careful
        judicial scrutiny of highly visual or emotionally charged evidence and
        extensive pretrial publicity to preserve the defendant’s constitutional right to a
        fair trial.

Accord, Mary Angela Bock, Framing the Accused: The Perp Walk as Media Ritual, 22 VISUAL

COMM. QUARTERLY 2016 206, 218 (2015), https://doi.org/10.1080/15551393.2015.1105104

(“Experimental research has shown that when in conflict, visual messages trump the linguistic,

which means that when coupled with video of a perp walk, the word ‘alleged’ may be

meaningless.”).


                                               164
       As this Court found, “[p]hotographs and visual recordings of Mr. Robinson in jail

clothing will likely be shared and disseminated in various public platforms” and “there is a

strong likelihood that a substantial number of potential jurors in Utah County will view these

images and may associate Mr. Robinson’s jail clothing with indicia of guilt.” Dkt. 137, at 5. The

Court also found that “[v]isual recordings and photographs of Mr. Robinson shackled may give

the impression that he is dangerous and may undermine his presumption of innocence.” Id. at 6.

Critically, in reference to the present factor, the Court also found that the “danger of prejudice is

compounded by technological advancements that allow the images to be manipulated in ways

that do not accurately reflect the truth of the court proceedings.” Id. at 5. Although the Court has

resolved the issued of jail attire, the problem of shackles remains, and as the Court correctly

perceived, if the Court allows capturing of images in the courtroom there is nothing to prevent

the creation of false images such as the following:




See supra, CYPHER CRIMES, Charlie Kirk Murder.




                                                 165
Unraveled Crime, Kirk Shooting: Street Clothes or Shackles? Shocking Courtroom Debate in

Tyler Robinson’s Case (YouTube, Oct. 28, 2025), (https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AL-

zTcldsYQ)

       The problem is not confined to real or fake images of Mr. Robinson in shackles, but also

exists with respect to phony lip reader, body language, or any other type of junk science

“analysis” of Mr. Robinson’s in-court demeanor, appearance, lip movement, or gestures, and

also extends to manipulation of courtroom scenes to create the impression of “court chaos”, or

“shocking” developments. And as Judge Brinkema pointed out in United States v. Moussaoui,

the worst part of the problem is that once these fake images are created, they “become eligible

for preservation by …the new generation of cameras or permanent placement on Internet web

sites and chat rooms (and) may be forever publicly known and available to anyone in the world.”

205 F.R.D. at 187. This means that a prospective juror browsing the internet six months from

now, or even a year from now, could easily come across the lip reader confession video which

was filmed in blatant violation of the Court’s decorum order, a result that would be devastating

to Mr. Robinson’s right to a fair trial. This factor, when considered in light of the extremely

damaging material that the electronic media has already created in violation of the decorum

order, and the greater likelihood of prejudice visual images create weighs heavily in favor of

prohibiting any further live streaming or court photography.




                                                166
       (vi) the adequacy of the court’s physical facilities for electronic media coverage;

       The court’s physical facilities do not seem to be large enough to accommodate electronic

media coverage without jeopardizing other interests protected by the court’s decorum orders.

       All three of the court’s decorum orders provide: “Media representatives shall use their

best efforts to avoid visually recording or photographing court employees-except for the judge-

without prior approval. This includes court security staff, clerks, and judicial assistants.” Dkt. 28,

at 5, ¶ 40; Dkt. 138, at 5, ¶ 40; Dkt. 265, at 5, ¶ 41. They also provide that “No visual recording

is permitted of a person’s lips, so as to be decipherable by a lip reader, during conferences

involving counsel at counsel tables or bench conferences with the judge.” Dkt. 28, at 5, ¶ 45;

Dkt. 138, at 5-6, ¶ 47; Dkt. 265, at 6, ¶ 48.

       The broadcast of the court appearance on September 29, 2025, which is still online today,

shows multiple clear images of court security staff, clerks, and judicial assistants. See THE SALT

LAKE TRIBUNE, Tyler Robinson, accused of killing Charlie Kirk, has second court hearing

(YouTube, Sep. 29, 2025), https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fX5LxStO_yI (3.1 million views

as of January 11, 2026).

       At the appearance on December 11, 2025, the camera was originally set up in the jury

box, which would of course not be feasible during the trial itself. The jury box was in close

proximity to the defense table and allowed the camera to zoom in on Mr. Robinson’s face, lips,

and handcuffs, and to read documents at the defense team table, resulting in the “lip reader” and

“body language” broadcasts referenced above. The close proximity of the camera to the defense

table also made it difficult for the lawyers to speak in confidence to Mr. Robinson. After it was

discovered that the media was violating the decorum order, the camera was relocated, but as

indicated above at least one person reported that Mr. Robinson’s restraints were still visible.



                                                 167
       At both camera angles, the courtroom is small enough that the camera person could have

and did pick up clear images of the court’s staff, an obvious violation of the decorum order that

creates an irreversible security risk once the live stream is posted on the internet.

       Also, at both camera angles, and indeed at any location in the small courtroom, the

camera person will still have the capability to zoom in on Mr. Robinson’s face and lips, which

means that if electronic media coverage is allowed to continue into the trial under the court’s

current decorum order, both Mr. Robinson and his lawyers will only be able to communicate

orally if they cup their hands over their lips. This will create yet another prejudicial image for the

media to zoom in on and broadcast during pretrial proceedings, which will no doubt be used by

pundits and “experts” as further evidence of Mr. Robinson’s guilt, the same conclusion that a

jury will likely draw if such conversation takes place in their presence, and this inference will be

all the more compelling to those who have viewed the New York Post’s online video of the lip

reader “confession”.

       Most importantly, for the defense the alternative to creating such a prejudicial image is to

cease having oral conversations with Mr. Robinson in the courtroom, which is both impractical

and detrimental to his Sixth Amendment right to the effective assistance of counsel.

       The court could consider eliminating the problem by modifying the decorum order to

prohibit zooming in on Mr. Robinson. As indicated above, this was attempted and then

abandoned in the Kohberger case because the media continued to zoom in on the defendant even

after being ordered not to do so. See Ex. 5. The reason that this solution did not work in

Kohberger and will not work here lies in the competitive “ethics” practiced by media video and

photo journalists. In a study of those “ethics”, it was reported based on interviews with video and

photo journalists engaged in covering criminal cases that there was a “make or break” pressure



                                                 168
on such journalists to bring back to their editors photographs or videos that could be spun as

incriminating, such a photographs of “perp walks”. See supra Bock, at 209.

       Bock notes that a guiding ethical principle “widespread among journalism scholars” is

that “news constructs rather than records reality.” Id. at 206-07 (emphasis in original). Reflecting

the cynicism of such “ethics”, she writes that “[s]hooting video of a perp walk is a work routine

for many photo- and television journalists in the U.S.”, and that “it is important to note that the

term ‘perpetrator’ automatically implies that this is the person who committed the crime; it’s not

a ‘defendant walk’” or ‘alleged perpetrator walk.’ The very name implies that a subject is

guilty.” Id. at 207. The study queried photo and video journalists about what would happen if

they came to their editors without the incriminating shot, and a couple of the photographers on a

court stakeout had this to say:

         Q: What would happen if he went back without the shot?

         His immediate response: “Aww, Christ Almighty . . . condemned to death.”
         Later, “N” continues about missing the shot: “They’re very condemning at our
         place. . . . If everybody else got it and you don’t and you were here . . . .” He
         doesn’t finish the thought with words, more of a head shake and sigh.

         Photographer #2: “Eh, what can they do? They can be a little mad. . . . It
         depends on how important it is.”

Id. at 209-10.

       Bock also reports that prior journalism studies on the topic had found that “[t]he pressure

seems as much a matter of professional pride as externally imposed by management”, and that

photo and video journalists are “so determined to do their job that they would meet the demands

of a shoot even it meant contradicting their personal ethical beliefs.” Id. at 210.

       These journalism studies help to explain why the prohibition on zooming in on the

defendant did not work in Kohberger and why the same solution would not work here. They also



                                                 169
help to explain why the overwhelming thrust of the electronic media coverage in this case has

been a prosecutorial-like attempt to incriminate Mr. Robinson through visual images of his

restraints, clothing, gestures, and demeanor, aided and abetted by the commentary of biased

lawyers, and other pundits and pseudo-experts.

       In sum, even this factor weighs in favor of granting the present motion because the size

of the courtroom, coupled with the electronic media’s track record of violating decorum orders in

this and other cases, based on a competitive “ethics” code, raises logistical and other problems

that are simply unsolvable.

       (vii) the public interest in and newsworthiness of the proceeding;

       The public interest in the proceeding in this case is fully protected by allowing the public

and all media personal to attend and report on court proceedings in any case. See Estes, 381 U.S.

at 541 (“It is true that the public has a right to be informed as to what occurs in its courts, but

reporters of all media, including television, are always present if they wish to be and are plainly

free to report whatever occurs in open court through their respective media.”) Discontinuing

electronic media live streaming would not prevent either the electronic media or the print media

from attending and reporting on the court proceedings in this case.

       According to the courts, a criminal case is newsworthy if it is either “notorious” or

“sensational.” Id. at 545 (“[W]e must remember that realistically it is only the notorious trial

which will be broadcast, because of the necessity for paid sponsorship.”); Sinclair 434 P.2d at

307 (“The public is intrigued by the sensational, and thus by crime and the facts surrounding it.

Consequently, it is but natural and perhaps necessary that the information media cater to that

appetite.”). The fallacy of the argument that sensational or notorious cases are newsworthy and




                                                 170
therefore should be televised was pointed out by Chief Justice Warren in his concurring opinion

in Estes:

            For the most part, however, the most important factor that would draw
            television to the courtroom would be the nature of the case. The alleged
            perpetrator of the sensational murder, the fallen idol, or some other person
            who, like petitioner, has attracted the public interest would find his trial turned
            into a vehicle for television. Yet, these are the very persons who encounter the
            greatest difficulty in securing an impartial trial, even without the presence of
            television. This Court would no longer be able to point to the dignity and
            calmness of the courtroom as a protection from outside influences. For the
            television camera penetrates this protection and brings into the courtroom
            tangible evidence of the widespread interest in a case—an interest which has
            often been fanned by exhaustive reports in the newspapers, television and radio
            for weeks before trial. The present case presents a clear example of this danger.

381 U.S. at 576-77 (Warren, C.J., concurring).

       As demonstrated above, five members of the Court determined in Estes and

Hollingsworth that televising a notorious trial such as the prevent one violates the due process

clause of the Fourteenth Amendment. It would turn the logic of these cases on their head and

would violate the Supremacy Clause to hold in this case that because the case is either notorious

or sensational it is thus newsworthy and should therefore be televised under a state rule.

       (viii) potentially beneficial effects of allowing public observation of the proceeding
              through electronic media coverage;

       This factor is specifically addressed by five members of the Court in Estes. Justice

Clark’s lead opinion reminds us that “[c]ourt proceedings are held for the solemn purpose of

endeavoring to ascertain the truth which is the sine qua non of a fair trial.” Estes, 381 U.S. at

540. “As has been said, the chief function of our judicial machinery is to ascertain the truth. The

use of television, however, cannot be said to contribute materially to this objective. Rather its use

amounts to the injection of an irrelevant factor into court proceedings.” Id. at 544.

“Embarrassment may impede the search for the truth, as may a natural tendency toward



                                                   171
overdramatization…. Indeed, the mere fact that the trial is to be televised might render witnesses

reluctant to appear and thereby impede the trial as well as the discovery of the truth.” Id. at 547.

“The judge decided that the trial proceedings would be telecast. He announced no restrictions at

the time. This emphasized the notorious nature of the coming trial, increased the intensity of the

publicity on the petitioner and together with the subsequent televising of the trial beginning 30

days later inherently prevented a sober search for the truth.” Id. at 550-51.

       Chief Justice Warren, joined by Justices Douglas and Goldberg, agreed that “the purpose

of trial [is to serve] as a vehicle for discovering the truth.” They further agreed that

         The present record provides ample support for scholars who have claimed that
         awareness that a trial is being televised to a vast, but unseen audience, is bound
         to increase nervousness and tension, cause an increased concern about
         appearances, and bring to the surface latent opportunism that the traditional
         dignity of the courtroom would discourage. Whether they do so consciously or
         subconsciously, all trial participants act differently in the presence of television
         cameras. And, even if all participants make a conscientious and studied effort
         to be unaffected by the presence of television, this effort in itself prevents them
         from giving their full attention to their proper functions at trial. Thus, the evil
         of televised trials, as demonstrated by this case, lies not in the noise and
         appearance of the cameras, but in the trial participants’ awareness that they are
         being televised. To the extent that television has such an inevitable impact it
         undercuts the reliability of the trial process.

Id. at 569-70 (footnote omitted).

       They also agreed with Justice Clark’s basic point that far from serving some educational

or other legitimate purpose, the use of television in a courtroom “amounts to the injection of an

irrelevant factor into court proceedings.” Chief Justice Warren wrote:

         It is argued that television not only entertains but also educates the public. But
         the function of a trial is not to provide an educational experience; and there is a
         serious danger that any attempt to use a trial as an educational tool will both
         divert it from its proper purpose and lead to suspicions concerning the integrity
         of the trial process. The Soviet Union’s trial of Francis Gary Powers provides
         an example in point. The integrity of the trial was suspect because it was
         concerned not only with determining the guilt of the individual on trial but also
         with providing an object lesson to the public. This divided effort undercut


                                                 172
         confidence in the guilt-determining aspect of the procedure and by so doing
         rendered the educational aspect self-defeating.

Id. at 575.

        In Justice Harlan’s controlling concurring opinion in Estes, he was even more emphatic

on this point:

              The arguments advanced against the constitutional banning of televised
         trials seem to me peculiarly unpersuasive. It is said that the pictorial
         broadcasting of trials will serve to educate the public as to the nature of the
         judicial process. Whatever force such arguments might have in run-of-the-mill
         cases, they carry little weight in cases of the sort before us, where the public’s
         interest in viewing the trial is likely to be engendered more by curiosity about
         the personality of the well-known figure who is the defendant (as here), or
         about famous witnesses or lawyers who will appear on the television screen, or
         about the details of the particular crime involved, than by innate curiosity to
         learn about the workings of the judicial process itself. Indeed it would be naive
         not to suppose that it would be largely such factors that would qualify a trial
         for commercial television ‘billing,’ and it is precisely that kind of case where
         the risks of permitting television coverage of the proceedings are at their
         greatest.

              It is also asserted that televised trials will cause witnesses to be more
         truthful, and jurors, judges, and lawyers more diligent. To say the least this
         argument is sophistic, for it is impossible to believe that the reliability of a trial
         as a method of finding facts and determining guilt or innocence increases in
         relation to the size of the crowd which is watching it. Attendance by interested
         spectators in the courtroom will fully satisfy the safeguards of ‘public trial.’
         Once openness is thus assured, the addition of masses of spectators would, I
         venture to say, detract rather than add to the reliability of the process. See Cox
         v. State of Louisiana, 379 U.S. 559, 562 [(1965)]. A trial in Yankee Stadium,
         even if the crowd sat in stony silence, would be a substantially different affair
         from a trial in a traditional courtroom under traditional conditions, and the
         difference would not, I think, be that the witnesses, lawyers, judges, and jurors
         in the stadium would be more truthful, diligent, and capable of reliably finding
         facts and determining guilt or innocence. There will be no disagreement, I am
         sure, among those competent to judge that precisely the opposite would likely
         be the case.

381 U.S. at 594-95 (Harlan, J., concurring).

        Five members of the Supreme Court could not have been clearer that the function of a

trial is to ascertain the truth, “not to provide an educational experience”, and that at best


                                                  173
televising the proceedings introduces an “irrelevant factor” into court proceedings and at worst it

impedes the search for truth, especially in a notorious case. Given the clarity and sweeping

language used by the Court it is disappointing, yet perhaps understandable that a “committee

…made up of judges, courtroom administrators and media attorneys”42 would write a Final

Report that contained a solitary and greatly watered-down description of the significance of

Estes:

           The United States Supreme Court weighed in on the debate in 1965 and again
           in 1981, ultimately recognizing the right of states to allow such coverage. In
           Estes v.Texas, 381 U.S. 532 (1965), the Supreme Court overturned the
           conviction of Billy Sol Estes, holding that coverage of the trial, which included
           some use of cameras, violated Estes’ due process rights. Four justices of the
           five member majority found that televising trials, at least under then-existing
           technology, was inherently unconstitutional. The fifth justice took a narrower
           view based on the specific circumstances of the Estes case and suggested that
           technological advancements might one day lead to a different result.

Final Report, at 9-10.

          What about the significance of the Court’s agreement on the principles quoted above?

The Report never says. What about the significance of Hollingsworth? The Report never

mentions it.

          Instead, the Report declares as an ipse dixit that “[p]ermitting electronic media coverage

will allow the public to actually see and hear what transpires in the courtroom, and to become

better educated and informed about the work of the courts.” Final Report, at 11. However, even

putting aside the fact that this statement is directly contrary to Estes, the premise of this assertion

is that the public would actually view lengthy live stream coverage to get “better educated about

the work of the courts.” Whatever the situation in the pre-social media days of 2012, the reality

today is that people in every state, including Utah, get their news from social media and other


42
     See supra, n. 35.

                                                  174
online media outlets, not by watching gavel-to-gavel coverage on television. See, e.g., Art

Raymond, Here’s where, and how, Utahns are getting their news, DESERET NEWS (Dec. 13,

2022, at 7:44 PM MT), https://www.deseret.com/utah/2022/12/13/23496469/how-do-utahns-get-

news-digital-devices-trusted-sources (statewide pool demonstrated that “[w]hen it comes to how

Utahns are consuming their news, a vast majority [91 %] say digital devices are their No. 1 go-to

and more than half report they rarely or never get news from television, radio or print

publications.”); 2024 Policy Issues Survey February 2024 Likely Voter Poll, SUTHERLAND

INSTITUTE (2024) https://utahnewsdispatch.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/05/Election-Trust-

Forum-VBM-survey-presentation.pdf (statewide poll of Utah voters indicating that only 19% of

those polled listed television as their most preferred media type for receiving news). What these

numbers mean is that most Utahns have been and will continue to be receiving their diet of

“news” about this case not from television live streaming of the proceedings, but by a stew of

online interpretations and repackaging of unrepresentative samples of that live streaming, mixed

in with a large dose of unverified and often unreliable hearsay, served up for consumption by

what the State has accurately described in its pleadings as “pundits and other attention seekers

with varying levels of mental stability”(Dkt. 109, at 9 (Classified Private)) and by what the

Governor has accurately described in his Meet the Press appearance as internet and social media

“conflict entrepreneurs.”

       That reality helps to explain the results of the study cited above that found that 100% of a

sample of social media postings in this case involved conspiracy theories or political agendas. It

is those false theories, not the workings of our courts, that the public is being “educated” about.

       That reality also helps to explain a recent poll conducted by KUTV. See Wendy Hollaran,

Gov. Cox questioned on accusations of prejudicing accused assassin Tyler Robinson’s case,



                                                175
KUTV (Nov. 25, 2025 at 8:27 PM), https://kutv.com/news/2news-investigates/do-you-think-

tyler-robinson-can-receive-an-impartial-trial-in-utah.

       The poll results were as follows:




       Where could 42% of those polled have come up with the idea that it would be “hard to

avoid bias after public comments”, and another 11% come up with the idea that Mr. Robinson

could not receive a fair trial in Utah County? It could not have been through watching the few

live streamed proceedings televised to date, where this Court has stressed from the outset that it

is committed to assuring Mr. Robinson a fair trial. See Dkt. 239, at 8-9. The only explanation for

such numbers is that a substantial number of the public is getting “educated” (i.e., biased) from

other sources.




                                                176
       It is also significant that the 2012 Final Report conveniently ignored an important part of

the 1994 Federal Report, although the 2012 Final Report claimed was supportive of its

conclusions. The 1994 Federal Report contained a content analysis study of broadcasted stories

in the pilot program that revealed that the stories did “a poor job of providing viewers with detail

about the legal process.” 2012 Final Report, Tab 3 at 119 (emphasis added).

       Chief Judge Becker, speaking on behalf of the Federal Judicial Conference, described this

aspect of the study in his statement and testimony before a Senate Hearing on allowing cameras

in the courtroom on September 6, 2000:

             V. The Putative Educational Benefit of Cameras in the Courtroom

             The proponents of cameras in the courtroom rely, of course, on the
         putative benefits of public education and understanding of court processes. The
         Judicial Conference supports that goal but does not agree that cameras in
         courtrooms will significantly further it. The FJC study analyzed the results
         achieved during the pilot project. The main approach to the issue lay in a
         content analysis of evening news broadcast using footage obtained during the
         pilot program. The content analysis is disquieting. The ninety stories analyzed
         presented a total of one hour and twenty-five minutes of courtroom footage,
         with an average of fifty-six seconds of courtroom footage per story. There is
         not too much educational content in 56 seconds. Moreover, most of the
         courtroom footage was voiced over by a reporter’s narration. On average,
         reporters narrated 63% of all courtroom footage. Thus, the witnesses, parties,
         and attorneys spoke on camera for just over one-third of the total air time. In at
         least one-half of the cases photographed, information on the nature of the case
         was provided by reporters or anchors without relying on the participants.

              The FJC report also sought to determine specifically the extent to which
         the stories provided basic educational information about the legal system,
         examining whether five pieces of information were conveyed to the viewer: (1)
         identification of the case as a civil matter; (2) identification of the type or
         proceeding, such as a hearing or trial; (3) statements about whether a jury was
         present; (4) descriptions of the proceedings on a given day; and (5) discussion
         of the next step in the legal process. The report concluded as follows:

              The vast majority of stories (95% of non-first day stories) did not identify
         the proceeding covered as a civil matter. In addition, 77% of the stories failed
         to identify the type of proceeding involved. Almost three-quarters (74%) of all



                                                177
        stories did not provide information about whether a jury was present, including
        half of the stories that identified the covered proceedings as a trial.

            Most stories (74%) did explain what transpired in court on a particular day,
        such as who testified or what evidence was presented. In multiple-day cases,
        90% of the stories explained the daily proceedings, compared to 63% in single-
        day stories. Seventy-six percent of the daily proceedings in a story were
        explained by a combination of reporter narration and participant discussion.
        Only 29% of stories mentioned the next step in the litigation process in the
        case.

            Thus, the stories did not provide a high level of detail about the legal
        process in the cases covered. In addition, the analysis revealed that increasing
        the proportion of courtroom footage used in a story did not significantly
        increase the information given about the legal process.

            In view of the foregoing, we suggest that the benefits of televised coverage
        of courtroom proceedings are overrated (and are certainly far outweighed by
        the detriments described above). Television news coverage oftentimes appears
        simply to use the courtroom for a backdrop or a visual image for the news
        story which, like many of such stories on television, are delivered in short
        sound bites and not in depth.

             The FJC study also reported that Court TV covered 28 cases under the
        program and that C-SPAN covered 7 cases. However, it does not appear from
        records available to us that these proceedings were broadcast either in their
        entirety or continuously. The paucity of cases selected by C-SPAN—seven in
        two years—suggests that the tediousness, technicality, and sheer length of
        trials are obstacles to comprehensive media transmission, except in the
        sensational kinds of cases where the harms described previously are the
        greatest.

Perry, 2011 WL 2419868, at *82-83.43

       The findings of the FJC study severely undermine the “educational benefit” justification

normally given for allowing such technology in courts. Additionally, the study found that



43
   The hearing transcript at which Chief Judge Becker and many others testified on September 6,
2000 was readily available in 2012 both through the Perry opinion cited above and on the
internet. See Allowing Cameras And Electronic Media In The Courtroom: Hearing on S.721
Before the S. Comm. On the Judiciary, 106th Cong.1029 (2000),
https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-106shrg73484/html/CHRG-106shrg73484.htm. It
is curious that neither this 2000 hearing transcript nor the FJC study is mentioned anywhere in
the 2012 Final Report, yet the report attaches as an exhibit a 1996 article by a media lawyer.

                                               178
“plaintiffs and their attorneys received more air time than defendants and their attorneys.” 2012

Final Report, Tab 3, at 119. The lack of balance in covering the parties of litigation necessarily

presented a biased, incomplete account of the trial.

       It is therefore not true that “[p]ermitting electronic media coverage will allow the public

to actually see and hear what transpires in the courtroom, and to become better educated and

informed about the work of the courts.” Id. at 11. That result has no chance of happening unless

the Court closes the spigot being used by pundits and the internet and social media “conflict

entrepreneurs” to generate their false content. Cf. Sheppard, 384 U.S. at 361-62 (explaining that

as a means of addressing and averting harm to the criminal justice process inflicted by

prejudicial pretrial publicity, the trial court should have “proscribed extrajudicial statements by

any lawyer, party, witness, or court official which divulged prejudicial matters” and that had the

trial court done so, “the news media would have soon learned to be content with the task of

reporting the case as it unfolded in the courtroom—not pieced together from extrajudicial

statements.”).

       (ix) any other factor affecting the fair administration of justice.

       There are a number of considerations that the Court should consider under the fair

administration of justice factor that all weigh in favor of granting the instant motion.

       First, as referenced above, it appears that the State agrees with the defense that the Court

should not permit further electronic media live streaming or photographic coverage. The positive

or negative effects of cameras depend on the facts and circumstances of each case. The parties,

who are familiar with the witnesses who will testify, the evidence that will be offered, and other

facts that might indicate the potential for prejudice, are in the best position to determine the

appropriateness of cameras. It is also true, as the judge stated in the Kohberger case, that



                                                 179
“[a]greement between the State and Defense on any issue in a capital case is rare, [which should]

further confirm[ ] to the Court the legitimacy and level of concern counsel have raised.” Ex. 5 at

6 (quotation omitted).

       Second, as was concluded in United States v. Trump,

         Messages designed to generate alarm and dread, and to trigger extraordinary
         safety precautions, will necessarily hinder the trial process and slow the
         administration of justice. For example, trial personnel and participants will be
         distracted or delayed by objectively reasonable concerns about their safety and
         that of their family members, as well as by having to devote time and resources
         to adopting safety measures or working with investigators.

88 F.4th at 1014.

       This no mere general concern in this case where the inflammatory rhetoric of the

President and his supporters were specifically designed to and have had the effect of

“generat[ing] alarm and dread, and [of] trigger[ing] extraordinary safety precautions.” See also

Ex. 5, at 7 (“Further, the duty on the bailiffs within the courtroom to ensure a safe and secure

environment is being eclipsed by the burden to monitor media and the public who may be audio

recording, videoing, or photographing the proceedings.”).

       A related concern is that the Court and both the defense and prosecution teams have

already had to devote a significant amount of their time and resources to litigating issues related

to the electronic media’s insistence on not only attendings court proceedings, but on live

streaming them. Given the number of lawyers who have appeared in this case on behalf of the

media, the constant need to be on the lookout for decorum order violations, and the reality that

there will be future developments that will no doubt necessitate revisiting any order allowing

electronic media coverage, there is no end in sight as to the need to pursue what is essentially

collateral litigation far removed from what should be the focus of the Court and both teams in

their representation of their clients in this death penalty case. See Trump, 88 F.4th at 1014


                                                180
(“requiring a court to conduct a mini-trial on that inquiry while readying a high-profile case for

trial would itself divert and delay the criminal justice process.”).



                                          CONCLUSION

       Based upon the foregoing, this Court should enter an order pursuant to Utah Rule of

Judicial Administration Rule 4-401.01 and the constitutional provisions cited above that

prohibits or restricts electronic media coverage in this cases because: (1) such action is necessary

to preserve and protect the constitutional provisions set forth above; (2) under Rule 4-401.01,

further electronic media coverage should be prohibited or restricted because (a) the electronic

media coverage in this case has not been and will not in the future be for the predominant

purpose of journalism or dissemination of news to the public (Rule 4-401.01(2)(A)); (b) the

reasons for doing so are sufficiently compelling to outweigh any presumption that electronic

media coverage by a news reporter shall be permitted (Rule 4-401.01(2)(A); (c) a judge may

terminate or suspend electronic media coverage at any time without prior notice if the judge

finds that continued electronic media coverage is no longer appropriate based upon consideration

of one or more of the factors in Paragraph (2)(B) (Rule 4-401.01(3)(B) ; and (d) the electronic

media has violated this Court’s decorum order so prohibition or restrict of electronic media

coverage is warranted under Rule 4-401.01(5).

                      DECLARATION OF CHRISTINE L. RUVA, PH.D.

       I, Christine L. Ruva, declare as follows:

       1)      I am a Professor of Psychology and Sarasota-Manatee campus chair of

psychology at the University of South Florida. I earned my Ph.D. in Psychology from the

University of South Florida in the area of Cognitive and Neural Sciences, where I focused on the

application of cognitive psychology to the legal system. For over 25 years I have explored the

effects of pre-trial publicity on jurors’ perceptions, emotions, memories, interpretation of trial

evidence, deliberation behavior, and verdicts. My research also explores how individual

differences of jurors and defendants influence jurors’ perceptions, deliberation behaviors, and

verdicts. My research also explores the effectiveness of remedies available to the courts for

reducing juror bias (e.g., deliberation, expert testimony, jury instructions, voir dire, and implicit

bias remedies). I utilize both quantitative and qualitative (i.e., content analysis of jury

deliberations) methods. My research has been published in a variety of high-impact psychology

and criminology journals including the following: Criminal Justice and Behavior; Journal of

Experimental Psychology-Applied; Law and Human Behavior; Psychology, Crime and Law; and

Psychology, Public Policy, and Law. My full Curriculum Vitae is attached as Appendix 1.

       2)      I have been asked by counsel for Mr. Robinson to review, evaluate, and update the

empirical research discussed in the Final Report by the Utah Judicial Council Study Committee

on Technology (April 10, 2012),

https://legacy.utcourts.gov/committees/Tech_in_Courtrooms/2012.03.23%20Final%20Report.pdf

which recommended adoption of what is now UT R JUD ADMIN Rule 4-401.01 (Electronic

Media Coverage of Court Proceedings). I have also been asked to summarize any empirical

research which bears on the various factors cited in that Rule, which requires that when



                                                                                          Page 2 of 36
determining whether the presumption of electronic media coverage has been overcome and

whether such coverage should be prohibited or restricted beyond the limitations in this rule, a

judge shall consider some or all of the following factors:

        (i) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will prejudice

        the right of the parties to a fair proceeding;

        (ii) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will

        jeopardize the safety or well-being of any individual;

        (iii) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will

        jeopardize the interests or well-being of a minor;

        (iv) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will

        constitute an unwarranted invasion of personal privacy of any person;

        (v) whether electronic media coverage will create adverse effects greater than those

        caused by media coverage without recording or transmitting images or sound;

        (vi) the adequacy of the court's physical facilities for electronic media coverage;

        (vii) the public interest in and newsworthiness of the proceeding;

        (viii) potentially beneficial effects of allowing public observation of the proceeding

        through electronic media coverage; and

        (ix) any other factor affecting the fair administration of justice.

(Rule 4-401.01(2)(B).)

                                 I. SUMMARY OF RESEARCH

        3)      Decades of research by cognitive psychologists into the causes and impacts of

bias, as well as individuals’ ability to self-identify bias, establish several empirical findings that




                                                                                          Page 3 of 36
bear on the factors listed in Rule 4-401.01(2)(B) in cases where pervasive and inflammatory pre-

trial publicity (PTP) has occurred.

        4)      First, pervasive and inflammatory pre-trial publicity, especially live streaming of

all proceedings, can lead to deep-seated bias. This bias is not merely an affinity for the victim or

aversion to the defendant. It is cognitive, and as such, it fundamentally affects how jurors will

process evidence during the trial and deliberate in the jury room.

        5)      Second, these effects are heightened in cases involving notorious crimes such as

alleged assassinations, terrorism, or violence against children. And, in today’s world of pervasive

social media and live streaming of all proceedings, where information is ubiquitous, is pinpoint-

tailored to the fears and beliefs of the reader and viewer, and may well be inaccurate, the impact

of pre-trial publicity is especially acute.

        6)      Third, cognitive bias can be unconscious, making it difficult even for the most

self-aware and well-intentioned potential juror to identify and self-report in response to general

questions about bias. In fact, individuals with unconscious bias may be unduly confident in their

ability to disregard pre-trial publicity and judge impartially. Once seated, it can be difficult or

impossible for jurors to set this bias aside.

        7)      In short, scientific research into bias confirms what logic and good sense suggest:

merely questioning potential jurors about their exposure to and unconscious bias created by

pervasive and inflammatory pre-trial publicity will not be sufficient to eliminate such bias. While

extended individual questioning of jurors to elicit bias in high publicity cases is certainly




                                                                                          Page 4 of 36
preferable to group questioning, the fact remains that unconscious bias caused by such publicity

remains largely intractable. 1

            II. LIVE STREAMING OF ALL PROCEEDINGS IN A NOTORIOUS
                         CRIMINAL CASE CAN CAUSE BIAS

       A. Live Streaming of Notorious Crimes Can Be Pervasive, Slanted, Sensational, and
       Emotional—All of Which Can Lead to Bias.

       8)      Media coverage of notorious crimes is different. The news media, and today,

social media, cover crimes every day, and a steady drumbeat of even serious crimes makes most

criminal coverage routine. But some crimes receive sensational coverage because they involve

public figures, are especially heinous, extraordinarily violent, or threaten the community at large.

And sensational coverage, by its sheer volume and by its content, has impacts that the everyday

crime beat does not, especially when it is live streamed and then filtered through social media

and other internet outlets.

       9)      The volume of coverage in notorious cases can be overwhelming. 2 And studies

show that the quantity of pre-trial publicity to which jurors are exposed affects their level of bias.

Across studies extending back several decades, researchers have demonstrated that individuals

exhibit a greater propensity to pre-judge defendants when they are exposed to more publicity—

both as a general conclusion (i.e., showing a general propensity to prejudgment based on an

A. Yelderman, M. T. Huss, & J. A. Cantone (Eds.), The Cambridge Handbook of Psychology
and Legal Decision-Making 110 (2024)(“[W]hile extended questioning aids the identification of
jurors’ explicit biases, it is unlikely to uncover jurors’ unconscious biases or reduce juror
bias.”)(discussing the empirical studies on this issue).

percent of suspects described in crime news stories were associated with prejudicial publicity, as
defined by the American Bar Association at that time).

                                                                                         Page 5 of 36
increase in the amount of exposure 3), and in response to specific thresholds (i.e., showing that

prospective jurors exposed to at least three newspaper articles about a crime were more likely to

believe there was ample evidence against a defendant 4). This effect has been demonstrated in

both naturally occurring and controlled settings, confirming the validity of the proposition. 5

       10)     Ruva (2018) reviewed research on how the amount of pre-trial publicity

influences juror bias, concluding that its effects are cumulative. 6 DeLuca (1979) found that mock

jurors exposed to three pieces of anti-defendant information were more likely to convict than

those exposed to only one or two pieces. 7 Consistent with this, Daftary-Kapur, Penrod,

O’Connor, and Wallace (2014) reported that jurors who read a high amount of anti-defendant

PTP (ten news stories) were significantly more likely to render guilty verdicts than those exposed

to a lower amount (five stories). Conversely, greater exposure to pro-defendant PTP reduced the

likelihood of guilty verdicts compared to lower exposure. 8

        11)    Survey research corroborates these findings: as potential jurors’ reported

knowledge of a case increases, so does bias against the defendant (Costantini & King,

1980/1981 9; Moran & Cutler, 1991 10). Notably, Moran and Cutler found that increased case

knowledge did not reduce jurors’ self-reported confidence in impartiality, suggesting bias

operates outside conscious awareness. Shaffer (1986) further demonstrated that both the number

of PTP articles and respondents’ estimated exposure predicted attributions of guilt in murder

cases. 11

        12)    Collectively, these studies indicate that greater PTP exposure amplifies juror bias,

increasing the likelihood of prejudicial verdicts. This supports the Supreme Court’s recognition

of pre-trial publicity’s biasing influence and the American Bar Association’s ethical rules

addressing pervasive publicity.

        13)    Media coverage of notorious crimes is likely to be sensational and slanted against

the defendant. A 2018 content analysis of over 1,800 pre-trial newspaper articles regarding 20

California capital cases from 1979 to 2005 found that certain highly prejudicial elements recur

repeatedly in the coverage. Seventy-five percent of the articles contained some form of negative

information, and 50 percent included information that the study authors classified as

“sensationalism,” which they defined as “any description of the crime that uses extreme, heinous,

shocking, or emotional language.” Negative character statements about the suspect, such as “cop

killer” or “monster,” appeared in 32 percent of the articles. By contrast, only 19 percent of the

articles included positive statements about the suspect. 12 Similarly, a 1995 survey of news stories

about crime across 14 different newspapers over an eight–week period found that 27 percent of

the reports about a criminal suspect contained content that the ABA described in its then-most

recent Model Rules as “potentially prejudicial,” including negative statements about the

suspect’s character and guilt. 13

          14)     The rise of social media has exponentially increased the quantity and quality of

information regarding notorious crimes available to the public. And social media not only is

pervasive, it also spreads misinformation. A study in the aftermath of the Boston Marathon

Bombing showed that misinformation related to the bombing was rampant on Twitter, and

largely went uncorrected. For example, a false rumor circulated about a young girl killed while

running the marathon, accompanied by a picture of a young girl. Over 92,000 tweets about this

story followed the attacks. Of those, 90,668 (or 97.7 percent) included misinformation, and only

2,046 (or 2.2 percent) included corrections. 14 In addition, several major news sources—including

       15)     In short, the volume and nature of traditional and social media coverage of

notorious crimes greatly increases the risk that prospective jurors for these cases may already

have formed beliefs about the case—which can lead to biased decision-making. 16 “Predecisional

distortion theory” predicts that jurors will weigh trial evidence to support their pre-existing

Frisbee, Caught In The Content Tornado: How To Protect Violent-Crime Trials From The

tornado… refers to the whirlwind of fact, fiction, and opinion that emerges when violent-crime
trials are live streamed on social media. This concept describes how live streamed trials generate
emotional and moralized content that gets amplified by social media algorithms. The emotional
nature of violent-crime trials—fueled by anger, grief, and public passion—draws viewers in, and
as they engage with the content, they become more likely to produce their own secondary
content on the subject. This user-generated secondary content—in the form of videos, comments,
and posts—amplifies the tornado and mixes accurate information with misinformation,
exaggerations, and public biases.”)

CRIM. JUST. & BEHAV. 511, 526 (2011), https://doi.org/10.1177/0093854811400823.

                                                                                        Page 9 of 36
publicity weigh the evidence to support anti-defendant out-comes. A study of 30 mock-jury

deliberations found that mock jurors exposed to anti-defendant pre-trial publicity were

significantly more likely than unexposed jurors to discuss ambiguous trial evidence in a manner

that supported the prosecution; and the exposed jurors rarely interpreted new evidence in a

manner supporting the defendant. 18 Another study found that jurors exposed to pre-trial publicity

distorted witness testimony in the direction consistent with their exposure—i.e., jurors exposed

to anti-defendant pre-trial publicity demonstrated a pro-prosecution bias, while jurors exposed to

pro-defendant pre-trial publicity exhibited a pro-defense bias. 19

          16)     Research also shows that pre-trial publicity that generates emotions such as anger

and disgust has a significant impact on judgments—more so than purely factual publicity—and

notorious crimes subject to exhaustive reporting can generate substantial emotional responses. 20

A 2014 study asked over 300 participants to read press articles about a criminal trial in France

and found that (a) the news stories aroused negative emotions, and (b) reading the stories was a

predictor of adverse judgments. At the first step, the study analyzed the articles for

“incriminating” versus “crime story” information, with “incriminating” information simply

  Christine L. Ruva & Michelle A. LeVasseur, Behind Closed Doors: The Effect of Pretrial

     Ruva et al., supra note 17, at 526.

  See, e.g., Rafaële Dumas et al., Press Articles and Influence Processes: The Different Effects of
Incriminating Information and Crime Story Information on Judgments of Guilt, 20 PSYCHOL.,

P. Kramer et al., Pretrial Publicity, Judicial Remedies, and Jury Bias, 14 LAW & HUM.

problem-not-murder-anger (describing how the killing of Charlie Kirk has led not only to “the
red-hot furious kind [of anger] where your blood boils and your temperature rises” but also to “a
hardened, determined malice, a fixed hatred of the person who provoked your anger in the first
place and a determination to get your revenge, or to silence them once and for all.”)

                                                                                         Page 10 of 36
designating the defendants as having committed a crime and “crime-story” information

presenting the story of the crime, often in graphic fashion. Study participants then were assigned

to read one of the articles, rate their anger arousal, and report their judgment about the defend-

ant. While both incriminating information and crime-story information increased juror propensity

to judge the defendant as guilty, crime-story information aroused anger and corresponded with

higher guilty judgment rates. 21

          17)    Similarly, a 2003 study analyzed 50 mock jurors’ recall of factual versus

“affective” pre-trial publicity. The 50 mock jurors were naturally exposed to “extensive and

prolonged” pre-trial publicity about a fraud case and then interviewed to determine what they

remembered about the case from that publicity. Their recall was coded as either factual or

affective/ evaluative, with factual recall referring to neutral data points about the crime (e.g.,

“The defendants are accused of fraud.”) and affective recall meaning memories reported with an

emotion or opinion (e.g., “They were all in on it . . . ruthless con-artists.”). The mock jurors then

watched a highly realistic depiction of the actual trial and were interviewed throughout about

their reasoning processes, whether they thought the defendants were guilty, and how confident

they were in their verdict. The study found that those mock jurors who had affective recall of

publicity were more likely to be pro-prosecution and were more confident in finding the

defendant guilty after watching a mock trial. 22 Mock jurors who demonstrated that they recalled

purely factual information from pre-trial publicity, as opposed to more affective or emotional

information, did not show the same tendencies. Therefore, the study found that “an affective

     Dumas et al., supra note 20, at 667.

                                                                                        Page 11 of 36
response to [pre-trial publicity] appears to have greater influence on subsequent reasoning about

trial evidence and verdict judgment than does purely factual recall.” 23

          18)       Indeed, studies consistently find that jurors who have experienced anger and

disgust prior to hearing the evidence are more likely to be punitive than jurors who report less

anger. 24 Some scholars theorize that these emotions lead to a desire to place blame, which finds

an outlet in the criminal defendant. 25 Other researchers suggest that anger increases one’s sense

of certainty, leading one to consider fewer factors when forming judgments—i.e., that

individuals experiencing anger do not readily accept new information or process all information

systematically. 26

          19)       Research also shows that the passage of time does not dampen the effect of

emotional pre-trial publicity, especially in high profile cases. 27 A 1990 study found that waiting
