(finding that even when a trial took place 14 months after a publicized crime, the impact of pre-
                                                                                        Page 12 of 36
twelve days between exposure to emotional publicity and trial did not reduce the biasing effects

of that publicity, while the wait did reduce the effect of factual publicity. Nearly 800 mock jurors

were exposed to either emotional or factual publicity before watching a highly realistic mock

criminal trial. The jurors were asked to report their verdict, their confidence in their verdict, and

an estimate of the likelihood of defendant’s guilt. Some of the mock jurors deliberated

immediately after observing the trial, and others waited twelve days before deliberating. The

results showed that the negative emotions of those exposed to emotional publicity remained

relatively constant, meaning “subjects’ memory of their emotional reactions was not diluted by

the passage of several days.” 28 In fact, for individuals suffering from acute stress and trauma,

further addressed below, the correlation between trauma and anger may increase over time. 29

          20)    Finally, the modality (visual, auditory, or text) of pre-trial publicity and case

information matters and has differential effects on juror bias, emotions, and memory: Ogloff and

Vidmar (1994) conducted an juror-simulation experiment to compare the relative effects of

television and print media on juror bias in a highly publicized child sexual abuse case. 30


trial publicity was not dampened). The study analyzed the persistence of the effects of pre-trial
publicity by assessing participants’ guilt ratings two weeks before trial, during the course of the
trial, and immediately after the trial and found that pre-trial publicity effects persisted throughout
the course of the trial. See also, Imrich et al, supra n.2 at 111 (“[I]t is likely that particularly
sensational cases, singled out for media attention (eg, the 0. J. Simpson murder case), are the ones
most closely followed by the most people, particularly as such cases get closer to trial and more
lurid details become available; Gunter, Furnham, and Gietson (1984) discuss increased memory for
violent print news. Several experimental studies have found that when prejudicial information is
presented to mock jurors just prior to trial evidence, jurors are likely to be influenced.”)
28
     Kramer et al., supra note 20, at 421, 431–32.
29
  Ulrich Orth & Elias Wieland, Anger, Hostility, and Posttraumatic Stress Disorder in Trauma-
Exposed Adults: A Meta-Analysis, 74 J. CONSULTING & CLINICAL PSYCHOL. 698, 703
(2006), https://doi.apa.org/doi/10.1037/0022-006X.74.4.698.
30
  Ogloff, J. R. P., & Vidmar, N. (1994). The impact of pretrial publicity on jurors: A study to
compare the relative effects of television and print media in a child sex abuse case. Law and
Human Behavior, 18(5), 507–525. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01499171

                                                                                         Page 13 of 36
Participants were randomly assigned to receive pre-trial publicity through newspaper articles,

televised excerpts of testimony, both media types, or control condition. The study found that

exposure to television coverage produced significantly stronger emotional reactions, greater

belief in the credibility of victims’ testimony, higher perceptions of the defendant’s guilt, and

harsher sentencing recommendations than exposure to print media alone. Combined exposure to

both television and print media had the most pronounced biasing effect. Importantly, despite

these differences, participants across all conditions reported similar confidence in their ability to

remain impartial, indicating that jurors may be unaware of their own bias. These findings

underscore that vivid, visual television coverage exerts a more powerful prejudicial influence

than print media, and that cumulative exposure amplifies this effect. The authors note that such

bias likely stems from emotional arousal rather than factual differences, raising concerns about

the fairness of trials in cases involving extensive televised coverage (Ogloff & Vidmar, 1994).

       21)     Edwards and Mottarella (2014) examined the prejudicial impact of visual versus

auditory evidence on mock-juror emotion and verdict decisions in a murder case. 31 Visual

evidence conditions included graphic images, neutral images, or no images; auditory evidence

consisted of a 911 call recording; and jury instructions were either standard or revised to warn

against emotional bias. Results indicated that graphic visual evidence significantly increased

conviction rates (31.3%) compared to neutral (26.3%) or no images (19.8%), whereas auditory

evidence had no significant effect on verdicts or emotional responses. Emotional state changes

were modest and did not mediate the relationship between evidence type and verdict, suggesting

visual evidence influences decisions independently of emotional arousal. Revised jury


31
  Edwards, E. R., & Mottarella, K. E. (2014). Preserving the right to a fair trial: An examination
of prejudicial value of visual and auditory evidence. North American Journal of Psychology,
16(2), 397–414.

                                                                                       Page 14 of 36
instructions aimed at reducing emotional bias were ineffective and, in fact, increased conviction

rates. According to Edwards and Mottarella (2014), and consistent with Ogloff and Vidmar

(1994), this research suggests that when case-related photos circulate before trial (e.g., in news

sites or social feeds), the same mechanism can prime community members toward guilt

judgments, with this bias persisting independent of their conscious emotional reports. Applied to

trial recordings: when courts permit video that includes still frames or inserts of graphic visuals

(crime-scene images displayed during recorded proceedings or streamed hearings), those visuals

can create distinctive, durable memory traces that affect perceived credibility and culpability,

thereby biasing adjudicative assessments even if jurors believe they can remain impartial

(Edwards & Mottarella, 2014).

       22)     Fishfader and colleagues (1996) examined whether the mode of evidence

presentation (print transcripts, videotaped testimony, or videotaped testimony plus a video re-

creation) affects juror retention, emotional state, liability assessments, and damage awards in a

wrongful death case. 32 Videotaped formats significantly increased emotional reactions versus

print. Changes in mood were inversely correlated with liability assigned to the plaintiff (i.e.,

heightened emotion reduced perceived plaintiff responsibility), particularly in the video

re-creation condition. Yet retention scores and damage awards did not significantly differ by

format (Fishfader, Howells, Katz, & Teresi, 1996).

       23)     Basic research in psychology has explored why visual stimuli are more

memorable and impactful than auditory or text-based stimuli. A recent experiment by Ensor,

Bancroft, and Hockley (2019) investigated whether the picture-superiority effect—the robust


32
  Fishfader, V. L., Howells, G. N., Katz, R. C., & Teresi, P. S. (1996). Evidential and extralegal
factors in juror decisions: Presentation mode, retention, and level of emotionality. Law and
Human Behavior, 20(5), 565–572.

                                                                                       Page 15 of 36
finding that pictures are better remembered than words—extends to auditory stimuli and whether

this effect is driven by dual coding, physical distinctiveness, or conceptual distinctiveness. 33

Across four experiments using recognition tasks, participants studied lists of sounds and words

under various conditions, including directed-forgetting cues, cue-duration manipulations, and

labelability variations. Results consistently showed no auditory analog of the picture-superiority

effect in recognition: sounds were not better recognized than words, even when sounds were easy

to label or when additional encoding time was provided. While free-recall studies have

demonstrated an auditory picture-superiority effect, these findings suggest that recognition

memory benefits for pictures arise from conceptual distinctiveness—the deeper processing

required to identify diagnostic visual features—rather than dual coding or physical variability.

The authors conclude that conceptual distinctiveness explains the picture-superiority effect in

recognition, as auditory stimuli lack the feature-rich processing demands of pictures. These

findings imply that visual evidence may exert a stronger biasing influence on jurors than auditory

evidence because of its inherent conceptual distinctiveness (Ensor, Bancroft, & Hockley, 2019).

Applied to trial recordings or live streaming: audio-only recordings of hearings or trials should,

on average, exert less prejudicial influence on community listeners than video streams, because

they lack the diagnostic visual features that create stronger, biasing memory traces (Ensor et al.,

2019; Edwards & Mottarella, 2014).

       24)     Collectively, these studies on modality effect of information presentation

demonstrate that visual and multimedia evidence pose a substantially greater risk of prejudicing

jurors than auditory evidence or print formats. Visual formats—especially graphic images, video


33
  Ensor, T. M., Bancroft, T. D., & Hockley, W. E. (2019). Listening to the picture-superiority
effect: Evidence for the conceptual-distinctiveness account of picture superiority in recognition.
Experimental Psychology. https://doi.org/10.1027/1618-3169/a000437.

                                                                                        Page 16 of 36
re-creations, and televised coverage—amplify emotional responses, increase perceptions of guilt,

and alter liability assessments, while auditory evidence exerts minimal biasing effect. Moreover,

jurors often fail to recognize their susceptibility to these influences, and judicial instructions have

proven largely ineffective in mitigating such biases. These findings strongly support the need for

careful judicial scrutiny of highly visual or emotionally charged evidence and extensive pretrial

publicity to preserve the defendant’s constitutional right to a fair trial.

        B. Publicity of Alleged Assassinations, Terrorist Attacks, or Other Mass Acts of
        Violence May Cause Trauma and Significant Anger, Disgust, Fear, and Related Bias.

        25)     Conventional, social media, and especially live stream publicity of or about

traumatic events such as assassinations, terrorism, and other mass acts of violence can cause

trauma and emotional responses such as anger, disgust, and fear that distort decision-making and

lead to bias. Frequent viewers of stories relating to such acts risk the “abnormal consolidation of

fear conditioning that is associated with development of acute and [post-traumatic stress]

responses” and can contribute to trauma-related disorders. 34 Psychological effects in the

aftermath of such attack are “virtually certain” even if individuals are not aware they are

experiencing them. 35

        26)     This effect has been documented with respect to acts such as the Boston Marathon

Bombing. A nationwide study found that media exposure and graphic images of the bombing

affected the cognitive function of exposed individuals. One survey even showed that event-

Marathon Bombings, 111 PROC. NAT’L ACAD. SCI. U.S. 93, 93 (2014),

35
   U.S. INSTITUTE OF MEDICINE COMMITTEE ON RESPONDING TO THE

related media exposure was associated with a higher likelihood of acute stress than direct

exposure to the bombing. 36

          27)    Furthermore, exposure to traumatic evidence through electronic media can trigger

unexpected, negative reactions from prospective jurors suffering from trauma-related disorders,

affecting their decision-making. Triggers vary depending on the type of trauma experienced and

may be directly or indirectly related to the origin event itself. For example, people could be

triggered by sounds, smells, tastes, colors, places and specific objects, as well as evidence related

to the traumatic event. In this mental state, individuals may be unable to precisely articulate what

they are thinking and feeling, and, of particular significance here, may even be unaware that the

traumatic event is the source of their anxiety. 37 Of significance to prospective jurors, traumatized

individuals may have a cognitive bias toward emotionally charged memories; and exposure to

evidence may cause flashbacks, trigger overwhelming feelings, and cause threat arousal—all of

which can impact cognition and information processing. 38 These studies bear directly on three of

the factors the Court is called upon to address under Rule: whether there is a reasonable

likelihood that electronic media coverage will jeopardize the safety or well-being of any

individual; whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will

jeopardize the interests or well-being of a minor; and whether electronic media coverage will

III. PRE-TRIAL PUBLICITY BIAS IS DIFFICULT TO RECOGNIZE AND SET ASIDE
                 AND CAN DISTORT JURY DELIBERATIONS

          A. Biased Jurors Interpret Information Differently Than Unbiased Jurors.

          28)     The impact of bias on juror decision-making is immense and difficult to

overcome. This is because jurors exposed to pre-trial publicity interpret new evidence at trial in a

different way than jurors who have not been exposed. In fact, bias from pre-trial publicity may

persist even when squarely contradicted by trial evidence. 39

          29)     Research into the “story model theory” and the “primacy effect” help explain why

pre-trial publicity bias is “sticky” and difficult to overcome. The story model theory posits that

the “central cognitive process in juror decision-making is story construction.” This means that

jurors turn information gleaned at trial into a narrative, and “the story the juror constructs

determines the juror’s decision.” 40

          30)     To test this theory, its originators showed study participants a mock trial and

asked them to reach a verdict while discussing their rationale out loud. Each participant’s

reasoning was mapped to determine whether the jurors processed the evidence presented

chronologically, in the structure of the legal argument, in terms of character sketches, or in a

story narrative. The results demonstrated that jurors analyze evidence in the structure of a story,




39
     Daftary-Kapur et al., supra note 5, at 474.
40
 Nancy Pennington and Reid Hastie, The Story Model for Juror Decision Making, in INSIDE
THE JUROR: THE PSYCHOLOGY OF JUROR DECISION MAKING 193, 193 (Reid
Hastie ed., 1993), https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511752896.010.

                                                                                         Page 19 of 36
not in terms of legal arguments or any other alternative, and—significantly—that jurors fill in

gaps in the story with information not presented at trial. 41

          31)     Research into the primacy effect demonstrates that it is difficult for individuals to

overcome their first impressions once they form initial opinions, and they therefore interpret later

information to be consistent with those first opinions. 42 Individuals remember early information

better than later information, and thus, early information holds a bigger influence on decision-

making and impressions. 43 When the primacy effect is combined with the story-model

understanding of juror decision-making, the juror who has a media-formed narrative of events

due to pre-trial publicity is at risk of prioritizing this narrative over subsequent, contradictory

evidence presented at trial, even when the juror endeavors to consider only the evidence from

trial.

          32)     Media-exposed jurors also may discount information presented at trial due to

“belief persistence,” which is the tendency to maintain a belief even after subsequent facts show

it to be incorrect, 44 or “confirmation bias,” which is the tendency to form a belief early in the

decision-making process and then evaluate new information in a manner favorable to that

     Id. at 204–06.

https://doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.32.5.880.

                                                                                         Page 20 of 36
belief. 45 In all, potential jurors tainted by pre-trial publicity face substantial hurdles in

overcoming those biases. 46

          B. Biased Jurors May Not Recognize Their Biases—Undermining Voir Dire and the
          Effectiveness of Limiting Instructions.

          33)    Prospective jurors may find it particularly difficult to recognize their own biases,

or may overestimate their capacity to set biases aside, in cases involving traumatic events. 47

Moreover, decades of studies have shown that neither jury instructions nor deliberation reduce

the impact of factual and emotional publicity. 48


45
     Id. See also Barns & Downey, supra note 16, at 91.
46
     Pennington & Hastie, supra note 40, at 193–99.

9039-7 (finding that when inadmissible evidence significantly influences jurors, judicial
instruction to disregard the evidence does not completely eliminate the impact of the evidence;
despite judicial instruction, inadmissible evidence had a significant impact on verdicts); Joel D.

Psychological Explanations for the Failures of Instructions to Disregard Pretrial Publicity and
Other Inadmissible Evidence, 6 PSYCHOL., PUB. POL’Y, & L. 677, 685 (2000),
https://doi.org/10.1037/1076-8971.6.3.677 (summarizing literature regarding jury responses to
inadmissible information and the limits of judicial instruction). Lieberman and Arndt found that
once jurors are exposed to pre-trial publicity, it may not be possible to eliminate the biasing
effect, and judicial instructions to disregard pre-trial publicity or other inadmissible evidence are
relatively ineffective. As was accurately summarized in United States v. Houlihan, 926 F. Supp. 14
(D. Mass. 1996): “Studies by social scientists suggest that pretrial publicity affects potential jurors
in ways of which they may not be aware, which they may not be able to articulate. Publicity, even
if it is not strictly speaking, inflammatory, which suggests culpability, or which links the defendant
to the criminal activities of others, as here, directly affects the readers' perception of guilt, how
likely they are to give weight to negative facts about the defendant at trial, to resolve conflicts in
credibility against the defendants.” Id. at 16-17, citing Kerr et. al, supra and Kerr, The effects of
pretrial publicity on jurors, 78 Judicature 120 (1994).
48
  Kramer et al., supra note 20, at 434–35; Christine L. Ruva & Anthony E. Coy, Your Bias Is

                                                                                           Page 21 of 36
       34)     Biased jurors may not answer voir dire questions accurately because their bias is

subconscious and they do not know or understand the impact that pre-trial publicity has had. In a

quintessential study, researchers exposed approximately 150 mock jurors to either neutral or

negative pre-trial publicity, asked them to complete typical voir dire questionnaires, excused

those jurors who said they could not be impartial, and then asked the remaining jurors for their

verdict. 49 Among those jurors—all of whom reported that they could be impartial—those who

were exposed to negative pre-trial publicity returned guilty verdicts at more than twice the rate of

those who had been exposed to neutral pre-trial publicity. 50

       35)     Even if jurors are given instructions not to use information learned outside of trial

in making their decisions, they may be unable to comply, despite best efforts. Source memory

errors occur when jurors misattribute the source of information, mistaking information learned

from pre-trial publicity with information presented at trial. 51 Studies have found that even when

such a declaration before one’s fellows is often its father.”).

believe—with a high degree of confidence—that information learned from pre-trial publicity is

        36)      Death-qualified jurors may present an additional challenge. According to another

study, death-qualified jurors were more likely than excludable jurors to answer negatively the

question whether pre-trial publicity could impact a defendant’s due process rights. 53 A potential

juror who does not recognize the impact of pre-trial publicity may be more confident in his or

her own lack of bias, and may be more likely to be mistaken in affirming his or her ability to set

bias aside in response to standard voir dire questioning. Supporting this conclusion, death-

qualified jurors who showed pre-trial publicity bias in their verdicts overwhelmingly self-

reported an ability to be impartial. 54

researchers found that jurors exposed to the misinformation inaccurately recalled 31 percent of it
as having been from the trial, and those who misremembered at the highest rates were also the
participants most likely to find the defendant guilty. The failure of jurors to recollect accurately
the source of their memories could lead to biased jurors distorting other jurors’ recollection of
trial events during deliberation, resulting in biased verdicts. Id. at 150.

  See id. (finding that death-qualified participants are not only more likely to find the defendant

                                                                                       Page 23 of 36
       C. Biased Jurors Distort Jury Deliberations.

       37)     The adverse effects of pre-trial publicity do not stop with the biased juror. Studies

show that pre-trial publicity bias can spread among the jury and even increase through a process

called “group polarization,” which occurs when group decision-making leads to a more extreme

result than the original views of the group’s members. Through this process, pre-trial publicity

bias can spread to others not originally affected. 55

       38)     A 2022 study analyzing mock jury deliberations involving some mock jurors who

had been exposed to slanted pre-trial publicity and others who had not found that (a) when mock

jurors exposed to slanted pre-trial publicity introduced facts into the deliberation which came

from pre-trial publicity but were not in the trial record, they were corrected less than 50 percent

of the time; (b) mock juries that included some members exposed to pre-trial publicity slanted

against the defendant and others exposed to no pre-trial publicity were more likely to find the

defendant guilty than juries with no exposed members; and (c) juries comprising some members

exposed to pre-trial publicity slanted against the victim and others who had been exposed to no

pre-trial publicity were more likely to find the defendant not guilty than juries with no exposed




Excluding those who self-identified bias or were unsure about their level of bias, the study found
the mock jurors exposed to prejudicial pre-trial publicity significantly more likely to find the
defendant guilty than those exposed to irrelevant articles.
55
   Ruva, C. L., Diaz Ortega, S.E., & O’Grady, K.A. (2022). What drives a Jury’s Deliberations?
The influence of pretrial publicity and jury composition on deliberation slant and content.
Psychology, Public Policy, & Law, 28(1), 32-52. https://doi.org/10.1037/law0000310; Ruva &
Coy, supra note 48, at 32–33, https://doi. org/10.1037/law0000220 (finding that in a criminal
case simulation split among anti-prosecution pre-trial publicity, anti-defense pre-trial publicity,
and one control group, mixed-jury deliberations do not correct juror-level errors and control
biases for anti-prosecution pre-trial publicity); cf. Ruva & Guenther, supra note 51, at 295; Kerr
et al., supra note 47, at 75 (finding that for moderate cases, jury deliberations accentuated pre-
trial publicity bias, but for extreme cases with more certain conviction or acquittal rates, jury
deliberation attenuated pre-trial publicity bias); Kramer et al., supra note 20, at 14.

                                                                                      Page 24 of 36
members. Together, these findings suggest that rather than correcting for the bias introduced by

pre-trial publicity, jury deliberations in fact spread individual jurors’ biases to others. 56

          IV. The Empirical Research Discussed in the 2012 Judicial Council Final Report Is
          Limited and Dated and Is Based on Unreliable Survey Research.

          39)    The Final Report by the Utah Judicial Council Study Committee on Technology

(April 10, 2012) states in part as follows:

                  The concern most often raised about electronic media coverage is that such
          coverage may harm the decorum of the proceedings and negatively impact trial
          participants. The extensive empirical research and broad-based experience of other states,
          however, suggest that these concerns are unfounded. For example, several states,
          including Arizona, California, Florida, Hawaii, Kansas, Louisiana, Maine, Massachusetts,
          Minnesota, New Jersey, New York, Ohio, Virginia, and Washington, have studied the
          impact of electronic media coverage on courtroom proceedings, focusing particularly on
          the effect that cameras have upon courtroom decorum and upon witnesses, jurors,
          attorneys, and judges. See Kelli L. Sager & Karen N. Fredericksen, Televising the
          Judicial Branch: In Furtherance of the Public’s First Amendment Rights, 69 S. CAL. L.
          REV. 1519, 1543 (1996) (“Televising the Judicial Branch”).

                  The results from the state studies were unanimous: electronic media coverage of
          courtroom proceedings—whether civil or criminal—has no detrimental impact on the
          parties, jurors, counsel, or courtroom decorum. Televising the Judicial Branch, 69 S.
          CAL. L. REV. at 1544, 1547. For example, the state studies revealed that fears about
          witness distraction, nervousness, distortion, fear of harm, and reluctance to testify were
          unfounded. Id. at 1543-44. A three-year pilot program permitting electronic media
          coverage of civil proceedings in six federal district courts and two federal circuit courts
          reported similar results, as well a favorable view of such coverage by the judges who
          participated in the program. See Federal Judicial Center, ELECTRONIC MEDIA
          COVERAGE OF FEDERAL CIVIL PROCEEDINGS: AN EVALUATION OF THE
          PILOT PROGRAM IN SIX DISTRICT COURTS AND TWO COURTS OF APPEALS
          (1994); see also Televising the Judicial Branch, 69 S. CAL. L. REV. at 1545; Alex
          Kozinski & Robert Johnson, Of Cameras and Courtrooms, 20 FORDHAM INTELL.
          PROP. MEDIA & ENT. L.J. 1107, 1114 (2010) (summarizing state and federal studies
          on electronic media coverage of judicial proceedings and concluding that the empirical
          research demonstrates no detrimental impact on trial participants or on courtroom
          decorum).

(Report at pp. 8-9).



56
     Ruva et al. (2022), supra note 55.

                                                                                          Page 25 of 36
        40)     Several observations can be made about this aspect of the report. First, the Sager

and Kozinski articles cited in the report are law review articles and not empirical research

articles. They cite state surveys and one federal survey regarding civil court proceedings. The

state surveys asked for the opinions of witnesses, jurors, attorneys, and judges after a trial is

completed or after the witness testifies. Survey data cannot be used to infer causation, and this is

not its only limitation – e.g., response bias, recall failure or misinformation effects (may not

accurately recall past event and information after the event affects recall). The one state that used

additional methodology was California which also used interviews and systematic observation.

Although this does improve upon survey data alone it does not allow one to infer causation. Each

trial, witness, set of jurors, judges, attorneys are different. There is no control over any variable

and hence there is no way to conclude that cameras do or do not affect trial players’ behaviors,

memory, or decision-making.

        41)     Also, the three-year pilot federal study published in 1994 that found judges have a

favorable opinion of electronic media coverage of court in civil cases tells us nothing about the

possible prejudicial effect such coverage could have on courtroom proceedings, witness

testimony and memory, and juror bias, behavior, and decision making. The study itself noted its

inherent limitations, stating:

                Several potentially relevant issues were not examined in the evaluation and
        therefore cannot be addressed in this report. First, the evaluation design as approved by
        the Ad Hoc Committee on Cameras in the Courtroom did not include a measure of the
        actual (as opposed to perceived) effects of electronic media on jurors, witnesses, counsel,
        and judges. The only way to measure objectively the actual effects of electronic media on
        jurors and witnesses would be to compare the behavior and perceptions of jurors and
        witnesses in two different groups of cases: those covered by electronic media and those
        not covered. The Federal Judicial Center suggested—and the Ad Hoc Committee on
        Cameras in the Courtroom concurred—that this approach was not feasible because,
        among other reasons, there would be too few cases in the pilot courts with high media
        interest to support such an evaluation.



                                                                                        Page 26 of 36
              Second, we did not directly measure the attitudes of jurors, witnesses, and parties
       because most have had little courtroom experience and could not, we believed, make
       judgments (as judges and attorneys could) about the effects of electronic media on
       themselves. (A witness who has never been in a court room might be nervous for many
       reasons but might attribute that state—in appropriately—to the presence of cameras.) We
       did obtain some information on these issues through other methods, such as judge and
       attorney questionnaires. Also, we reviewed results from state court studies exploring
       these questions.

                Finally, because the pilot program limited coverage to civil proceedings, the
       impact of electronic media coverage on federal criminal proceedings was not addressable
       in this evaluation. Opinions on the issue of criminal coverage were obtained through
       questionnaires and interviews.

               Another consideration relevant to interpreting the findings in this report is that the
       pilot courts were chosen from among courts that had volunteered to participate, and most
       of the analyses in our study focused on judges who actually had experience with
       electronic media coverage. Thus, it could be expected that judges whose responses we
       report would on average be more favorable toward electronic media coverage than would
       a randomly-selected sample of judges throughout the country.

2012 Study at 8.

       42)     As this passage suggests, survey research is widely used, but it comes with

several limitations and pitfalls that researchers need to consider:

       a. Sampling Bias
          • If the sample isn’t representative of the population, results can be skewed.
          • Common issues include nonresponse bias and convenience sampling.
       b. Response Bias
          • Participants may give socially desirable answers rather than truthful ones.
          • Wording of questions can lead to leading or loaded responses.
       c. Low Response Rates
          • Declining participation rates can reduce reliability and increase bias.
       d. Limited Depth
          • Surveys often capture surface-level attitudes or behaviors without exploring
             underlying reasons.
          • Complex topics may require qualitative methods for deeper insights.
       e. Measurement Error
          • Poorly designed questions can lead to misinterpretation.
          • Ambiguous or double-barreled questions confuse respondents.
       f. Recall Issues
          • Respondents may not accurately remember past behaviors or experiences.
       g. Mode Effects



                                                                                      Page 27 of 36
         •   Online, phone, and face-to-face surveys can produce different results due to context
             and interviewer presence.
       h. Difficulty in Establishing Causality
          • Surveys are typically correlational; they rarely prove cause-and-effect relationships.
       i. Data Quality Concerns
          • Inattentive respondents or straight-lining can compromise validity.
       j. Cultural and Language Barriers
          • Misinterpretation of questions across different cultural contexts can distort findings.

       43)     In fact, five peer-reviewed journal articles have critiqued the survey studies relied

upon in the 2012 Final Report, and three of these studies were published before the 2012 Report

and are nowhere discussed in it. See Borgida, E., DeBono, K. G., & Buckman, L. A., Cameras

in the courtroom: The effects of media coverage on witness testimony and juror perceptions, 14

Law and Human Behavior 489–509 (1990), https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01044224; Slater, D., &

Hans, V. P., Methodological issues in the evaluation of experiments with cameras in the courts,

30 Communication Quarterly 346-380 (1982); Christo Lassiter, TV or not TV—That is the

Question, 86 J. CRIM. L. & CRIMINOLOGY 928, 966–67 (1996) (explaining that surveys are

flawed because respondents are inherently biased in favor of believing that they participated

objectively in a trial, and because of the small sample size involved in most such studies);

Cristina Carmody Tilley, I Am A Camera: Scrutinizing The Assumption That Cameras In The

Courtroom Furnish Public Value By Operating As A Proxy For The Public, 16 Journal of

Constitutional Law 719 n. 122 (2013) (explaining that “[m]ost of the studies involved were ex

post surveys of parties who had participated in televised trials asking their self-assessment of the

camera’s effect on their behavior.”); Kaufman, A.R., Williams, L. and Thorley, D., Performative

Judging: Measuring the Effect of Video Recording on Judicial Behavior in Circuit Court Oral

Arguments (2025)(same), https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=5283589 . 57 Both



57
  The Tillery article also addresses the reliance in Judge Kozinski’s article and in other sources
on the broadcast-press-as-proxy conceit. She correctly points out that “[a]dvocates have been
                                                                                      Page 28 of 36
Slater and Hans (1982) and Borgida et al. (1990) identify significant methodological flaws in

research evaluating cameras in the courtroom. Examples of these limitations are summarized

below.

         44)   The Slater and Hans study notes that most state-level evaluations were not true

experiments. They relied on post-hoc surveys and interviews rather than controlled experimental

designs, lacking randomization, control groups, and systematic manipulation of variables (Slater

& Hans, 1982). Nearly all research commissioned by states considering cameras in courtrooms

consisted of nonrepresentative surveys and anecdotal evidence (Arkansas Supreme Court

Committee, 1979; Cameras-in-the-Courtroom Test Committee, 1974; Hawaii State Bar

Association, 1982; Humphries, 1978; Supreme Court of Florida, 1978; Supreme Court of

Wisconsin, 1979). Chief Justice Burger in Chandler v. Florida, 449 U.S. 560, 576 n. 11 (1981)

noted that the available data were “ ‘limited and non-scientific’, quoting In re Petition of Post-

Newsweek Stations, Florida, Inc., 370 So. 2d 764, 781, 768 (1979).

         45)   The Slater and Hans article also notes that this reliance on survey research is

problematic because surveys capture perceptions but cannot isolate causal effects of cameras

versus conventional media coverage (Craig, 1979; Judicial Planning Coordination Unit, 1978).

Many studies were conducted only at the appellate level, where there are no jurors or

witnesses—the groups most likely to be affected. For example, Delaware’s one-year test

excluded trial courts, limiting its relevance for concerns about juror and witness impact (Slater &

Hans, 1982). Additionally, trials were often selected based on media interest rather than




implying for some time that cameras are inherently likely to realize public values in the same
way that actual public attendance or camera-free media would.159 But this implication wilts
under empirical scrutiny.” Tillery, supra, at 727.

                                                                                      Page 29 of 36
randomly assigned to coverage conditions. Without randomization, differences may reflect trial

characteristics rather than the presence of cameras.

       46)     Borgida et al. (1990) highlight further issues in state surveys, such as the 1978

Florida study (Supreme Court of Florida, 1978). Participants were asked to recall experiences

from televised trials over the prior year, without accounting for elapsed time or the date

questionnaires were completed. The unreliability of self-report data over such intervals was

compounded by asking witnesses to reconstruct their prior attitudes toward cameras. Two-thirds

of those contacted refused to participate. Despite these problems, the committee concluded

cameras had few adverse effects (Borgida et al., 1990, p. 491).

       47)     Borgida et al. further notes that California courts employed a multimethod

approach, collecting surveys, interviews, and observational data (Short, 1981). Although this

evaluation attempted to compare trials with extended coverage versus conventional coverage,

contamination occurred when trials switched coverage conditions midstream or were retried

under different conditions. Five trials allowed direct comparisons between media types while

controlling for case type, but the sample was too small for reliable conclusions. These design

flaws reduced internal validity and made causal inference unreliable.

       48)     Borgida et al. further notes that early laboratory studies also suffered from

methodological shortcomings. Hoyt (1977) and Kassin (1983) used small samples (12 and 25

participants per condition, respectively), likely resulting in underpowered analyses. These studies

also failed to include a conventional media condition (e.g., journalist or sketch artist), an

essential comparison group. Without this comparison, no valid statement can be made about the

effects of cameras relative to conventional media coverage (Borgida et al., 1990).




                                                                                       Page 30 of 36
       49)     In addition to not acknowledging these critiques, the 2012 Final Report’s

description of the existing studies is flawed. As indicated above, the Final Report claims that the

Kozinski law review article at page 1114 “summarize[ed] state and federal studies on electronic

media coverage of judicial proceedings and conclud[ed] that the empirical research demonstrates

no detrimental impact on trial participants or on courtroom decorum.” The only study discussed

in any detail in the Kozinski article at pp. 1112-1114 is the 1994 federal civil trial pilot program,

and regarding that program Judge Kozinski acknowledged that “[t]he program didn’t directly

survey witnesses and jurors, as they lack the experience needed to meaningfully compare their

experiences to a courtroom without cameras…” Id. at 1113 n. 26.

       50)     The 2012 Final Report’s description of the outcome of the 1994 federal civil trial

pilot program is as follows: “The pilot program was in effect from 1991 through 1994.

Notwithstanding the Federal Judicial Center’s ultimate recommendation that federal trial courts

allow cameras in civil proceedings, the federal judiciary declined to continue the program when

the study period expired.” Final Report at 10. However, that description does not tell the whole

story. As explained by the United States Supreme Court in Hollingsworth v. Perry, 558 U.S. 183,

193 (2010):

              In 1996, the Judicial Conference of the United States adopted a policy opposing
       the public broadcast of court proceedings. This policy was adopted after a multiyear
       study of the issue by the Federal Judicial Center which drew on data from six district and
       two appellate courts, as well as state-court data. In light of the study's findings, the
       Judicial Conference concluded that “the intimidating effect of cameras on some
       witnesses and jurors [is] cause for concern.” Administrative Office of United States
       Courts, Report of Proceedings of Judicial Conference of the United States 47 (Sept. 20,
       1994).

              In more than a decade since its adoption the Judicial Conference has continued to
       adhere to its position on the broadcast of court proceedings.

(emphasis added)



                                                                                       Page 31 of 36
       51)     Hollingsworth v. Perry itself recognizes that “[t]his Court has recognized that

witness testimony may be chilled if broadcast.” Id. at 195, citing Estes v. Texas, 381 U.S. 532,

547 (1965); id., at 591 (Harlan, J., concurring). The 2012 Final Report does not discuss

Hollingsworth.

       52)     It should also be noted that the state surveys and the one federal survey discussed

in the 2012 Final Report attempted to measure, quite imperfectly, whether having cameras in the

courtroom during a civil trial caused jurors to be distracted, to be influenced in their deliberations

by the mere presence of the cameras during trial, to feel that the presence of cameras highlighted

the importance of the trial or a particular witness, or to be reluctant to serve as jurors. 2012 Study

at 124-125. None of these surveys assessed, or could assess, whether live streaming of all aspects

of a high profile criminal case in the age of social media could cause irreparable conscious and

unconscious bias and trauma to prospective jurors or caused untoward effects in other trial

participants

       53)     Finally, the 2012 Final Report does not address the pre-2012 empirical research

discussed above and could not have considered the substantial empirical research that has taken

place since 2012, also discussed above. In addition, and especially relevant in this case, although

early PTP research focused primarily on factual, incriminating information about the defendant,

more recent work has expanded the conceptualization of PTP to include emotional, community

based, victim focused content, and attire of the defendant. This broader approach has revealed

that PTP effects are not limited to cognitive contamination from inadmissible facts but also arise

from social, emotional, and narrative mechanisms that shape jurors’ pretrial schemas and

subsequent decision making. This research too is not addressed in the 2012 report. I briefly

review that literature here.



                                                                                       Page 32 of 36
       54)        Community Outrage and Emotional PTP: Zimmerman et al. (2016) addressed

a longstanding gap between legal concerns and empirical research by isolating the effects of

community outrage—a form of PTP frequently cited by courts but rarely studied

independently. 58 Using a factorial design that separated factual PTP from community sentiment

PTP, the authors found that exposure to community outrage and victim hardship increased mock

jurors’ perceptions of the defendant’s guilt, even in the absence of incriminating facts. Notably,

neither factual PTP nor anticipated social interaction with community members influenced guilt

judgments. These findings suggest that emotional and community based narratives may exert a

unique influence on jurors by activating moral intuitions, empathic responses, or perceived social

norms, rather than by supplying case relevant information. Zimmerman et al.’s work highlights

the need to conceptualize PTP not merely as a source of factual contamination but as a vehicle

for communicating collective sentiment that may implicitly pressure jurors toward conviction or

death verdicts.

       55)        Victim Pretrial Publicity: Research demonstrates that exposure to victim-

focused pretrial publicity (victim-PTP) significantly influences juror decision-making,

perceptions, and cognitive processes. The more recent research on this issue includes only

negative-victim PTP (painting the victim in a negative light) exposure, while older studies

explored positive-victim attributes directly conveyed to mock juries in trial simulations.

       56)        Jurors exposed to negative-victim PTP consistently exhibit a pro-defense bias,

making them less likely to render guilty verdicts and more likely to interpret trial evidence in a

manner favorable to the defense, regardless of the actual probative value of that evidence (Ruva


58
  Zimmerman, D., Rodriguez, D., Bergold, A., & Penrod, S. (2016). The influence of pretrial
exposure to community outrage and victim hardship on guilt judgments. Psychology, Crime &
Law, 22(5), 435–454. https://doi.org/10.1080/1068316X.2015.1120870

                                                                                      Page 33 of 36
& Guenther, 2017; Ruva et al., 2022). This bias is explained by predecisional distortion theory,

which posits that jurors distort trial evidence to align with their favored side, and by the story

model, which suggests that jurors incorporate PTP into mental schemas that guide interpretation

of trial evidence (Hope et al., 2004; Ruva et al., 2011; Ruva & Guenther, 2017).

       57)     Negative-victim PTP also affects perceptions of the defendant, with jurors

exposed to such publicity rating the defendant as more credible than jurors exposed to negative-

defendant PTP or irrelevant PTP (Ruva & Guenther, 2017). These credibility assessments

mediate the effect of PTP on guilt ratings, pushing verdict preferences toward acquittal when

negative-victim PTP is present (Ruva & Guenther, 2017).

       58)     Regarding memory, negative-victim PTP exposure leads to source monitoring

errors, where jurors misattribute PTP information to the trial. Although negative-victim PTP

jurors initially demonstrate somewhat better recall of trial facts than no-PTP jurors—likely due

to multiple exposures to case-related information—they also make more critical source memory

errors, incorporating PTP details into their recollection of trial evidence (Ruva & Guenther,

2017). Deliberations do not reliably correct these errors and may even facilitate social contagion,

spreading misinformation among jurors (Ruva & Guenther, 2017; Ruva et al., 2022).

       59)     These findings collectively indicate that victim-PTP can significantly distort

jurors’ verdicts, perceptions of credibility, interpretation of evidence, and memory accuracy, and

that deliberations are not a reliable corrective mechanism. Instead, deliberations may perpetuate

or spread bias, undermining the fairness of trial outcomes.

       60)     Supporting these studies are older studies in which the effect of positive-victim

attributes are directly conveyed to mock juries in trial simulations. Greene, E., Koehring, H., &

Quiat, M., Victim impact evidence in capital cases: Does the victim's character matter? 28



                                                                                       Page 34 of 36
Journal of Applied Social Psychology (1998) 145. doi:10.1111/j.1559-1816.1998.tb01697.x,

mock jurors watched a reenactment of the penalty phase in the case of Booth v. Maryland. The

authors manipulated the respectability of the victim described in the victim impact evidence and

found that jurors' judgments of the victims, victims' survivors, and severity of the crime were all

affected by this description. The authors also found that jurors disregarded the defendant’s

mitigating circumstances (being brought up in a bad neighborhood) if the defendant was on trial

for killing a highly respectable victim. The authors noted that their finding that information on

the character of the victims influenced mock jurors’ willingness to consider mitigating evidence

replicated the same finding made in Burkhead, M., Luginbuhl, J., & Wrenn, S, Victim impact

evidence in capital trials: What are its effects?, Poster presented at the meetings of the American

Psychology-Law Association, Santa Fe, NM.,

       61)     Defendant’s Attire: Street, Kylene L., et al. The Cloak of Innocence: Perception

of Attire in the Courtroom, 40 J. of Police & Criminal Psychology 732 (2025),

https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11896-024-09710-w was a mock jury study that found

that a defendant dressed in a prison jumpsuit versus a dress suit was seen as “more aggressive”,

and that this perceived aggressiveness resulted in defendants being found 5.27 times more likely

than mock jurors in the formal attire condition to render a guilty verdict than a not guilty verdict.

       62)     In an earlier study, Barnett, Guilty and Threatening: Visual Bias in Television

News Crime Stories, 5 Journalism and Communication Monographs 104 (2003),

https://doi.org/10.1177/152263790300500301, forty subjects watched television crime news

stories, which contained either visual (dressed in an orange suit, wearing handcuffs and being

restrained by a police officer) or aural (mention of a prior record) bias. Results showed that

subjects who saw the visual bias evaluated the accused as more threatening, dangerous and guilty



                                                                                       Page 35 of 36
than those who did not see the bias. Those who heard about a prior record evaluated the accused

as more threatening and guilty, but not as more dangerous than those who did not hear about the

record. These impressions of guilt remained after a two-week delay. “Findings illustrate how

common TV news portrayals of a person accused of a crime can prejudice the general public.

Also, accidental viewing of a criminal defendant in prison clothes or being restrained can affect

snap judgments about that person during the trial.” Id. at 105.

                                           CONCLUSION

        Substantial empirical evidence exists demonstrating that live streaming of all proceedings

in a notorious criminal case can cause bias because (a) live streaming of notorious crimes can be

pervasive, slanted, sensational, and emotional—all of which can lead to bias.; and (b) publicity of

alleged assassinations, terrorist attacks, or other mass acts of violence may cause trauma and

significant anger, disgust, fear, and related bias. Substantial empirical evidence also exists

demonstrating that pre-trial publicity bias is difficult to recognize and set aside and can distort

jury deliberations because (a) biased jurors interpret information differently than unbiased juror;

(b) biased jurors may not recognize their biases—undermining voir dire and the effectiveness of

limiting instructions; and (c) biased jurors distort jury deliberations. Finally, the empirical

research discussed in the 2012 Judicial Council final report is limited and dated and is based on

unreliable survey research.

my knowledge.

                                                                                        Christine L. Ruva
     Jury Consulting and Expert Testimony
       x     This work includes consulting on high-profile cases, writing reports for the court, and testifying
             in court as an expert witness regarding the impact of pretrial publicity on defendants’ right to a
             fair trial guaranteed under the Sixth Amendment of the US Constitution. I have also consulted
             other jury consultants in high-profile cases.
